# In Praise of the Baal Shem Tov (Shivchei HaBesht)

*An English translation of `shivchei-habesht.md` (the introduction and §§1–210).*

> Translation notes: This is a faithful working translation of the classic 1814 collection of tales about Rabbi Israel Baal Shem Tov and his circle. Honorific abbreviations (e.g. ז״ל "of blessed memory," השי״ת "the Blessed Name," זיע״א "may his merit protect us") are rendered into readable English. Where the Hebrew is terse or elliptical, the translation lightly fills in sense without adding content. Hebrew proper names and place names are transliterated.

---

## The Author's Introduction

I said: it is written for me in the scroll of the book, so that the reader will not wonder what I saw in this matter and what moved me to come to the point of writing down a recounting of such things — especially since there are some people who speak with a mocking tongue, asking "What is this silence?" And if one is inclined to scoff he will scoff, saying, "If you had stayed silent, your silence would have been finer than your words." Yet there is nothing to say, nothing in which one could find grounds to speak harshly and rebelliously — only that one prophesies in a different style.

Therefore I said: the Judge is my faithful witness, and my witness is on high, that my intention is not to glorify myself, Heaven forbid, nor to walk among great and wondrous things beyond me, to make myself a name in the land in the manner of the great ones.

I will reveal a handbreadth of my reasons and motives: I heard from the mouth of the great, famous rabbi, R. Gedaliah, of blessed memory, who said in the name of the famous pious one, R. Shemeril, of blessed memory, preacher of the holy community of Verkhivka — and to my mind, with all the signs and wonders and miracles that are famous and revealed, there is none who understands my counsel: that a person should hold fast to faith alone.

I, too, have taken to heart that from the time I became a man until old age, close to gray hair, I see — because of our many sins — that day by day the miracles have diminished and the wonders have departed. For in former days it would sometimes happen that people lay dying — what is called *hinerbet* (in a deathlike swoon) — and when they rose and lived, they would recount the awesome things they had seen in the upper world, as is mentioned in the Gemara, "you have seen a clear world," and similar tales in the Gemara.

For example, my father-in-law, R. Alexander, of blessed memory, recounted that there was a certain man lying in a deathlike swoon in the holy community of Bershtat. This was at the time the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi — may his name be blotted out — was stirring, and they showed this man places in the books where certain rabbis had erred, and nearly went astray after that sect, and they commanded him to tell them the true explanation of those passages.

In his days, too, there were madmen who would cut themselves with stones during the reading of the Torah, and would disclose to people their sins, saying of themselves with what sin they were caught in the sling.

I remember that in my youth I studied in a village with a certain teacher, and poor folk came to the schoolroom and were given food. Among them was a woman who had an evil spirit in her, which no one recognized. When the teacher began to study the order of the Chumash with the children, he had scarcely studied two or three verses when the evil spirit threw the woman to the ground. Her husband came and asked the teacher to stop his teaching, for the impure spirit within her could not bear anything of holiness; and when he stopped teaching, she rose and sat in her place.

On account of all these things, many would repent, and faith was strengthened in the heart of every Jew.

But now, because of our many sins, the righteous have diminished, and the seers' windows have darkened, and faith has fallen exceedingly low, and much heresy has been cast into the world — it is not permitted to write rebelliously against the people of the Lord, and may the Blessed Name atone for this.

Therefore he counseled me to write down the awesome things I heard from men of truth; and in every account I wrote from whom I heard it. Thank God, who graced me with memory, I added nothing and subtracted nothing — and all of it is true and certain, and the matters were not altered from what I was told.

I wrote all this as a remembrance for my children and my children's children, that this may be for them a remembrance and a strengthening of faith in the Lord and His Torah, and faith in the righteous, and faith in all who draw themselves near to the Blessed Name and His Torah — and how His Torah, may He be blessed, purifies the soul of those who study it, so that a person can come to great levels. As our Sages said: a person should always look forward and say, "When will my deeds reach the deeds of my forefathers Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob?"

Therefore let every reader of this know faithfully that I did not write this in the manner of storytelling and chronicles, but rather that one should truly observe, with the eye of the intellect, in each and every deed of the awesome acts of God, and take to himself moral instruction from this, to bind his heart to the fear of God, to faith in the sages, and to the power of our holy Torah that issues from the deeds.

It was my intention to write after each deed some moral lesson and some point of faith that issues from it; but I said, lest I fall short, Heaven forbid, I left it that each and every person should learn from it according to his measure and according to the measure of his discernment — the small one according to his smallness, and the great one according to his greatness.

I said: along the way, alongside my children, may I also merit the rest of the children of Israel. And may the Blessed Name grant me merit and assist me to be among those who bring merit to Israel, and to make known to those who issue from my loins that they should give to all. And to every reader of this I will utter riddles from of old, which we have heard and known, and our fathers have told us; we will not hide from their children, recounting the praises of the Lord and the wonders He has done — and He raised up testimony in Jacob and set a Torah in Israel, which He commanded them to make known to their children, that they heard, so that the last generation should know.

"A watcher and a holy one came down from heaven" — blessed is the Maker of the luminaries of light. These, these are the souls of the righteous; these, these are the soul of the everlasting light, the holy and shining lamp, Rabbi Israel Baal Shem Tov and his companions, who was a spark of Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai — as I heard from the son of R. Yaakov of Mezhibuzh, called R. Yokel: that once his father took him to the study hall, and the Baal Shem Tov was standing and praying before the ark, and he said to him in these words, "My son, see and observe, for the like of this will not be seen in the world until the coming of our righteous Messiah, for he is Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai and his companions."

And also from the deed written within concerning his brother-in-law R. Gershon of Kotov: that he had ascents of the soul during the afternoon prayers, to raise up many souls — and as is written in the book *Etz Chaim*, in the Gate of the Sabbath, chapter 8, that Moses our teacher, peace be upon him, was diligent in this matter every Sabbath eve, to raise up all the souls, both of the living and of the dead. From this deed it appears that he also had an aspect of the soul of Moses our teacher, peace be upon him; and see the deed within — and what is known needs no proof.

---

## §1

**Says the printer:** Since I saw in the manuscripts that I copied — concerning the order of the unfolding and revelation of the Baal Shem Tov (may his merit protect us) — matters not in their proper order, and from the mouth of our master the rabbi (may his rest be in Eden) I heard everything in correct order and with good explanation, therefore I will print it as they heard from his holy mouth; and what branches out from this I will write afterward, as written in the aforementioned manuscripts:

The rabbi R. Eliezer, father of the Baal Shem Tov, dwelt in the land of Wallachia, near the frontier. He and his wife were old. Once, raiders came to the city and took him captive, while his wife escaped to another city; and out of her great poverty she became a midwife, and from this was her livelihood. The captors led him to a distant land, a place where there were no Jews, and they sold him there.

He served his master faithfully and found favor in his eyes, and his master appointed him over his household. He sought of his master that he grant him leave to rest and cease from labor on the holy Sabbath, and his master fulfilled his request. When the days grew long for him, he wished to flee to save his soul, but it was revealed to him in a dream: "Do not press the hour, for you still need to be in this land."

One day, it happened that his master had some affair and matter with the viceroy, the king's counselor, and he gave the rabbi R. Eliezer to him as a gift. He found him wondrous and esteemed him greatly, and as soon as he came to the viceroy he found favor in his eyes. He gave him a special room to sit in, and no service in the world was laid upon him, except that when the viceroy came from the king, he would go out to meet him with a basin of water to wash his feet — for such is the custom of great nobles. And all that time he occupied himself with Torah and prayer in his special room.

Once the king had a great war, and he sent for the counselor to take counsel as to how the strategy of the war should be — the deployment and the assault. Since it was difficult to grasp, they could not arrive at the truth of the matter, how to act. The king was filled with rage and fury against the viceroy, who could not stand firm in a time of distress. The viceroy went home sullen and angry. When he came home, the rabbi R. Eliezer went toward him with a basin of water to wash his feet, as was his custom, but the viceroy did not accept it and lay down on his bed with an angry face. The rabbi said to him, "My lord, why is your face angry? Pray tell me." But the viceroy rebuked him.

Yet, being a faithful servant to his master and desiring his master's vindication, he cast his life aside and came to him once and twice, until the viceroy was compelled to recount to him the whole affair. He said to his master, "Surely interpretations belong to God; for the Lord is a man of war. I will fast a *hafsaka* (an unbroken fast) and ask of the Blessed Name concerning this secret, for He is the revealer of secrets." He inquired by means of a dream-question, and they answered him all the modes of the war — how everything would be, with good explanation.

The next day he came to his master and recounted to him all the counsel of God that they had shown him from heaven. It pleased him greatly, and at once he went to the king with great joy. He answered and said, "My lord the king, this is the counsel that is counseled," and recounted to him everything in full. When the king heard all the words of the viceroy, the king said, "Behold, this counsel is a wonder of counsel, and this is not human intellect — unless from a man of God upon whom there shines... 'whose dwelling is not with flesh' — or, to the contrary, from a spirit of impurity. Now, I know you, that you are by no means a man of God; this can only be that you are a sorcerer."

The viceroy was compelled to admit the truth and to recount to him the whole affair. (And in the manuscripts it is written in these words: Once the king traveled with his armies in ships to make war upon a certain fortress. When they arrived, it seemed small in his eyes, and the king said, "Behold, the day is far spent and the fortress is small; let us lodge here, a little way off, and early in the morning let us not trouble all our men of war, for with a small force we will capture it." Now the aforementioned rabbi was also a sailor on one of the ships, and they informed him in a dream that he should go and say to the king that he should not treat this war lightly, for he and all his army would perish — for behold, in the sea around the city there were iron pillars all around the city, and it was impossible to reach there with ships, for the ships would immediately overturn, except by way of the city's entrance; and they also revealed to him the straight way, with clear signs, so that he would not stray on the road. The king rose in the morning with diligence, with his whole camp, and they began to rouse the sailors to set out with the ships. The aforementioned rabbi did not want to set sail, and said, "I have a secret matter with the king." Immediately they shaved him and changed his garments, and he came to the king in a small ship and recounted to the king what had been revealed to him from heaven; and he said to the king, "If you do not believe this, let the king send a small ship with men condemned to death, and we will see what becomes of them." The king did so, and when the ship came to the place of the pillars, the ship overturned and all the men in it perished. The king said, "If so, give counsel as to what to do." The rabbi said to him, "There is a straight way, which is the city's entrance, by which the people of the city come and go," and he showed him the signs. They did so, and the king succeeded and captured the aforementioned fortress.)

The king elevated the aforementioned rabbi and made him commander of his armies, for he saw with an open eye that the Lord was with him and that all he did prospered; and in every place the king sent him to wage his war, he succeeded greatly. Meanwhile, it fell into his heart: what will be in the end? Perhaps now is the time to flee to his place. But they revealed to him from heaven, "You still need to be in this land."

One day the viceroy died, and the king set him in his place to be counselor to the king, for he had found favor in his eyes; and he gave him the viceroy's daughter for a wife. But the rabbi did not touch her, with God's help, and he saw to every stratagem so as not to sit at home; and even if it happened at some time that he was at home, he did not touch her at all. Now in that land no Jew was permitted to dwell, and when they found a single Jew his sentence was death — and so it was for him, for several years. Once his wife said to him, "Tell me, what blemish did you find in me, that you have not touched me and do not deal with me in the way of all the earth?" He said to her, "Swear to me that you will not reveal it to any person, and I will tell you the truth." She swore to him, and he said to her, "I am a Jew." Immediately she sent him to his land with very much silver and gold.

But robbers plundered him on the way and took all that he had with him. While he was on the road, Elijah, of blessed memory, was revealed to him and said, "In the merit of this, a son will be born to you who will light up the eyes of Israel, and in him will be fulfilled, 'Israel, in whom I will be glorified.'" He came home and found his wife alive, with God's help, and the Baal Shem Tov was born to them in their old age, when both of them were close to a hundred years. (And the Baal Shem Tov said that it would not have been possible to draw down his soul, except when his [father's] desire had been spent.)

The child grew and was weaned, and the time came for his aforementioned father to die. They took him on his arm, and he said, "Behold, I see that you will light up my lamp, and I did not merit to raise you. But, my beloved son, this you shall surely remember all your days: that the Lord is with you; therefore fear nothing." (In the name of our master I heard an explanation drawing this near by way of nature, as our Sages said, "the speech of a child in the street is that of his father or his mother" — all the more so a child of old age. For behold, Jacob loved Joseph because he was a child of his old age, and therefore the bond between them was very great, as is written in the holy Zohar. So too here: because of the greatness of the bond, even though the Baal Shem Tov was still a very small child, on account of the true bond the words were fixed in his heart.)

After his father's passing, the child grew. Now the people of the city, because the soul of his father was very precious in their eyes, dealt kindly with him and gave him to a teacher to study with him, and he succeeded greatly in his learning. But because his way was to study for several days and then flee from the schoolhouse — they would search for him and find him sitting alone in the forest, and they attributed the matter to his being an orphan with no one watching over him, an abandoned lad — they brought him once again to the teacher. And so it was many times: he would flee to the forest to seclude himself there.

Until, as time went on, the matter cooled, and they despaired of giving him to a teacher. The child grew up in an unusual way. He hired himself out to be an *assistant* (a helper) to lead and bring the schoolchildren to the study hall and to lead them to the synagogue to recite "May His great Name" and the Kedushah and Amen in a pleasant voice. This was his service — holy service — with the schoolchildren, who are breath without sin. And every time the children walked, he would sing with them in a pleasant voice and with great longing, and it was heard from afar, for his service ascended higher and higher. For there was great satisfaction above, like the song the Levites used to sing in the Temple, and it was a time of great favor above.

The Satan, too, came among them, for he understood how far matters were reaching, and he feared for himself, lest the time come when he would be swallowed up from the earth. The Satan clothed himself in a certain sorcerer. When the Baal Shem Tov was walking with the children, with great longing and a pleasant voice of song, the sorcerer turned into a wild beast called a *werewolf* and fell upon them, and they all fled in fear of it. Some of them fell ill, God spare us, and the regular service was disrupted.

Afterward the Baal Shem Tov remembered the words of his father, of blessed memory, that he should fear nothing, for the Lord was with him, and he strengthened himself in the Lord his God. He went to the householders, the fathers of the children, and spoke to their hearts that they should give the children into his charge, for he would stand against it and slay it in the Name of the Lord — and why should the schoolchildren, which is a very great thing, be disrupted on its account? They heeded his words. He took in his hand a good, strong staff, and when he went with the children in song, in a pleasant voice, in gladness and rejoicing, the aforementioned beast fell upon them. He ran toward it and struck it on its forehead, and it died. The next day the gentile sorcerer was found, his carcass lying on the ground.

From then on he became the watchman of the study hall, and this was his way: as long as those who sat in the study hall were awake, he would sleep, and when those who were awake slept, then he who had slept awoke and performed his service, complete service, until the time came for people to awaken — then he returned to sleep. And they supposed that he slept the whole night from beginning to end.

---

## §2

(I heard from the rabbi R. Shimshon, the rabbi of the holy community of Rashkov, son of the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, a tale: There was a certain man named R. Adam, and from him came the manuscripts to the Baal Shem Tov, for he once came to a certain cave and found there manuscripts containing the secrets and mysteries of the Torah. Once the aforementioned R. Adam requested of the emperor a certain feast, and the emperor promised it to him — and he lived in a very small house. The feast was [based on] this: he had seen that one king prepared a feast for another king, and the preparation took two years, so that in that time large palaces could be built; and he made upper chambers of fine glass, called crystal, splendidly done, and over the chambers ran a channel of water with fish swimming in it to and fro; and he prepared sideboards of silver and gold, food and drink and servants befitting the king's honor; and they fixed a time, a certain day, for that king to come to the feast. For that very day the aforementioned R. Adam invited the emperor to a feast, and the emperor traveled with all the ministers of the realm to R. Adam for the feast.

Among the ministers of the realm was one minister who greatly hated Israel, and he wished to persuade the emperor not to go to the feast, for it would be a shame to him to learn that [R. Adam] had neither palaces nor feast; but the emperor did not heed him and set out. On the way he again tried to persuade him to return to the emperor's court, but he did not heed him either. Nevertheless, [the emperor] sent one minister to see whether he had prepared anything for the feast and whether there was room in his house, and the messenger came and said that he had a small house, as it had been before. Even so, the emperor traveled on, and when he came near the city he sent another messenger, who returned an answer as the first. When the emperor came to the street where R. Adam lived, he saw large palaces and a great courtyard, and he entered the courtyard with his ministers and servants. At once the servants took all the horses to place them in stables, and the servants each did the work assigned to them — only they were all mute — and they ate and drank all good things. R. Adam said to them, "Eat and drink as you please, only let none of you take any vessel from here."

Afterward R. Adam said: let each one say before me, "I want such-and-such a thing," and let him put his hand in the pocket of his garment, and he will find that thing. The emperor began and named a thing, and found it in the pocket of his garment, and so did all the ministers of the realm. When it came to that minister who hated Israel, he too named some thing, and [R. Adam] said to him, "Put your hand in the pocket of your garment." When he put his hand into the pocket of his garment, his hand was befouled, and he drew out his hand soiled with human excrement, and there was a great stench. The emperor commanded that he be thrust outside, and he washed his hand with water, but it was of no use. He began to plead before R. Adam, and R. Adam said to him, "If you take upon yourself to be no longer a hater of Israel, good; and if not, your hands will be befouled all the days of your life." And he took it upon himself. [R. Adam] said, "You have no remedy except that a Jew urinate on your hand, and with that you shall wash, and it will help you" — and so it was.

The emperor took two cups from one of the sideboards and hid them in his bosom. After the emperor departed, the palaces vanished, and nothing was missing in it except two cups and what they had eaten and drunk. When the *gazettes* (newspapers) of the world reported on the wonder he had done — that the palaces with all their preparations had vanished, and that the palaces had returned, all in their completeness, except that two cups were missing and what they had eaten and drunk — and when the gazettes reached the emperor, he sent a letter to the aforementioned king, in which was written: "There is with me a certain Jew who brought us your palaces, and we ate the feast that you prepared; and as a sign, I am sending you your two cups."

Once R. Adam's wife said to him, "How long shall we suffer in this distress?" He said to her, "What is your request? Whatever garment you wish, go to the chamber called a *kammer* and take the cloak from the pole, and it will appear as you ask." She went to the chamber and took the cloak from the pole as she pleased, and brought it into the house, and he urged her to return it, saying, "It is better for us to bear the distress in this world." She did so and returned the cloak. And he made a dream-question as to whom to hand over his manuscripts, and they answered: to hand them over to R. Israel son of Eliezer of the city Okup.)

Before his death he commanded his only son: "Behold, there are in my possession manuscripts that are the secrets of the Torah, but you are not worthy of them. Rather, inquire after a certain city named Okup, and there you will find a certain man called Israel son of Eliezer, who is about fourteen years old, and hand over to him the manuscripts, for they belong to the root of his soul. If you merit that he study with you, that is well."

After the passing of the rabbi R. Adam, his only son — who was greatly distinguished in learning and perfect in all virtues — took a wagon with horses and traveled from city to city until he reached the city Okup, and he lodged with the head of the rabbinical court. He asked him, "Whence are you, and where are you going? For by what appears, your intention is not to gather money." He answered him, "Behold, my late father, of blessed memory, was a famous righteous man, and he commanded me before his passing to take a wife here in the holy community of Okup, and I am bound to fulfill all his words." At once the whole city was astir, for there were before him several matches, and he became betrothed to a certain wealthy man, for he found favor in the eyes of all who saw him. After the wedding he began to search for the man he sought, and he found none but Israel, who served in the study hall. After he looked upon him with an open eye, it appeared to him that his inside was not like his outside, and that this might be the man he sought. He said to his father-in-law, "Behold, in your house the disturbance is hard for me, and I cannot study. Let your honor make me a small partition in the study hall, that I may study there in order, and 'in the place of song, there let there be prayer.'" His intention was that [Israel] would always be near him, so that he might fully ascertain his nature. His father-in-law did not delay in doing the thing, for he was very precious in his eyes. He did so, and he commanded the Baal Shem Tov to serve his son-in-law the rabbi, and for this he would pay him; and so he did.

Once at night, when all were asleep, the rabbi's son made as though he too were asleep. The Baal Shem Tov rose at his post, as was his way, over the Torah and the service, and [the son] observed him once and twice. On the third night the Baal Shem Tov rose and studied, and meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov fell asleep. The rabbi's son rose and took one quire of the manuscripts and laid it before him, and again made as though he were asleep. When the Baal Shem Tov awoke and saw the quire, he was greatly startled, and he studied them and hid them in his bosom. The rabbi's son did this again on another night, until he saw clearly that this was the man his father had commanded him to hand over the manuscripts to. He called to him and said, "Behold, my master, my father, of blessed memory, left me these manuscripts and commanded me to hand them over to you. Now, all the manuscripts are before you; but I beg of you, let my soul be precious in my master's eyes, that you study with me." He answered him, "The matter is good, but on condition that no person know but you, and that no movement of yours betray it, except that you command me to serve you as before." And so they did.

Afterward the rabbi's son said to his father-in-law, "Behold, I now desire greater seclusion. If it is possible to obtain some house outside the city by itself, I will sit there over the Torah and the service." He did so. He asked his father-in-law to hire the lad Israel to sit with him for company and to serve him whenever he needed to send [for something], and he did so, for the man was very honored. When the people of the community saw that R. Israel was studying with the rabbi's son, they said that surely the merit of his father had caused the rabbi's son to come here and to draw him near, for it seemed to them that now he walked in the way of the good; and they gave him a wife. But the days were not long for her, and his aforementioned wife died. When they entered their house of seclusion, they studied there all that was needed in Talmud with its commentaries and the codes, and also all the holy writings; and in the aforementioned manuscripts there was divine Kabbalah and also practical Kabbalah. Once the rabbi's son asked the Baal Shem Tov to bring down the Prince of the Torah, so that he might understand a certain matter. The Baal Shem Tov put him off, saying that if we err, Heaven forbid, in the intention, there could be danger, Heaven forbid, because of the absence of the ashes of the [red] heifer. But he urged him day by day, until he could not refuse him, and they fasted an unbroken fast from Sabbath to Sabbath and performed immersions properly. On the night after the Sabbath they directed certain known intentions, and at once the Baal Shem Tov cried out, "Woe, woe, for we have erred, and the Prince of Fire has descended, and he will surely burn the whole city! Therefore you, who are held by all to be a great pious one, hurry to your father-in-law and to the whole city, that they may save their lives, for soon he will burn the city." And so it was.

The man grew very great in the eyes of all as a man of God and a man of wonders. After the days grew long for him, he again urged the Baal Shem Tov to bring down the Prince of the Torah, and the Baal Shem Tov refused; nevertheless, he urged him several days until he was compelled to do his will, and they fasted an unbroken fast as before. On the night after the Sabbath, when they directed the known intentions, the Baal Shem Tov cried out, "Alas, for on account of this, death has been decreed upon the two of us this night. But this is our remedy: if we strengthen ourselves at our post to direct intentions all night, not to taste the taste of sleep, then 'when judgment is annulled, the judgment is void'; but if one dozes off, Heaven forbid — for sleep is from the side of death — there will be a hold for the adversary to rule over him completely." They did so all night, and before the light of morning the rabbi's son could not hold out, and he dozed off a little. When the Baal Shem Tov saw this, he ran in haste and made a great commotion, for [the son] had suddenly fainted. They roused him, but to no avail, and they buried him with great honor.

After this [the Baal Shem Tov] took another way, and went from there to a community near the holy community of Brod, and became there a teacher to good effect, and he found favor in the eyes of all, for he was an exceedingly distinguished scholar and a great sage, so that all the affairs of the community were decided and concluded by his word. When there was a quarrel between people and they came to him, he would bring their judgment to light, and both the innocent and the guilty had their hearts at peace with him, for in his great wisdom he would speak to their hearts so that he was pleasing in the eyes of all.

Now the rabbi R. Gershon of Kotov was head of the rabbinical court in the holy community of Brod, and his father, the rabbi R. Avraham, had a legal dispute with one of the people of the community where the Baal Shem Tov was. The rabbi R. Avraham came there and spoke with his opponent at law that he should travel with him to the holy community of Brod to settle between them according to Torah law. The man said, "Behold, there is with us here a certain teacher, distinguished in Torah, and he is a righteous judge; for every time a case comes before him, both sides have their hearts at peace with him, for he explains the ruling well. Let us come before him and set out our claims, and if it is not pleasing in your honor's eyes, then I will travel with you to the holy community of Brod." He heeded his counsel, and they came before him.

As soon as the rabbi R. Avraham came before the Baal Shem Tov, he saw by the holy spirit that [the Baal Shem Tov's] daughter [i.e. his own daughter] was his destined mate. Now in those days it was the way of great scholars, when a worthy guest came before them, to say a Torah insight before him; and the Baal Shem Tov said before him an interpretation of some passage of Maimonides with great sharpness, and said before him several more interpretations, until his soul was drawn after him and his soul was bound to his soul. Afterward the litigants set out their claims before him, and there were among them many claims and entanglements, and in about an hour he brought their judgment to light — a true judgment, true to its truth. He marveled greatly and loved him very much. Meanwhile it became known to him that the Baal Shem Tov was unmarried and needed to take a wife, and the rabbi R. Avraham had a divorced daughter. He came to him in secret and said to the Baal Shem Tov, "Behold, I have heard that your honor needs a wife; perhaps it is fitting in your honor's eyes to take my daughter." He answered him, "The matter is good; but since in this community many of the wealthy desire me, we will need to conceal the matter so that it not become known to anyone, for I cannot be brazen before them after they have always treated me well and exalted me on high. And if your honor desires this, then we will write the binding of the conditions, for good fortune, in private. And this too I stipulate with you: that you make the match with me, not with my Torah and my wisdom; for I in no way wish that you increase my praises and many virtues. Rather, write simply 'R. Israel son of R. Eliezer.'" Since his soul was bound to his soul, he agreed to all his words, and they wrote, for good fortune, the binding of the conditions plainly, and did not write the name of the place, and the matter became known to no one.

When the rabbi R. Avraham traveled home, he fell ill on the way with the illness of which he would die, and he passed away to his world. They informed of the matter to the holy community of Brod, to his son, the great rabbi R. Gershon of Kotov, and he came to his father to eulogize him as is proper. He received all the manuscripts he found in his possession, and meanwhile he found the conditions, [showing] how he had betrothed his daughter — who is the sister of the rabbi R. Gershon — to a certain man named Israel. It was a wonder in his eyes, for his father was a famous man — how could he make a match with one of low degree? And moreover, with some unknown person whose place and family were not known. He told the matter to his sister, and she said, "After it was approved in our father's eyes, there is nothing to question about it."

The Baal Shem Tov waited until the term of the teaching post had passed. He came to the householder and said, "I will go and return to my place." They spoke to his heart and offered him much in dowry and gifts to remain with them still, but he did not turn to them, for this was not his intention. He disguised himself and changed his clothing like one of the empty fellows, in a short jacket and a wide belt, and he changed his speech and his tongue, and went to the holy community of Brod, to the house of the aforementioned rabbi R. Gershon. Now two rabbinical courts sat before him, and he stood over them to examine continually the cases that came before them. [The Baal Shem Tov] stood by the door, and the rabbi R. Gershon supposed that he was a poor man, and took a small coin and wished to give it to him. [The Baal Shem Tov] said, "I have a secret matter with you." He went with him to a certain room and showed him the conditions, and said to him, "Give me my wife." When the aforementioned rabbi saw the man and his garb and his speech, he was greatly alarmed and astonished at the sight — what had his father, of blessed memory, done? He called to his sister and recounted to her the whole affair, and she answered him, saying, "After our father did this, there is nothing to question about him, and surely this came from the Lord, and perhaps from him will issue worthy offspring." They did so and set the time of the wedding.

Before the wedding canopy, the Baal Shem Tov said that he wished first to speak with his wife, as our Sages said. He spoke with his wife in secret and revealed to her the truth — how and what, everything in full — and he made her swear that she would not reveal any of this at all, even though periods upon periods would pass over her in which she would be in great distress. After the wedding the rabbi R. Gershon wished to study with him, [hoping] perhaps to receive some Torah; but he concealed the matter, as though he could receive nothing. The rabbi said to his sister, "Behold, in truth this is a great disgrace to me from your husband; and if you wish to be divorced from him, that is well; and if not, I will buy you a horse, and you will travel with him to dwell wherever you dwell, for I cannot bear the disgrace on your account." She agreed to this. They went wherever they went, and he fixed for her a place to dwell there, while he went to seclude himself among great mountains called the *Gevirg* (the highlands). And this was their livelihood: two or three times a week she would come to him on the horse with a wagon, and he would dig clay, and she would carry it to the city, and from this she had her livelihood.

The Baal Shem Tov was always fasting, with great unbroken fasts; and when he wished to eat, he would dig a hollow in the earth and put flour and water there, and it would bake from the heat of the sun — this was all his food after his fast — and all his days he was in seclusion. Now the mountains were exceedingly great mountains, and between them was a deep valley with no slope, but the cut-off ground was level. Once he entered into the depths of contemplation and was walking on the mountains, and on one side, on the second mountain, robbers and plunderers were standing; and when they saw him from afar walking to the edge of the mountain in deep thought, they said, "Surely he will fall into the valley and his limbs will be shattered, Heaven forbid." When he came near the valley, the second mountain drew near before him and became level, and he walked on, and the two mountains separated as before; and on his return, when he came near the valley, the one mountain drew near to its fellow and became level. And so it was several times, in his going and his returning.

They saw that he was a man of God, since the Lord was with him, and they made peace with him and came before him and recounted to him the whole affair, and said, "Behold, with our own eyes we have seen that you are a man of God. Therefore our request is that you pray on our behalf that the Lord prosper our way, for we always go to risk our lives." The Baal Shem Tov said to them, "If you swear to me that you will not harm a Jew and will not rob him, then I will do as your words." They swore on this matter, and from then on, if they had some quarrel or claim between them, they would come before him, and he would make a compromise between them.

Once two robbers came to be judged before him, and the ruling did not please one of them. He said in his heart, "Behold, when the Baal Shem Tov is awake it is impossible to do him harm, but when he is asleep I will fulfill my desire and kill him." And so he did. When the Baal Shem Tov slept, the robber came to him and raised the axe to cut off his head. At once he was seized — he knew not by whom — and they struck him with cruel blows, bruises and wounds, until scarcely the breath of life remained in him, and even the power of speech was not in him. The Baal Shem Tov awoke and saw the stricken man before him, filthy with blood and bruises and wounds, and he asked him, "Who are you, and who did this thing to you?" But he had no strength to answer him a word, and the Baal Shem Tov went on his way. The next day, when he had recovered a little of his strength, the robber recounted to him the whole affair.

---

## §3

(Once the robbers came to him and said, "Our master, behold, we know a short way to go to the Land of Israel through caves and tunnels. If you wish, come with us, and we will be guides before you on the way by which you shall go." He consented to go with them. Now in their going on the way there was a certain deep valley full of water and mire and clay, and their way of passage was over a single board laid from side to side, and they leaned on a stake that they would thrust into the marsh. They went first, and when the Baal Shem Tov wished to walk on it, he saw that here was the flame of the ever-turning sword, and he turned back, for there was great danger to him if he crossed by this passage. The Baal Shem Tov said in his heart, "Surely I did not come here for nothing." And on his return he met the aforementioned frog. And some say that) once the rabbi entered into great profundity and walked in this contemplation for three days and three nights, and knew nothing of it. Afterward he recognized that he was in a great wilderness, very far from his place. He marveled greatly that he had strayed into such a wilderness, and surely it was not an empty matter. Meanwhile there came before him an exceedingly large frog, so that he scarcely recognized what creature it was. He asked it, "Who are you?" It said that it was a Torah scholar and had been reincarnated in this frog. (The Baal Shem Tov said, "You are a Torah scholar," and with this utterance he raised it up much.) It recounted to him that it was five hundred years that it had been reincarnated in it.

And although the rabbi, the Ari (of blessed and holy memory), rectified all the souls, yet because of the greatness of its sins they sent it to wander far off, to a place where people are not found, so that they would not rectify it. He asked it, "What was your sin?" It said that once it was negligent in the washing of the hands, that it did not wash its hands properly, and the Satan accused it; and they answered him that he could not be blamed for a single transgression — but "one transgression drags along another." "If you can cause him to stumble in another transgression, then this one too will be reckoned to him; and if he remembers the Lord and repents of it, then he will be clean of this one as well." He tested it with another transgression, and it stumbled in it and did not withstand the test; and so it repeated and tripled, until it had transgressed almost the whole Torah. And the ruling was to thrust it away, not to accept its repentance. Yet even so, had it pressed upon the gates of repentance, they would have accepted it, as is known — for after a heavenly voice went forth, "Return, wayward children, except Acher" [Elisha ben Avuyah]. This was a punishment for the sin, to thrust it away; but had it pressed and repented, they would have accepted it, for there is nothing that stands before repentance. The Satan incited it, and it became a great drunkard, until it had no leisure to contemplate and repent, and it transgressed all the transgressions in the world.

And because the first sin — that it was negligent in the washing of the hands — caused the [other] sins, therefore when it died it was reincarnated in a frog, which is always in water, and in a place where people are not found; for when a Jew passes by, or recites some blessing, or thinks some good thought, by this he can "bring forth the precious from the vile." The Baal Shem Tov made a rectification for its soul and raised it up, so that the frog was left dead.

---

## §4

Afterward the rabbi R. Gershon rented for him a certain village, so that he might earn his living there, and there he acquired great perfection. For he built himself a house of seclusion in the forest, and there he would pray and study all the days, including the nights, the whole week; only from Sabbath to Sabbath he came to his home, and there too he had white garments for the Sabbath. He also had a bathhouse and an immersion house. His wife was occupied with the livelihood, and the Lord sent blessing and success in the work of her hands, and they would take in guests to feed them and give them drink with great honor. When some guest came, she would send for him, and he would come and serve before them, and they knew nothing of him. Now his way was, when he came to the city for Rosh Hashanah, to remain in the city the whole month.

Once, on the intermediate days of Sukkot, the rabbi R. Gershon saw that he was not putting on tefillin — and his way was to pray specifically at the eastern wall. He asked him, "Why do you not put on tefillin today?" He answered, "Because I have seen in the German books that whoever puts on tefillin on the intermediate days of a festival is liable to death." The rabbi was very angry at this, for he was a teacher of rulings who derived them from the books of Ashkenaz, and who knows what might come of it. He went with him to the rabbi of the community, that he might speak words of moral instruction to him — for he was held in their eyes to be God-fearing, except that "an ignoramus cannot be sin-fearing." The rabbi was a very great righteous man. When they came to the rabbi's house, R. Gershon kissed the mezuzah, while the Baal Shem Tov placed his hand on the mezuzah but did not kiss it, and the rabbi R. Gershon was vexed with him over this too. When they entered the rabbi's house, the Baal Shem Tov removed the veil [from his face], and the rabbi saw a great light and rose before him; and then [the Baal Shem Tov] put the veil back over his face, and the rabbi sat down. He did this several times, and the rabbi was greatly alarmed, for he did not understand how it was that at one moment he seemed a holy man and at another a lowly man. When R. Gershon presented before him the matter of the tefillin and the mezuzah, the rabbi took the Baal Shem Tov aside in secret and said to him, "I decree upon you that you reveal to me the truth." He was compelled to reveal it to him, but the Baal Shem Tov in turn decreed upon him that he too should reveal nothing of this at all. When they came outside, the rabbi said to R. Gershon, "I have reproved him; in my opinion he will not do anything outside the proper method, for he acts in his innocence." The rabbi went and took the mezuzah, and they saw that it was unfit.

---

## §5

The first thing I heard from the mouth of my father-in-law (may his memory be for the life of the World to Come), namely R. Alexander the slaughterer, who was with the pious holy rabbi, the master of the children of the exile, our teacher and our rabbi R. Israel Baal Shem Tov of Mezhibuzh, with whom he was a scribe for eight years — some years before the coming of the famous pious scribe R. Tzvi. Afterward they were both scribes, because [the Baal Shem Tov's] affairs were too great for one; and [my father-in-law] later became a slaughterer and inspector, because he had the trouble of raising children. The Baal Shem Tov gave him a place in the holy community of Sdeh Lavan, and afterward he was a slaughterer in the holy community of Berditchev. In his old age the Baal Shem Tov gave a letter into my father-in-law's hand for the great luminary rabbi of the holy community of Nemirov, the author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef*, and he became a slaughterer in the holy community of Nemirov.

I heard several times from the aforementioned rabbi (may his memory be for the life of the World to Come) that he said to my father-in-law, by way of moral instruction, at the third Sabbath meal: "Remember what the Baal Shem Tov said to you. Once I went to the Garden of Eden, and a great multitude of Israel went with me; and the more I drew near to the Garden of Eden, the fewer the people became, until I came to the Tree of Life, and very few remained with me. And among them I saw that Senderl too remained behind." [And the rabbi recounted:] he also did not have whole fingernails on his right hand, for two grew — one at the tip of the finger and one at the base of the finger, and between the two nails there was flesh separating them; and when the upper ones grew, he would cut them off every Sabbath eve until he had finished the upper nail, and meanwhile the lower one rose to the tip of the finger and separated from its root, and another began to grow. From what did this come to him? Once the Baal Shem Tov sent him to adjure a certain madman, and at night, in his sleep, the spirit frightened him; and he pictured in his mind the holy Names upon the image of his hand, upon each and every finger, as I have seen an image of a hand in the books, and he slapped the spirit on its cheek and struck the wall, until blood came out from under all his nails, and all the nails fell off; and from that day onward the nails grew in this manner, as above. And immediately the Baal Shem Tov came and said to him, "Were you frightened? Do not be concerned and do not fear."

Now I will recount the great things I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory, [about] how his revelation came about. For he dwelt in a small village at the inn, and his way was, when he had brought his wife the brandy, to go off to seclusion across the river called the Prut, and there he had on the mountain a rock like a small house in the mountain; and he would take for himself one loaf of bread for one meal, and would eat once a week. Thus he fasted several years, and on the Sabbath eve he would come to his home. His brother-in-law R. Gershon of Kotov held him to be an ignoramus and a boor, and would try to persuade his sister to be divorced from him; but she would not, for she knew about him, though she revealed it to no one.

Once he came to the city, and there was there a certain madwoman who would disclose to each and every person his abominations and his good deeds. When the Baal Shem Tov (may his memory be for the life of the World to Come) came to the city, the aforementioned R. Gershon asked the pious rabbi, head of the rabbinical court of the holy community of Kotov — the pious luminary rabbi R. Moshe — to bring the Baal Shem Tov to that woman, perhaps he would receive some moral instruction from her and return to the good. They all went, and when the rabbi of the holy community of Kotov entered, she said, "Blessed is he who comes, holy and pure," and so to each and every one according to his level. The Baal Shem Tov entered last, and when she saw him she said, "Blessed is he who comes, R. Israel" — for he was still tender in years — and she said to him, "Do you think that I fear you? No; for I know that they warned you from heaven not to occupy yourself with the holy Names until thirty-six years are complete for you." He hid himself among the vessels, and the aforementioned people asked her, "What are you saying?" and she repeated her words before them, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked her and said to her, "Will you not be silent? Behold, I will seat upon you [the matter of] a court, and they will permit me, and I will drive you out of this woman." She began to plead with him, "I will be silent." But the pious ones who had come with him urged him to permit himself [to act] and to drive it out of the woman; and the Baal Shem Tov asked them not to permit it, for it was very dangerous; but they did not desist from him. Then the Baal Shem Tov said, "See what you have caused. Therefore my counsel is that you depart in peace from the woman, and we will all study on your behalf." He asked it its name, and it said, "I cannot reveal it before many; let the people go from here, and I will reveal it to you, for it is an atonement for its sons who are in this city." When the people turned away from there, it revealed to him who it was — and my father-in-law, of blessed memory, also knew him; and the spirit had come about only because of the scoffing of one of the pious ones of the holy community of Kotov. And that spirit separated itself from this woman in peace. From then on they did not let him dwell in the aforementioned village, and a certain tax-farmer took him as a teacher for his sons.

My father-in-law, of blessed memory, also told me that this tax-farmer who took him as a teacher said to him that he had only one house available, which they held to be impure. The Baal Shem Tov said that he would dwell in it, and he set apart the upper chamber for the "outer ones" (the demonic forces, God spare us); and when they would play [pranks], he would rebuke them and they fell silent. There was also an incident: they were in great need of rain, and the gentiles were bringing out their idols around the village, as their custom is known, and the rain did not come. Once the Baal Shem Tov said to the tax-farmer, "Send to the surroundings that they come here for a prayer quorum," and he decreed a fast, and the Baal Shem Tov himself prayed before the ark, and they prolonged the prayer. A certain gentile asked, "Why did you pray at length today, and why was there great weeping among you?" He answered him the truth, that they had prayed for rain. The gentile scoffed greatly, saying, "We went with our idols and it was of no use — and what will you accomplish with your prayer?" The tax-farmer recounted the words of the gentile to the Baal Shem Tov, and he said to him, "Go say to the gentile that today there will be rain" — and so it was.

Afterward he fixed his dwelling in the holy community of Tluste, and there too he was a teacher; and he had not the means to make a prayer quorum in his house, so he gathered people to himself and prayed with them. He was clothed only in a short coat of homespun, and the toes of his feet protruded through the holes of his shoes, for he was very poor. He would go before prayer to the ritual bath even in the season of Tevet [midwinter], and during prayer drops of sweat would drip from him like beans. Afterward several sick people came his way, but he did not want to receive them — until one time they brought a madman or madwoman, and he did not want to receive him; and at night they told him that thirty-six years had been completed for him. In the morning he reckoned his account, and so it was, and he received that madman and healed him. He left the teaching post and took my father-in-law, of blessed memory, as a scribe, and people would travel to him from all places.

---

## §6

Once a certain tax-farmer came from a small city, upon whom witchcraft had been worked, so that whatever stood in his house — whether in some vessel, or money in a purse — once a night had passed over it, only half remained. He traveled to the Baal Shem Tov, who commanded the scribe to write protective amulets for all his rooms, and traveled with the tax-farmer. When they came to his house, he heaped the amulets upon the table and went to the marketplace. The members of the household saw that the spirit began to whirl about like a *zavirucha* (a snowstorm) from corner to corner, until it separated itself and went out of the house. There was a great courtyard around the house, with a wicket-gate in it; and when the Baal Shem Tov went to his lodging, there appeared to him something like a very large Cossack by the wicket-gate. The more the Baal Shem Tov drew near to the courtyard, the smaller the husk [demonic shell] became, until he came near and the husk vanished. The Baal Shem Tov set protective amulets there, and the members of the household recounted to him how the spirit had whirled about and gone out of the house. He also overturned things in the bed and in the rooms, among the barrels standing there, to drive out the husk, for such was his way; and he traveled home. Immediately a gentile woman, the wife of the mill-owner, came weeping and said to the tax-farmer in the Russian tongue (*"Ya modre ale te modreite"*), that he had gone to her house sullen and angry — "and I was not in his house, and he killed two creeping things."

---

## §7

A certain tax-farmer also came whose fortune had become very broken, and the Baal Shem Tov saw that the Angel of Death was dancing behind him, and he spoke to him compelling words, that he should repent: "And why should you go after the acquisitions of this world? Better to repent and mend your ways." But they suspected that the Baal Shem Tov wanted money, and [the man] wished to give him money, but he did not accept it from him. When he went out, the Baal Shem Tov said, "Oh, fool — today or tomorrow he will die, and he worries over his fortune." His brother-in-law, the rabbi R. Gershon, rebuked him and said to them [i.e. protested], "Why does he bring forth from his mouth words that are not good about a son of Israel?" He answered him, "What shall I do for you, that you are blind and did not see that the Angel of Death goes behind him?" And [the man] traveled home.

At that time the Baal Shem Tov's brother-in-law R. Yaakov was a widower from his wife, and he traveled to take a wife by way of the aforementioned tax-farmer, for they had one road. When they came to the lodging, they spent the night in a certain village near the village of the tax-farmer, and the aforementioned tax-farmer made a fitting feast for him. But the rabbi R. Yaakov did not want to eat meat, and they gave him dairy food at the end of the table, and the tax-farmer scoffed. Afterward they went to sleep, and at night the tax-farmer awoke with a great headache and a great cry, and he asked that they recite the confession with him; but there was no one there to recite the confession with him, so the rabbi R. Yaakov recited the confession with him, and he answered the confession after him in a melody. R. Yaakov wondered at this, and afterward his wife went from him, saying, "Perhaps he will sleep a little and it will be easier for him"; and after a short time she looked at him, and he was dead. The aforementioned R. Yaakov returned home to make known that "in truth we are blind."

---

## §8

An incident that happened: he traveled and came to the city of Krivosh. The head of the rabbinical court in the aforementioned city was the pious rabbi R. Abba, who was afterward head of the rabbinical court in the holy community of Sdeh Lavan. The aforementioned head of court had a guest, a relative by marriage, and they were debating sharply over a certain Mishnah; and when the Baal Shem Tov came, they did not go out to meet him. He entered and said, "The Lord be with you. With what were you occupied?" And they showed him another Mishnah. He answered them, "I know that you were occupied with another Mishnah; but this Mishnah — how do you study it?" And he showed them a great difficulty in this Mishnah. Afterward he told them the [correct] interpretation of the Mishnah, and traveled from them about a *parsah* [a measure of distance], and stopped to graze the horses in a field. They began to say to one another, "How did we study this Mishnah?" And at once they hired a wagon and pursued him, and found him in the field, and tarried with him. From that time the rabbi R. Abba would travel literally every month to the Baal Shem Tov for one Sabbath. Once he spent the Sabbath with him and erred in the evening prayer, beginning to say "You graciously grant" [a weekday passage]; and when he came home the Baal Shem Tov said, "Nicely did the rabbi of Krivosh pray 'You graciously grant.'"

When the Baal Shem Tov came to the holy community of Sdeh Lavan, there was there a certain righteous head of court named R. Chaim. The Baal Shem Tov prayed in the field, and when they came to the city they were still praying in the synagogue, and he went to hear the Kedushah and stood in the vestibule of the synagogue. He commanded the beadle to go and remove the lamp that was opposite the rabbi on the wall, and the beadle did so. When the rabbi lifted his feet from his prayer [i.e. finished], he saw that the lamp was not opposite him. He asked about the matter, and they told him that the Baal Shem Tov was standing in the vestibule. Immediately after the Kedushah he went out to him in the vestibule and asked him, and [the Baal Shem Tov] said, "You have a certain alien thought in your prayer on account of this lamp." There are differing opinions about this: some say that the scribe played with a dog while writing that lamp [i.e. that section of the prayer]; some say that he was a great wicked man.

The preacher of the aforementioned holy community was famous, and his name was R. Avraham of Podlisk, a great and famous righteous man. Once no slaughtering knife was pleasing in their eyes, and they sent for several slaughterers from several communities, but none could provide them a knife that pleased them. Once the Baal Shem Tov came to them and said, "The guest is very dear to us, but we have nothing with which to honor him." They recounted to him the affair, and he said that he would provide them a knife, and he provided them a knife, and it pleased them. They slaughtered and ate, and he promised to send them a slaughterer, and sent them my father-in-law, of blessed memory, and he grew wealthy there. My father-in-law, of blessed memory, told me that the aforementioned pious rabbi lived several years in his presence, until the time of his passing. He sent for the Baal Shem Tov, for he was gravely ill, and the Baal Shem Tov tarried with him a long time and busied himself with his healing. When the rabbi's sons saw that he had no effect, they sent for a doctor to the holy community of Ostrah, and he traveled to Sdeh Lavan. The Baal Shem Tov saw that the doctor was traveling, and said to the aforementioned rabbi, "I am traveling from here, for to here travels the doctor of the holy community of Ostrah." The rabbi did not know of this and urged him greatly not to travel. He stayed the night there because of his great urging, and he said that the doctor was lodging two *parsah* from the city. Now the doctor had long before raised his hand against the Baal Shem Tov, saying, "When I see the Baal Shem Tov I will kill him with the firearm in my hand." In the morning the Baal Shem Tov hurried to travel, but the sick rabbi detained him until the doctor came to the city. The Baal Shem Tov said, "Behold, the doctor has come to the city," and he bade the rabbi farewell and sat on the wagon and traveled. When he came opposite the doctor's lodging, he descended from the wagon and entered the house and greeted him. [The doctor] asked him, "From where do you know [medicine], doctor?" and he answered, "The Blessed Name taught me," and he traveled home. The sons of the sick rabbi brought him [the doctor] to their father, and whatever the doctor said to do, they told him, "The Baal Shem Tov already did this for him." And the doctor went off crestfallen. Then the sick rabbi complained against his sons, "What have you done? Even though there was no healing for me from the Baal Shem Tov, nevertheless when the Baal Shem Tov entered to me, I knew that the Divine Presence came with him; but when the doctor entered to him, it seemed that a certain priest had entered to me."

After his passing, the aforementioned rabbi R. Abba of the holy community of Krivosh was accepted among them as head of the rabbinical court. On his account I will write the tale as well — a tale of the Baal Shem Tov that I heard from a certain man of our community, named the pious R. Yitzchak Aizik, son of the rabbi of the holy community of Brehin, who heard it from the mouth of his father. I did not wish to write the body of the tale, for a reason hidden with me: how the rabbi of the holy community of Brehin was in Sdeh Lavan, with the rabbi of Sdeh Lavan, in the winter, and he showed him a letter from the Baal Shem Tov; and these are its words, [written] in that aforementioned winter: "You will come from the road and find in your house, in your courtyard, a great commotion — men, women, and children — and you will faint from fright. When they rouse you, then command at once that blood be let from you from two veins, and at once send a special messenger to inform me about the matter of the commotion. Although, praise God, I have eyes to see from afar, nevertheless send me a letter by messenger. Spoken by Israel Baal Shem Tov." And the root of the tale I did not wish to reveal.

---

## §9

I heard from the mouth of the great luminary pious rabbi of our community, R. Gedaliah: Once the Baal Shem Tov requested of the rabbi of the holy community of Kotov and asked of him the book of the Zohar; and on his way home his brother-in-law R. Gershon met him and asked him, "What do you have under your arm?" He did not want to tell him, and R. Gershon descended from the wagon and drew out the book of the Zohar from under his arm and took it from him, and said in a tone of astonishment, "You need the book of the Zohar?" Afterward, [R. Gedaliah] said that the Baal Shem Tov came to the city to pray in the study hall, and during the recitation of the Shema he gave a great sigh; and after the prayer the rabbi R. Moshe, head of court of the aforementioned holy community, asked him, "What is this great sigh I heard from you during the prayer?" and he coaxed him with words, and he told him the truth: that this sigh was from the heart — he said to him, "The mezuzah is unfit," and he found it as he said. And he gave him the aforementioned book of the Zohar and blessed him that the aforementioned R. Gershon would not encounter him.

---

## §10

I heard from the mouth of the aforementioned R. Gedaliah: Once he had neither matzah nor meat for Passover, and he harnessed his donkey to the wagon and traveled on his way to the villages, and slaughtered in the villages, and brought back flour and meat for Passover. When he came, he said to his wife, "See that the flour is removed from the wagon." But rain fell and the flour became leavened, and she informed her husband. He inspected the knife and traveled to the villages on the other side and brought flour for the festival. When he came near his home — and there was a great hill there — the horse could not pull the wagon, and he himself pulled the wagon to help the horse. Halfway up the hill the horse died, and he was compelled to pull the wagon himself, for he feared to leave the flour without watch; and his strength gave out, and he stood by the wagon and wept a great weeping before the Blessed Name. In the midst of his weeping, sleep fell upon him and he slumbered, and Elijah, of blessed memory, came to him in a dream — and this was the first revelation to him — and said to him, "Your tear has been accepted and your prayer received. Behold, I will send you a certain gentile who will take your wagon with the flour to your house." Immediately when he woke from his sleep, a certain gentile came with a wagon and horses and said, "Israel, tie your wagon to my wagon and I will bring you to your house."

When he came home, the gentile said, "What will you give me to flay the hide from your horse?" and he gave him a *zekser* [a small coin]. Afterward a gentile came and bought the hide for the sum of four gold pieces, and the buyer said to him, "See that you clothe yourself and your wife for the coming festival with this money." In time, traders of caravans brought hides of marten, and [the man's wife] went to her husband and asked him whether to buy or not — for in winter he had his house of seclusion in a village, with a gentile who rented him a house there — and he said to her, "You may buy, for it is ownerless, since robbers would all kill him [the seller]." She bought, with that money, for the sum of four gold pieces. He made himself a coat of fur and his wife a dress of fur for the festival. When the tax-farmer who held the inn from him saw that he had clothed himself and his wife, he suspected that he was buying brandy from some place other than him. The tax-farmer brought a complaint before R. Gershon and said, "I show you respect; but if he does not confess and pay me my losses, I will have him struck in the fortress [imprisoned]." R. Gershon, of blessed memory, said to him, "I will reprove him." When the Baal Shem Tov came to the city, he reproved him, and [the Baal Shem Tov] said to him, "There was never any such thing, Heaven forbid." R. Gershon said, "From where did you get money to clothe yourself and your wife?" and he answered him, "The Lord gave to me." He said, "Why does the Lord not give to me?" and he answered him, "To you He does not give, and to me He gives." He said, "See that the tax-farmer will inform against you in the fortress and you will be flogged." He said, "I do not fear him." In the end [the tax-farmer] wished to inform against him, and the aforementioned tax-farmer died.

---

## §11

I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory, that the Baal Shem Tov had a certain horse, and it was stolen from him. R. Gershon was reproving him, and the word was suddenly cast from his mouth, "The horse will be returned to him." The aforementioned R. Gershon laughed at him, and every time he came to the city he would provoke him and say to him, "Israel, has the horse been returned to you?" The horse was gone for about a year. In the end a certain gentile came riding on the aforementioned horse and knocked at the window to get fire for his pipe, and [the Baal Shem Tov] recognized his horse and said, "Surely it is my horse." At once [the gentile] descended from the horse and returned the horse to him.

---

## §12

I heard from the rabbi R. Gedaliah, and also from my father-in-law, an incident that happened. R. David, preacher of the holy community of Kolomyya, traveled to collect Hanukkah money and strayed on the road and came to the house of the Baal Shem Tov, may his soul rest, while he was sitting in his house of seclusion. He asked [the Baal Shem Tov's wife] about her husband, where he was, and she answered that he had to go to the tax-farmer to help him water his cattle. The aforementioned preacher said, "What can I eat here?" and she answered, "My husband slaughtered a fowl; look at the knife, and if it is pleasing in your eyes, I will cook the hens." He looked at the knife and it pleased him. The righteous woman went and informed her husband, and he came home and made himself, too, as though he had come from the tax-farmer, and served before him as a junior serves before an elder. He himself made up the bed for him and set out water for him and a vessel for pouring, and he [the Baal Shem Tov] lay with his wife in one bed in the manner of a villager. At midnight the Baal Shem Tov rose — for I heard that he would sleep only two hours — and sat at the base of the stove in silence, occupied with what he was occupied with, in silence. The aforementioned preacher R. David awoke and saw a great light beneath the stove, and supposed that the wood chips on the stove had been kindled and were burning, and he began to rouse his wife. He said, "Hannah, Hannah, fire is burning on the stove!" and she answered him, "I am a woman; before I clothe myself and cover myself with a veil, it will burn more and the fire will grow stronger, God spare us. Let the rabbi pardon me; for there is water by him, and he can put it out." He took the vessel with the slop water and went to put out the fire. When he came to the stove, he saw that the Baal Shem Tov was sitting and a light shone upon him — and the rabbi told this from mouth to ear, that the light stood over him like a bow [an arch]. When the preacher saw this, he fainted, and Hannah had to rouse the Baal Shem Tov and confuse him [break his concentration] so that he would rouse the aforementioned preacher from his faint. In the morning the aforementioned R. David said, "Thus I saw; tell me what this is." He answered, "I do not know. I was reciting Psalms; perhaps I cleaved to the Blessed Name, and this was it." R. David decreed upon him [to tell the truth], and he revealed himself before him; and from that day onward the aforementioned preacher R. David would travel to him every time to hear from him words of Torah. When his fellows heard his Torah from him, they said, "From where did you get this?" and he answered, "From a certain poor and destitute man." From then on, when R. David heard that R. Gershon was reproving the Baal Shem Tov with insults, he said to him, "Leave him be, for he is wiser than you" — for the Baal Shem Tov had decreed upon him not to reveal it.

---

## §13

In the holy community of Mezhibuzh there was a certain wealthy man who was not at peace with the Baal Shem Tov. With him was a teacher, a distinguished scholar in all his ways, enlightened, who also kept himself far from the Baal Shem Tov; and the rabbi greatly desired to draw him near to the service of God, for he was fit for all good. But it was a great wonder in his eyes how the matter could come about, for he kept himself very distant — and especially since even the very air of his house served him as an excellent protection.

Once, on a Sabbath night, [the teacher] dreamed that he was strolling through the whole city of Mezhibuzh, and he saw a certain palace, beautiful and splendid with the greatest possible splendor. He marveled greatly at it, and his eye was not sated with seeing it, for the more he looked, the more wonders he saw. Afterward he took to heart that all this splendor was only from the very material of the building; how much more so when he would gaze upon the beauty of the craftsmanship and the engravings. When he set his heart to delve into the beauty of the craftsmanship, he marveled greatly at it, for in every small surface there was wisdom and craft such as the like of it had never been seen in all the world, and his heart perceived much wisdom, and he was drawn after it with literal expiration of the soul. Again he said in his heart, "Since the exterior of the palace is so splendid, how much more so the interior of the palace — surely there is nothing to compare to it." When he approached the window to see its interior, behold, the Baal Shem Tov and all his holy company were reclining at the table, and the Baal Shem Tov was saying words of Torah. His heart was inflamed to enter inside, and he ran to the door; and when he wished to enter, the servant pushed him and did not let him enter inside, and he was greatly grieved at this. Even so, in his great longing and desire to hear the word of the Lord, words of the living God, he stood by the window and heard all his holy words. He awoke, and behold, it was a dream. He began to repeat the words of Torah he had heard, and they were sweeter than honey, and he repeated them two and three times. But since it was still about midnight, he was compelled to sleep again. In the morning, when he rose from his bed, behold, he remembered the dream with complete clarity, but the words of Torah were entirely forgotten from him, and he was greatly grieved at this, until he was literally confused from the abundance of grief, and he did not know how he prayed. At the morning meal he sat astonished. The householder said to him, "Why is this day different? If your honor dreamed some bad dream, we will make it good before three who are well-disposed to him." But he answered him nothing. He could perhaps have gone of his own accord to the Baal Shem Tov, except that he remembered the rejection — that the servant had rejected him in the dream — and he greatly feared lest it be so when awake too, and disgrace and contempt be enough for him; and so he was the whole day in great grief.

At the time of the third meal, the Baal Shem Tov commanded, "Go to the house of the known wealthy man and call the teacher for me, that he come here." This was a great wonder in their eyes. But immediately, when the messenger opened the door and said to the teacher, "The Baal Shem Tov has called your honor to come to him at once," he leaped over the table and ran like a madman without his coat; and then he heard all those words of Torah he had heard in the night, and at once he fainted. When his spirit returned within him, the Baal Shem Tov said to him with humor, "If you had heard something new, you would have been so moved; but this is nothing new, for you already heard all this in the night." And he understood that it was the word of the Lord, and from then on he drew near to him with all his heart and became a completely righteous man.

---

## §14

In the aforementioned holy community there was a certain great merchant, and he was among those people who were still opposed to the Baal Shem Tov. The aforementioned merchant had an only son, wise and distinguished in learning and in other virtues. Once the only son traveled to Breslau for merchandise, and his way was to delay on such a journey for four weeks. Once he delayed more than ten weeks, and there was no word from him at all, and the household was greatly grieved at this. Once his mother, the merchant's wife, said, "Why do you harden your heart against the Baal Shem Tov? The whole world travels to him, literally from the four corners of the earth, and he works deeds for them; and you do not wish to go to his house to ask of his mouth and to inquire about our son, for it is no empty matter." But he paid no heed to this. As the days went on, she pressed him greatly, and he was compelled to do his wife's will. He went on the Sabbath eve to the Baal Shem Tov and said, "My master, it is more than ten weeks that my beloved son traveled to Breslau, and I have no word from him, and we do not know what has befallen him." At once the Baal Shem Tov commanded that they bring him the book of the Zohar, and he opened it and looked in it, and answered him, "Your son is, with God's help, alive and well, and this Sabbath he will spend in a village near our city, one *parsah* away." But [the merchant] did not believe his words, for how could it be so — surely his son was not unaware of their great longing, and "as water reflects the face" [his son surely longed too], and surely money would count for nothing, and [his son] would give for the ferry whatever they asked in order to come home for the Sabbath. Nevertheless, for the sake of thoroughness, he sent a gentile to the aforementioned village and commanded him to delay there until night, perhaps [the son] would come before night. At night the gentile returned and said that there was no sound and no answer — nothing heard and nothing seen of his son. Then the merchant said to his wife, "This is the very thing I said before — that all his words are mere coaxing words with nothing real in them."

But in truth this is how the matter was with his son: the wagon broke several times on the road, and he came to the aforementioned village at midnight; and immediately, on the night after the Sabbath, his son came home. Then he saw, in retrospect, that all the words of the Baal Shem Tov were true and just, and he went to the Baal Shem Tov and asked his forgiveness, since he had spoken slander about him, and he recounted to him the whole affair. He said to him, "Fool — behold, the light that the Holy One, blessed be He, created in the six days of Creation, with which a man could look from one end of the world to the other, was hidden away for the righteous in the future to come. And where did He hide it? In the Torah. Therefore when I open the book of the Zohar, I see the whole world, and I will not err in what I see, with God's help."

---

## §15

I heard from our teacher R. Falk, the famous pious one of the holy community of Tytschilnik, who heard from R. Avraham, head of court of the holy community of Dubossary, who was formerly the prayer leader of the study hall of the holy community of Mezhibuzh. Once they needed to recite the Hallel, for it was the New Moon or the intermediate days of Passover, and the aforementioned R.
