Avraham was praying the morning prayer before the ark, while the Baal Shem Tov prayed at his place. Now his custom was to pray himself before the ark from the Hallel onward; and during the silent Amidah, recited aloud, the Baal Shem Tov began to tremble greatly, and he kept trembling, as was his way to tremble always in his prayer — and whoever saw him at the time of prayer saw his trembling. When the aforementioned R. Avraham finished the repetition of the prayer, the Baal Shem Tov was standing in his place and trembling and not going to the ark. The pious R. Wolf Kitzes came and gazed at his face, and behold, [his eyes] were burning like torches, and his eyes were bulging and open and standing motionless, like one expiring, God spare us. The aforementioned R. Zev signaled to R. Avraham, and they took him, one by one hand and one by the other, and led him to the ark, and he went with them and stood before the ark and trembled a while, and then opened and recited the Hallel, all in trembling. Afterward, when he finished the Kaddish, he stood and trembled a long time, and they had to wait with the reading of the Torah until he rested from his trembling.

And not only this, but I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef*, that once a large vessel of water stood while the Baal Shem Tov was praying, and they saw that the water was stirring and moving — for truly the resting of the Divine Presence was upon him, and from this the earth trembled, as it is written, "because the Lord descended upon it in fire, and the whole mountain trembled greatly." Thus it was not perceptible [on the earth], but in the water it was very perceptible.

I also heard from the preacher R. Eliyahu, of blessed memory: when he first came to the Great Maggid of the holy community of Mezeritch, he told him this tale and said: Once on a festival — and I do not know whether it was the first day of Passover or Shemini Atzeret, when one must bless [for] rain or dew — the Baal Shem Tov was praying before the ark with great longing, for so I heard several times from his chosen ones, that he would lift up the head of them all and would pray with a great cry. The great rabbi, the Maggid, could not bear it, for he was very ill, and he went out of the study hall and entered a small room within the study hall and prayed there alone. Before the Additional prayer, the Baal Shem Tov went and came to the small room to don the *kittel* [white robe]. The Maggid said that he recognized that the resting of the Divine Presence was upon him, and when he saw him he recognized in him that he was not in this world. When he donned the kittel, the kittel was creased upon his shoulders, and the great Maggid took hold of the kittel to smooth it from its crease; and when he touched it, he began to tremble, and he grasped the table that was there, and the table too began to tremble with him; and the Baal Shem Tov went off, until [the Maggid] had to pray and beg of the Blessed Name to take this thing from him, for it was not in his power to bear it.

I also heard from the pious R. David Forkes of Mezhibuzh that once the Baal Shem Tov prayed on the road, in a house, at the eastern wall, and near the western wall stood barrels full of grain, and they saw that the grain trembled. I also heard from him — but not literally from his mouth — that when he was a slaughterer in the holy community of Tultshin, the aforementioned R. David was being praised, and R. David of the village Khladovka made the betrothal conditions and sent wagons for all the invited guests, and the aforementioned preacher R. David also traveled to the aforementioned conditions and told [this] on the way. When the people of the community came, they told me how once, on the Day of Atonement at dawn, the Baal Shem Tov did not come to the study hall as is the custom on the Day of Atonement, to pray at dawn. He tarried until the day was far advanced, and they waited for him before praying; and afterward he came and sat in his place, with his head on the lectern, raising his head and laying down his head, raising and laying, several times. Afterward he signaled that they go to pray before the ark, and the aforementioned R. David came to pray, for he was the prayer leader on the Days of Awe; and when he came near the ark, the Baal Shem Tov began to shame him before all the people and the assembly, saying in these words, "Old fool, where are you going?" and he disgraced and reviled him about half an hour. [R. David] wished to turn and go from the lectern, for he was greatly angered, for he said, "Perhaps, Heaven forbid, he has seen in me something disgraceful." But [the Baal Shem Tov] rebuked him and said, "Stand!" And he began to pray and wept, and did not know what he was saying, but only cried and wept, for his heart was greatly broken. Afterward, at the conclusion of the Day of Atonement, the aforementioned preacher R. David went to the Baal Shem Tov and said to him, "Our rabbi, what disgraceful thing did you see in me, Heaven forbid?" He answered and said, "Heaven forbid, I saw nothing in you; only at dawn I saw the *samech-mem* [the adversary] on the road, at the place where the prayers go to be received — [he was waiting] to receive all the prayers, Heaven forbid. And I said in my heart, why should I go to pray and hand over to him the prayers, Heaven forbid? Therefore I tarried from praying. And thank God, I cleared another way, that the prayers should go by this way, so that he could not seize them. And therefore I feared lest, Heaven forbid, [doubt] enter your heart and spoil the matter; so I broke your heart, so that there should be no alien thought in your heart, and afterward I commanded you to pray."

---

## §16

I also heard from the mouth of the pious rabbi R. Gedaliah, of blessed memory: Once the Baal Shem Tov prayed the afternoon prayer in a certain village, in a room called a *speicher* (granary), and there were many barrels full of grain there. During the prayer there was a great dancing among the barrels because of the trembling — as it is written, "because the Lord descended upon it in fire, and the whole mountain trembled greatly." And thank God, I saw [in him] the aspect of the soul of Moses, of blessed memory.

I also heard from the mouth of the aforementioned rabbi R. Falk, who fled to the land of Crimea with R. Yosef Ashkenazi, the prayer leader of the Baal Shem Tov, at the time of the flight, and heard from his mouth — and I heard from him: Once R. Yosef dreamed that he saw a certain pious man who had passed away and left a small son, a wise child, and a match of low degree had jumped at him [been arranged for him]. The pious man came to R. Yosef in a dream and asked him to go to his wife and tell her, in his name, not to make that match, for it was not pleasing to him; rather, with the man who would come to her house first that day, with that one she should make a match. Afterward he saw in the dream the likeness of an altar, and that departed one ascended the altar and put his head between his knees and began to cry out the Selichah, "Answer us, O Lord, answer us; answer us, our Father, answer us," and the whole alphabet to its end. Afterward he said, "Answer us, God of our fathers, answer us; answer us, God of Abraham, answer us; answer us, Fear of Isaac, answer us; answer us, Mighty One of Jacob, answer us; answer us, You who discern tears, answer us; answer us, God of the Chariot, answer us," and he soared to the firmament. In the morning [R. Yosef] went to the Baal Shem Tov and recounted the dream before him. He said to him, "You suppose that you dreamed a dream — not so; you saw a vision. I too saw it, and before my eyes it was made tangible. The altar is the altar upon which Michael offers the souls before the Blessed Name." [R. Yosef] asked him why [the departed] said those "answer us" verses, and he answered that no angel and no souls can ascend from world to world except by means of the "answer us" prayers. He asked him why he said this particular text, and he answered that above, this is the text. And the Baal Shem Tov sent for the wife of the dead man and told her the matter concerning the match.

---

## §17

I also heard from his mouth [R. Falk]: Once, on the eve of the Day of Atonement, the Baal Shem Tov saw a great accusation against Israel, that the Oral Torah would be removed from them, and he was greatly grieved all the eve of the Day of Atonement. Toward evening, when the whole city came to bless them, he blessed one or two and said, "I cannot, because of grief," and he did not bless them. He went to the synagogue and spoke before them compelling words, and fell upon the holy ark and cried out, saying, "Woe! For they wish to take the Torah from our hand. How can we endure among the nations even half a day?" And he was greatly angered at the rabbis, saying that it was on their account, for they fabricate falsehood from their own hearts with false premises. And he said that all the Tannaim and Amoraim had risen to judgment. Afterward he went to the study hall and spoke more compelling words, and they prayed Kol Nidrei. After Kol Nidrei he said that the accusation was growing and strengthening, and on account of this he hurried all the prayer leaders so that he might stand at the closing Ne'ilah prayer while the day was still long, for he himself always prayed the Ne'ilah prayer. Before Ne'ilah he began to speak compelling words and wept, and he laid his head back upon the ark and groaned and cried out; and afterward he began to pray the silent Amidah, and then began the Amidah aloud. His custom was always, on the Days of Awe, not to look in the prayer book, for the rabbi R. Yekel of Mezhibuzh would recite the liturgical poem from the prayer book before him, and he would answer after him. When he reached the words "Open for us the gate" — or the words "Gates of heaven, open" — and R. Yekel said it once and twice, it was noticed that he was not answering after him; he fell silent and began to make awesome movements, bending backward until his head came near his knees, and the people saw, and feared lest he fall to the ground, and wished to take hold and support him, and they were afraid. They informed R. Zev Kitzes, of blessed memory, and he came and looked at his face and signaled that they not touch him; and his eyes were bulging, and with his voice he made movements like a slaughtered ox, and this lasted about two hours. And suddenly he stirred and straightened himself at his post and prayed with great speed and finished the prayer. At the conclusion of the Day of Atonement they all came to greet him, for such was always the custom, and they asked him about the accusation, how it had turned out.

He recounted to them: "As I stood at the Ne'ilah prayer, I was able to pray, and I went from world to world without any hindrance, through the whole silent Amidah; and in the Amidah aloud I also went on, until I came to a certain palace, and I had one more gate to enter, so that I would come before the Blessed Name. And in that palace I found prayers of fifty years that had had no ascent; and now, because we prayed this Day of Atonement with proper intention, all the prayers ascended, and each prayer was shining like the dawn newly established. I said to those prayers, 'Why did you not ascend before?' and they said, 'For so we were commanded, to wait for your honor to lead us up.' I said to them, 'Come with me.' And the gate was open." He told the people of his city that the gate was as large as the whole world. "And as we began to go with the prayers, a certain angel came and shut the door and hung a lock upon the gate." He told them that the lock was as large as the whole of Mezhibuzh. "I began to turn the lock to open it, but I could not, and I ran to the rabbi known [as the author] in the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef*, and I begged him and said, 'Israel is in such great distress, and now they do not let me enter; for at another time I would not press myself to enter.' And the rabbi said, 'I will go with you, and if it is possible to open for you, I will open.' And when he came and turned the lock, he too could not open it, and then he said of me, 'What can I do for you?' I began to grow bitter before the rabbi: 'How could you abandon me in such a time of distress?' and he answered, 'I do not know what to do for you; but I and you will go to the palace of the Messiah — perhaps there will be salvation from there.' I went with a great commotion to the palace of the Messiah, and when the Messiah, our righteous one, saw me from afar, he said to me, 'Do not cry out,' and he gave me two letters, and I came to the gate. And thank God, I opened the lock and opened the gate and led up all the prayers; and because of that great joy that all the prayers were raised up, the mouth of the prosecutor was silenced, and I did not need to plead, and the decree was annulled, and there remained only the trace of the decree." From that trace I remember that there was a great commotion in the world, for several people sent their books to the land of Wallachia. And this accusation was from the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi — may his name be blotted out — and a certain bishop of the city of Kamenets burned two volumes of the Talmud. The rabbi of our community said that he took a Talmud by force from the tax-farmer of his village and paid him for the Talmud and placed it on the fire, and, because of our many sins, it was all burned. And thank God, immediately when the bishop stood before the fire he was harmed, and they carried him to the city of Kamenets, but they did not bring him there alive, and that wicked man died on the way. Afterward there was a disputation before the bishop of the holy community of Lvov, but the dread of the Torah fell upon him and he did not act wickedly. And that whole wicked sect apostatized, for [the disputation] brought upon them disgrace and great contempt, in that he shaved off one sidelock and half a beard for them, so that it would be recognized that they were neither Jew nor gentile, and out of shame they apostatized.

But once the pious rabbi R. Menachem Nachum of Chernobyl was in our city and recounted this tale before the people, and I came in the middle of the telling and heard from him some variation, and also a new matter: that the Messiah said to the Baal Shem Tov, "I do not know whether you will open; but if you open, there will surely be salvation for Israel." He also said that this gate was of the palace of the Bird's Nest, into which none ever entered except the Messiah, as is said in the holy Zohar; and he said that he heard the voice of God, who said to him, "What shall I do with you, that I am compelled to do your will?" And from the rabbi of our community the implication is that he was still grieved and angry at the guests because the guests had traveled from his house; and afterward he sent a messenger, and the messenger heard that his wife reproved him for being angry at the guests, and he leaned on the table and said, "Behold, I am ready to receive reproof," and afterward he sent to invite the guests back, and in the morning he stood in prayer with the congregation. And regarding those who apostatized, I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov said that the Divine Presence wails and says: as long as the limb is attached [to the body], there is hope that it will have some healing; but when the limb is cut off, it has no remedy ever — for each one of Israel is a limb of the Divine Presence.

---

## §18

I heard from the mouth of the father-in-law of R. Falk the priest, the pious R. Shemeril, preacher of the holy community of Wirtiwka, that once he spent the Sabbath in the holy community of Balta and said that this city would one day be destroyed, and afterward would be rebuilt and would be a city and a mother in Israel, and would stand until the coming of the Redeemer, soon in our days. The rabbi of our community explained how the statement came about: there were great fairs in the holy community of Balta, and there were there many gentile merchants and Ishmaelites and Tatars and other nations; and they were rushing about in wagons during the third meal, at the time when the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, was saying Torah, and they were disturbing him. He grew angry and said, "What is with you, Balta? Trouble will yet sprout upon you."

---

## §19

I heard from the aforementioned R. Shemeril, one year before the flight, that when war was made between Ishmael and Greece [the Ottomans and the Russians], the aforementioned city was destroyed, and also its surroundings, and the city of Tytschilnik and all the villages around it; there remained in Balta only a small house in front of the courtyard.

---

## §20

I heard from the aforementioned R. Falk: When R. Gershon of Kotov traveled from the Land of Israel to outside the Land, to make matches for his sons, he said, "Along the way, thank God, that I have crossed the sea, I will travel to my brother-in-law the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory." He came to him on the Sabbath eve. The Baal Shem Tov stood to pray the afternoon prayer and prolonged his prayer until the stars came out, while R. Gershon also prayed, within the prayer book of the Ari, of blessed memory, and afterward reviewed the weekly portion [twice in scripture and once in Targum], and afterward commanded that they bring him cushions, and lay down to rest. On the Sabbath night, during the meal, R. Gershon asked his brother-in-law the Baal Shem Tov, "Why did you prolong your prayer so much? I too prayed with intentions and read the portion twice in scripture and once in Targum, and had to lie down and rest, while you were standing and trembling and making your movements" — for he wished to draw words from his mouth. The Baal Shem Tov laughed at him and answered him nothing. [R. Gershon] pressed and asked again, and then the Baal Shem Tov answered him, "When I reach the words 'who revives the dead' (R. Falk was uncertain whether it was the words 'who revives the dead' or 'who revives the dead with mercy'), and I direct the intention of unifications, then the souls of the dead come by thousands and ten-thousands, and I must speak with each and every one — why he was driven from his portion — and I make a rectification for him and pray on his behalf and raise him up, the more important first. And they are so many that if I wished to raise them all, I would need to stand at the Amidah three years; but when I hear the proclamation, and the holy one proclaims that it is forbidden to raise up [more] souls, I step out of the Amidah."

R. Gershon said to him, by way of jest, "Why do they not come to me?" and he answered him, "Stay with me here another Sabbath, the second, and I will hand over to you the intentions in writing on paper, and they will come to you too." And so he did. The next Sabbath eve, when the Baal Shem Tov finished the Kaddish before the prayer, R. Gershon stood to pray as well, but the Baal Shem Tov had not yet begun the prayer, for he knew that R. Gershon could not bear it and would be frightened, and meanwhile he fixed his watch and smelled tobacco, until R. Gershon passed the place of the intention; and when [R. Gershon] directed this intention, he saw that the dead came to him like a great flock of sheep, and he fell into faintness. Then the Baal Shem Tov roused him and commanded him to go home. At night, at the table during the meal, the Baal Shem Tov asked him why he had grown faint, and he said, "When I directed the intention, the dead came to me like flocks." Then the Baal Shem Tov said to his men, by way of jest, "Strike him, that he not laugh at the Baal Shem Tov."

---

## §21

I heard from the famous pious and wise rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, who was head of court in the holy community of Sharigrad: He heard that the Baal Shem Tov had come to the holy community of Mohilev, and he said in his heart, "I too will travel," for he was not yet a pious follower. He traveled and came to him on the Sabbath eve, before the morning prayer, and he saw that the Baal Shem Tov was smoking a pipe, and this was an astonishing thing to him. Afterward, during the prayer, he wept a great weeping such as he had never wept in his life, and he understood that this weeping was not his own. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov traveled to the Land of Israel, and I remained desolate until he returned. Then I began to travel to him and sat with him a time, and the Baal Shem Tov would say that he needed to raise me up, and I sat about five weeks, and asked when his honor would raise me up. The rest I heard from the rabbi of our community, R. Gedaliah, of blessed memory.

He said that he heard from the aforementioned rabbi [of Polonnoye] that the preacher [of Polonnoye] was first among his deeds [the Baal Shem Tov's]: that [the Baal Shem Tov] came to him in the holy community of Sharigrad for the Sabbath and asked to lodge with him, and [the preacher] did not turn him away empty; and he asked him to make a prayer quorum for him in his house to pray, and he gave him permission, but he himself went to the synagogue. On the Sabbath, when the preacher went to preach, [the Baal Shem Tov] asked him to attend the sermon, and in the sermon [the Baal Shem Tov] clothed him [drew him in] from the sole of his foot to his head; and [the preacher] said in his heart, "This is none other than a prophet, for from where does he know my thoughts?" and he cleaved to him. Immediately he invited the people of the community for the third meal, and it was astonishing to them, for they knew that he did not hold with the pious ones. Afterward, when the rabbi held firm in his righteousness, the people of the city began to quarrel with him, and they became great enemies of his. The dispute kept growing stronger until they drove him from the city on the Sabbath eve, and he had to spend the Sabbath in a certain village. The Baal Shem Tov was on the road, not far off, and when the Baal Shem Tov saw the matter, he said to his treasured men, "Let us travel to such-and-such a village, for I know that he is in great grief, and we will spend the Sabbath there with the rabbi of the holy community of Sharigrad, that we may be with him to lighten his grief." And so they did. And the preacher too was there with them. On the Sabbath, after the Additional prayer, the preacher saw that the rabbi [the Baal Shem Tov] was grieved, and he said to him, "Do not grieve; I heard a proclamation that they proclaimed, saying, 'So-and-so your enemy will be killed, and so-and-so your enemy will die on the road, and the whole city will be burned with fire.'" When the Baal Shem Tov heard his words, he cried out at the preacher and said, "Fool, do you too hear proclamations?" and the preacher fell silent. The rabbi [of Polonnoye] traveled to the holy community of Rashkov and was accepted there as rabbi and head of court, and there he repented greatly; for he had wealth of some sixty thousand, and he distributed all his money, for he returned all the fines he had received, and everything in his business that had a tinge of prohibition he returned, and he proclaimed under ban that whoever had received from him [anything] not properly [acquired] should come and return his money — until he was left poor.

---

## §22

I heard from the rabbi R. Yoel, preacher of the holy community of Nemirov, whose burial place is in the Land of Israel, in Jerusalem (may it be built and established): Once he traveled with the rabbi of the holy community of Rashkov to the holy community of Mezhibuzh and came to the holy community of Bar. They prayed there at the lodging, in a house, with ten, and at the end of the prayer the rabbi slipped away from among them. They went to seek him and found that he had come out of a certain house, and they asked him, "What did the rabbi do in your house?" but he did not wish to reveal it, as [the rabbi] had decreed upon him not to reveal it. The great men who were there with the rabbi were angry at him, and they decreed upon the aforementioned goldsmith that he tell them the truth. The goldsmith said, "I see in him vengeance, for when he dwelt in the holy community of Rashkov he took from me a fine of two gold coins, and now he has given me his Sabbath prayer shawl until he returns from the road and redeems it from me." The men decreed upon him at once, "Forgive him the two gold coins and return him the prayer shawl." He returned it to him, but [the rabbi] did not want to accept it, until the goldsmith gave his pledge that he forgave him with a whole heart; then he accepted the prayer shawl.

As for the fasting of the aforementioned rabbi, this is how it was: he would delight [in fasting] in the manner of the *Tashuvot HaKaneh*, in this fashion. He fasted day by day, and every single month he fasted once from Sabbath to Sabbath; and none of the household knew of this fast, except one maiden, the daughter of the rabbi's sister, with whom he made an arrangement that she would bring him food, and no one else, and would also clear away the food so that the household would not know. Thus he fasted five years, and in the sixth year it happened that he was fasting from Sabbath to Sabbath, and the Baal Shem Tov was on the road and heard a proclamation: "Hurry, run to him in haste, for today his mind [higher faculties] will be nullified." He ran to him in great haste, until a horse worth ten gold coins died on him on the road. He said to the rabbi, "A horse worth ten gold coins died on me on the road on your account, and may it be an atonement in your stead." And at once [the rabbi] ate, and afterward heeded him.

There is in my possession a letter that the Baal Shem Tov sent to the rabbi, in these words — a copy of what was written in the exchange of letters from the holy community of Mezhibuzh to the holy community of Greater Nemirov, these words in writing "running and returning, like the appearance of lightning" — to the priestly luminary, head of court of the aforementioned holy community, fortress and tower, the wondrous rabbi, worker of wonders, the all-encompassing sage R. Yosef the priest, head of court of the aforementioned holy community. This is the language of the letter: "To the hand of my beloved, my friend, the great luminary rabbi, the humble and modest one, famed in piety, the perfect sage, the wondrous one, worker of wonders, joined to the walls of my heart, cleaving to me, R. Yosef the priest. Behold, I received this [letter] and saw one matter in the two upper lines, where it says that your honor speaks as though he is compelled to fast — and my insides shudder at the sound of the speaker. And I add, by the decrees of the watchers and by the union of the Holy One, blessed be He, and His Divine Presence, that you should not bring yourself into such danger; for this is a deed of black bile and melancholy, and the Divine Presence rests not out of melancholy, but only out of the joy of a commandment, as is known to your honor — matters I taught several times, and these words were on his heart. And concerning the branches of his thoughts that bring him to this, I will counsel you, and may God be with you, mighty man of valor: every single morning, at the time of his study, let him cleave himself to the letters in complete cleaving to the service of his Creator, blessed and praised be He; and then the judgments will be sweetened at their root, and the judgments will be eased from upon him. And from your own flesh do not hide, Heaven forbid, to fast more than the obligation and the need. And if you will indeed heed my voice, God will be with you. And with this I will be brief and say peace, from me, who seeks his welfare. Spoken by Israel Baal Shem Tov."

---

## §23

And a tribute is offered to his only son, the wondrous rabbinic scholar, the dear and honored R. Shimshon, and with him his son, the dear R. Hertz (may he live), and to all of them in general and in particular. I heard from the rabbi of our community, R. Gedaliah, of blessed memory, that several times the rabbi [R. Yoel of Nemirov] longed to travel to the Holy Land, and the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov said that he should not travel. He said to him, "Let this sign be in your hand: every time a longing falls upon you to travel to the Holy Land, know faithfully that there are judgments upon the city, God spare us, and the Satan distracts you so that you will not pray for the city. Therefore, when a longing for the Holy Land falls upon you, pray for the city."

Once the rabbi traveled to the Baal Shem Tov for the Sabbath, and had to travel by way of the city of Sharigrad, and he had great anger at the city and did not want to stop in the city; but because he had to give fodder to the horses, he was compelled to stop, and he did not rise from the wagon, saying, "When they eat, we will travel on at once." When they entered the house, it began to rain heavily, so that they could not travel, and the rain did not stop until he had to lodge there against his will. In the morning, too, the rain did not stop, growing greater, until Thursday; and when he saw that the rains did not stop, he said, "Master of the world, perhaps it is pleasing to You that I spend the Sabbath here and the rains will stop." And so it was — the rains stopped at once. And he prayed the whole Sabbath for the city. The Baal Shem Tov saw that he had spent the Sabbath in the holy community of Sharigrad, and he knew that [the rabbi] had been greatly angered at the city, and he laughed and said to his treasured men, "Behold, the rabbi of the holy community of Rashkov has spent the Sabbath in the holy community of Sharigrad," and he laughed again. After the Sabbath, when [the rabbi] came to the Baal Shem Tov, he greeted him and laughed; and the rabbi said to the Baal Shem Tov, "My master, what is the laughter?" He said to him, "That you spent the whole Sabbath in Sharigrad." He answered, "So it is," and he longed before him like an only son, and recounted to him that he had been compelled to spend the Sabbath there because of the rains. Then the Baal Shem Tov answered him, "Do you suppose that the preacher [of Polonnoye, in §21] was lying, Heaven forbid? For it is true that he heard the proclamation; and what I was angry at him for was that he brought it forth from his mouth — for had he not brought forth from his mouth the words of the proclamation, I would have annulled the decree from the city that very Sabbath entirely; but now I accomplished only that they were granted leeway, that it would not burn all at once, but at one time through this entrance and at another time through that entrance, until the whole city burns." And so it was, that it burned several times. And those upon whom murder was decreed were killed in the holy community of Uman at the time of the flight, God spare us. And those upon whom death by plague was decreed, God spare us, died in the plague that was two years after the flight; and they had a great extension of time, about twenty years or more — for I know that he was head of court of the holy community of Nemirov sixteen years before the flight, besides the time he was in the city of Rashkov, which was several years before this incident.

---

## §24

I will write a little of the praises of the rabbi when he was head of court in the holy community of Nemirov, one year after the flight. My way was, when I put on the tefillin of Rabbenu Tam, to be afraid to put them on before people, for I was still tender in years; and I was accustomed, at the time of "Ashrei" and "And a redeemer shall come to Zion," to go to the large room, for there was no one there during the prayer — for they prayed in a room adjoining the small house where they prayed — only his beadle was in the house, and sometimes the beadle went to the market to buy household needs. I entered the house and put on there the tefillin of Rabbenu Tam, and tarried; and meanwhile they finished the prayer, and the rabbi, of blessed memory, entered his house and turned this way and that and saw that there was no one. I asked him, "Our rabbi, what are you looking for?" and he said to me, "You are walking about in tefillin." At once I removed the head-tefillin from upon me, and he said, "Go to the study hall and see whether they have finished the prayer." I went, and they were still praying "Ashrei" and "And a redeemer shall come to Zion," and I came to his house and did not find him, for he had removed his tefillin and gone to relieve himself; and I waited for him. When he came to the house and asked me, I said that they had finished the prayer, for I had supposed that surely they had finished. He began to put on tefillin, and I knew that he would go to the study hall, so I went ahead and found that someone was standing before the ark and reciting Psalms as though asleep. I knew that the rabbi would be angered at this, for he used to say that the covenant of the tongue corresponds to the covenant of the [circumcised] flesh: just as with the covenant of the flesh it is impossible to give birth with a dead organ, so with the covenant of the tongue it is impossible to give birth with a dead organ. The rabbi had commanded that Psalms be recited in all the synagogues — [the verses spelling out] the letters of "Nemirov" and the letters of "Tear apart Satan" interwoven with the Name of Havayah, from the verses of "Happy are those whose way is blameless" [Psalm 119]; and first they should recite chapter 83, and recite each day a "May it be Your will" after the prayer. When he came to the study hall and heard the recitation of Psalms, he was angered and said, "I have no more strength to bear you; you reckon thoughts each one only for himself. I say, do not take lightly in your eyes the matter of the Tatar." From there he went to the synagogue, and there he rested from his anger and hinted at the matter by way of plain interpretation; and had I not heard his words beforehand in the study hall, I would not have understood his hint. By day he gathered the schoolchildren to the synagogue and commanded that they recite "May the pleasantness" ninety-one times; and before the afternoon prayer they heard that the previous night the Tatar had marched a great camp half a *parsah* near Tultshin, and they were near the city of Nemirov. And thank God, the Ishmaelites sent a certain minister, a pasha, after them, and they all turned back.

---

## §25

I heard from a certain man named R. David, who dwelt at that time in the holy community of Sharigrad, that one day earlier the rabbi R. Mordechai of the aforementioned holy community commanded the community to send a special messenger to the rabbi of the holy community of Nemirov, that he pray concerning the Tatar. They sent a messenger and commanded him to go early in the morning. The aforementioned rabbi [of Nemirov] said that he had no need of a messenger, for the rabbi of the holy community of Nemirov already knew. May the Lord save His people Israel from every adversary and distress, Amen.

I will write a little of his conduct: for he would study every day in prayer shawl and tefillin. Before eating he studied a measure of Gemara, seven pages; and even during eating, between one dish and another, his mouth did not cease from recitation. After eating he would sleep a very little, and rise from his sleep and put on tefillin and study until the afternoon prayer. At night too he studied much; not a single night passed over him in which he did not rise at literal midnight, in summer and in winter, whether on Sabbath or festival; and he gave much charity to the poor. In his house his livelihood was in straits and pressure. Once his son complained to him about the straits and pressure, and he answered him, "It is written in *Midrash Shmuel*, 'And they shall be poor, householders.'" And his study and his prayer and everything of holiness he would do with such force that his flesh shuddered upon him.

---

## §26

I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory, that the pious rabbi R. Gershon immersed himself in the sea while the ship was sailing. I asked him how it was permitted to place oneself in such great danger, and he had nothing to answer me. When R. Gedaliah came to us, I remembered and asked him about this, and he answered me, "Once I sat with the pious rabbi R. Tzvi of Kamenets on the bench in front of the house and asked him about this matter, and he answered me, 'On this very bench I sat with the aforementioned R. Gershon, and I asked him in these words: I have heard that you immersed in the sea while the ship was sailing — how was it permitted to place yourself in such danger?' And he answered with a laugh and said, 'What of it? For all the ship's craftsmen bathe in the sea, for there is a certain leather sack with a leather ladder hanging from the ship, and they go up and down on it and bathe within it.

'"But I will tell you: I did not bathe in that sack; rather, this is how it was. I saw judgments upon the ship and wished to immerse to annul the judgments, and it happened that they came to a certain harbor and stopped the ship, and all the people of the ship went out to stroll. I said, now is the fitting time to immerse, and I removed my garments at the bottom of the ship — what is called the *hold* — and leaped into the water and sat in the water. Meanwhile the ship moved off, and when I came up from the water and saw that the ship had gone, I said in my heart, if I go onto the harbor, what shall I do, for I am naked and cannot bring forth a word of holiness from my mouth, and moreover I have nothing there to eat and drink. I said, I will pursue the ship and overtake it, for I was confident in myself that I could swim well — for once I saw in the Dniester river a certain Jew drowning, and he was about half a *parsah* from me, and I saved that Jew. So I did, and swam after the ship and reached it; but because it was high and smooth with pitch, I had no place to take hold of the ship, and I cried out, but my voice was not heard because of the noise of the ship's machinery, and again the ship moved off from me. I swam with all my strength, but my hands gave out; I did this several times, until I was greatly terrified and began to sink in the water, going down and coming up, and I could not speak any word, even the confession. I thought in my heart, even to drown and also to lose the World to Come, for it is almost like one who takes his own life knowingly. At once one of the ship came out and saw me and pulled me out and placed me in his small ship and brought me to the large ship and threw me into the large ship; and I went down to the rear of the ship and lay about two hours, and I was vomiting much from the abundance of water I had drunk. I lay until I rested a little, and put on my garments, and said, I will pay the man for the good he did with me, and let him not inherit through it the World to Come. I took a purse of money and searched for him throughout the whole ship, but there was no Ishmaelite there. And I said, it appears from this that he was that [Elijah]."'"

---

## §27

I heard from the rabbi of our community a tale: the Baal Shem Tov saw upon R. Gershon, his brother-in-law, a mark of adultery, and he had great grief from this, and he was ashamed to tell him, and wondered at the matter; nevertheless he told him. [R. Gershon] answered him, "You are a liar," and struck the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Wait for me until tomorrow, for nevertheless I see upon you a mark of adultery." And at night, in the unifications that he made, he looked, and the next day he said to him, "You took upon yourself abstinence from your wife, and I heard in the firmament that Maimonides ruled that this is as though he said of his wife, 'She is to me as a mother.' Although it is not a complete vow, nevertheless the mark of [a seminal] emission was aroused upon you, for which you fasted from Sabbath to Sabbath; and because you transgressed the bounds of abstinence, the mark grew strong, and I see upon you as adultery." And he admitted to him the truth. This — that he transgressed the abstinence — was because he wished to travel to the Holy Land and could not appease his wife in this matter, for he kept distant from her; therefore he transgressed the abstinence, and the two of them busied themselves with rectification until he rectified this matter. And from this union was born the preacher of the holy community of Ladyzhin, of whom the preacher of the holy community of Polonnoye said that he never in his life had a [seminal] emission.

---

## §28

I heard from the mouth of the aforementioned preacher: Once R. Shalom of the land of the West [the Maghreb] decreed a fast for rain in Jerusalem. He was a great and esteemed righteous man, and he commanded that they all gather in one synagogue to pray in assembly, and he sent to the aforementioned R. Gershon that he should pray before the ark. In Jerusalem the custom is that whoever prays before the ark must say compelling words; and there the custom of the preachers is to recite the Gemara or the Midrash or the verses in their [exact] form and as they are, and if one alters the language it is a disgrace to him. [R. Gershon] was preparing himself to preach and repeated the wording several times in order to say it as it is, and he did not sleep the whole night; and at the morning watch his throat became hoarse. He said to his son R. Leib, "My speech has become spoiled; go to R. Shalom and tell him that I cannot pray." And the bundle was undone, and each prayed in his place where he was accustomed, and R. Shalom in his study hall in his place, and another went down before the ark. In the midst of the song, R. Shalom heard that R. Gershon's voice had returned, and immediately after the silent prayer he commanded that the prayer leader go from the lectern, and R. Gershon went down to the lectern to pray the Amidah aloud and recite the Selichot. R. Gershon went down before the ark, and after the Amidah he began to recite Selichot and said the opening, and then went from the ark and did not wish to recite more Selichot. His son R. Leib asked him why he did this, and he answered him, "Because in the opening my prayer was fluent in my mouth, and I sensed that if I recited more Selichot, rain would fall at once; and I feared arrogance of spirit, so I did not pray, for I know that rain will fall two or three consecutive days after this." And so it was.

---

## §29

I also heard from R. Pinchas the teacher, who was formerly a preacher — and it seems to me that the tale was in the city of Safed, and there was there the rabbi of that holy community, called in their tongue *chacham* (sage). He was a very good man and a friend of R. Gershon. Once R. Gershon said to the aforementioned sage, "Money has run out, and I had to borrow for the Sabbath expenses." It was a very cold day, and R. Gershon wore a *barslik* sewn over cotton wool, and he went to the study hall to pray. After the prayer they tarried in the study hall, each studying his measure, and R. Gershon was walking to and fro within the study hall and snuffing tobacco from the snuffbox, and there fell from the barslik a small purse called a *schlossbeutel*, and in the purse were five *zekser* — and in the Holy Land they did not know this [type of] purse. The sage saw that the aforementioned purse had fallen from R. Gershon, and he picked it up and felt it from the outside, for he could not open the purse, and he sensed that there were five coins in it and supposed that these were five gold coins. The sage wondered at R. Gershon, that he had brought forth a false word from his mouth — and there they are very particular about a false word. He began to speak with R. Gershon, "Your honor borrowed money for the Sabbath, for the expenses, and your honor had no money?" and he answered, "So it is." The sage said to him, "Your honor speaks falsely," and he said, "I spoke the truth," and they repeated the dispute, this one saying thus and that one saying thus. Meanwhile the sage swore by the Holy Land that he was speaking falsely, and R. Gershon, in turn, placed him under ban. The sage removed his shoes and went home and gathered the whole community and recounted to them the affair, and showed them the purse, and they too felt the purse and said that there were five gold coins in it; and [the sage] complained, "Not only is he a liar, but he also placed me under ban." The community went to R. Gershon and asked him, "Why did you place our sage under ban?" and he said, "Because he swore a false oath." And they showed him his purse and said, "Behold, there are five gold coins here." R. Gershon said, "Why did he not open the purse before he swore?" They answered, "He could not open it." He said to them, "He should have torn open the purse and looked; and this coin does not pass here, and it is left as silver, for I have a myrtle of silver and also other vessels of silver." And the sage appeased R. Gershon, and he released him from the ban. A letter came from the Baal Shem Tov to R. Gershon, and he wrote there: "I saw that they judged you in a certain palace and wished to sentence you to death, Heaven forbid — why did you act brazenly against the rabbi, head of court? And I wished to go to the palace to plead for you, but they shut the palace before me, and I could not enter there; and I said, 'Master of the world, he did it for Your jealousy.' And a voice went forth: 'Leave him be, for he was jealous with the jealousy of the Lord of Hosts.' Yet even so they sentenced him to be blind one month — and a blind man is considered as dead — and therefore from now on it will not be so hard." R. Gershon wrote to the Baal Shem Tov, "I do not know how to judge you, for the matter is true; but it is astonishing to me that you saw that they were judging me before the incident" — for it was marked in the Baal Shem Tov's letter on what day it was written, and the like. Similarly I heard from the rabbi of our community that R. Lipe, of blessed memory, of the holy community of Khmelnik, had a quarrel with the rabbi of that holy community concerning a certain question — this one forbidding and that one permitting. When R. Lipman was at the Baal Shem Tov for Rosh Hashanah, the Baal Shem Tov said during the meal, "I saw today that they were judging a certain scholar to death for acting brazenly against the rabbi, head of court of his community." And R. Lipman understood that he was speaking about him, and began to weep; and the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Do not fear, for I prayed for you, and they released you from the judgment."

---

## §30

I also heard from R. Pinchas: When R. Gershon was in the Holy Land — and there the custom from ancient days is to strain the water, both for cooking and for drinking, because their waters are stored, [being] cistern water, and worms and gnats are common in their waters — and R. Gershon's wife did not know of this and did not strain the water, for in her country it was not yet the custom to strain the water; and the women composed a mocking song and scoffed at her in the synagogue. She came home in great weeping: "Why did you bring me to a distant land, where they mock me?" R. Gershon appeased her and said, "Show me the woman who composed this song," and she showed him the woman who was walking from the synagogue. He spat and said in a tone of astonishment, "What is this — this woman can speak?" and that woman became mute.

---

## §31

I heard from my son R. Leib (may he live): Once R. Hirsch, son-in-law of the head of court of the holy community of Ostrah, the great luminary R. David, spent the Sabbath with us, and R. Aizik was the brother-in-law of R. Hertz, son of the aforementioned rabbi. On the night after the Sabbath, my aforementioned son recounted a tale that he heard from him: the wealthy R. Aizik, son of the rabbi, had a small daughter, thirteen years old, and she was married, and she was ill. When the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Zaslav, they showed him the sick girl, and he commanded that the next day they call for her the wife of a doctor [a healer] to apply leeches to her, and he said to open for her [the veins called] *meridin*. The wealthy R. Aizik was afraid to rely on the Baal Shem Tov, and he asked the doctor of the duchess of that holy community, who said that even in a young girl *meridin* is common. He came home and sent for the doctor's wife. The Baal Shem Tov's lodging was at R. David's, who had formerly been head of court in the holy community of Ostrah. R. Hirsch, son-in-law of the rabbi, went with the Baal Shem Tov to the house of R. Aizik, through the vestibule of the house, and several horses were standing there, and [the Baal Shem Tov] went from horse to horse and looked at them and patted them, and one horse pleased him. He said to R. Hertz, "If it is possible for him to sell me this horse," and R. Hertz answered that he did not know, and they entered the house, and he said to R. Aizik, "Sell me this horse." [R. Aizik] answered him, "Our rabbi, it is written, 'You shall not covet.'" He said to him, "Fool, even so I covet it," but [R. Aizik] did not wish to sell. Afterward he saw the doctor's wife and said, "Is there no other in the city, that you called this leech?" — and this woman was esteemed in the city — and she opened the *meridin* for her, and even so [the girl] did not recover. The Baal Shem Tov traveled home, and R. Aizik traveled with his daughter to the Tatar in the city of Konstantin; and the Baal Shem Tov heard of this and traveled himself to Konstantin and was angry at him, saying, "You too do this to me?" R. Aizik promised him that he would travel home, but his mind was not so; and when R. Aizik saw that the Tatar neither raised nor lowered [had no effect], he traveled home with his daughter.

Once she grew very weak, near the Sabbath, and the rabbi R. David made a commotion and gathered teachers to recite Psalms, and they blessed her with another name, and he sent a letter by the hand of a gentile to Mezhibuzh with a messenger, so that he could go on the Sabbath to the Baal Shem Tov to pray for her. The gentile came on the Sabbath day, after the reading of the Torah, before the Additional prayer; and on the night after the Sabbath [the Baal Shem Tov] commanded that a letter be written to be given into the hand of the gentile, and in it was written: "This Sabbath, before the Additional prayer, I received [your letter] and prayed for her a great prayer, but I was not answered — not a word and not half a word — and I do not know how to judge it, unless they blessed her with another name; and inform me at once of the name." In the end it became known that this young girl was pregnant, and when she gave birth she recovered. Afterward, when the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Zaslav, they said, "You recognized the doctor's wife, and from there you knew also the new month — why did you not recognize that she was pregnant?" He answered, "Do you suppose that she was truly pregnant? It was from my prayer and from your father's prayer that she became pregnant." And the letter that the Baal Shem Tov sent was in the handwriting of R. Yaakov of Yampol.

---

## §32

Another tale: the Baal Shem Tov was sitting in the holy community of Zaslav and spent several Sabbaths there. Meanwhile they found a gentile dead in the field and brought him to the city, and they wished to make a blood libel against the Jews, and they went to the duchess and told her that the Jews had killed him. The duchess feared for herself, for her husband the duke had died a foul death on the highway because he had killed holy ones [martyrs] on false libels. Therefore she said, "I cannot judge capital cases unless the matter is investigated clearly, that the Jews killed him." When it became known to the people of the city, the children of Israel, trembling seized them, and they sent the aforementioned R. Aizik to the Baal Shem Tov's lodging to inform him. The Baal Shem Tov was standing with one foot on the bench and one foot on the ground, and R. Tzvi the scribe was reciting the Zohar before him, and the Baal Shem Tov answered after him and was inflamed with great inflammation, his face burning. When R. Aizik came to the threshold of the house, [the Baal Shem Tov] signaled to him with his hand that he should go, and he went, and the fear kept growing greater. They sent him a second time, and again he signaled that he should go, until they urged the rabbi R. David to go himself to the Baal Shem Tov — surely [the Baal Shem Tov] would not put him off with "go and return," for he was old and venerable. R. David went to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "I do not hear that they are speaking of this in the courtyard [of the nobility] at all."

And once, after the Sabbath, in the middle of the day after eating, the Baal Shem Tov lay on a certain chest and leaned on his arm, with people standing before him, and he inclined his ear as though he wished to hear, and said that they were speaking in the courtyard about this libel. And he said, "Heat the ritual bath for me quickly," and he went to the ritual bath, and came back and said, "Do not be afraid, for there is no concern at all." And the community sent the intercessor to the courtyard, and he heard that the gentiles had brought a complaint before the duchess concerning the slain gentile, and she had commanded her doctor to examine the slain one and ascertain whether he died his own death or not. The community was afraid to rely on the Baal Shem Tov and gave the doctor thirty gold coins; and thank God, there was no libel from this at all. The head of court of the holy community of Zaslav was the brother-in-law of the aforementioned R. David, and they asked the Baal Shem Tov, "Why did you not tell us before they found the slain one, so that we would not be afraid when we found the slain one in the field?" He answered them, "I prayed in the ritual bath about this matter — why was it hidden from me beforehand? And they answered me that this was a punishment for me, because I was lax in the eulogy of a certain Torah scholar called R. Chaim of Brod." And he said to the rabbi of the holy community of Zaslav, "Do you suppose that I do not know that you hid yourself several times — once in the bathhouse under the benches, and in other places?" And to all of it [the rabbi] admitted to him.

---

## §33

I heard at the coming of the rabbi of the holy community of Nemirov: Once, on Simchat Torah, the treasured men of the Baal Shem Tov were rejoicing and dancing and drinking much wine from the Baal Shem Tov's cellar. The Baal Shem Tov's righteous wife said, "They will not leave wine for the Kiddush and the Havdalah," and she went in to the Baal Shem Tov and said to him, "Tell them to stop drinking and dancing, for there will be no wine left for you for Kiddush and Havdalah." The Baal Shem Tov said to her with a laugh, "You have spoken well; go and tell them to stop and go home." When she opened the door and they were dancing in a circle, and she saw that fire was blazing around them like a wedding canopy, she took the vessels and went herself to the cellar and brought them wine as they desired. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov asked her, "Did you tell them to go?" and she said to him, "You should have told them yourself."

---

## §34

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Drohobycz, who was head of court in the holy community of Pavoloch, the tale of how the great Maggid drew near to the Baal Shem Tov. The Maggid fasted seven or eight times from Sabbath to Sabbath consecutively, and became very ill. Once R. Mendel of the holy community of Bar came to the holy community of Tortshin and lodged with the head of court. The Maggid dwelt in a small house near the head of court, called the *lower room*, and R. Mendel entered the vestibule of his householder and heard the words of the Maggid, who was studying with a certain student, and his words pleased him. He entered to him and saw that he was very ill. He said to him, "Have you not heard that there is a Baal Shem Tov in the world? Let your honor travel to him and he will heal you." He answered, "It is better to take refuge in the Lord than to trust in man." When R. Mendel came to the Baal Shem Tov, he recounted in praise of the Maggid and said, "I was in the holy community of Tortshin and saw a beautiful vessel." The Baal Shem Tov said, "I have seen him for several years, and I have a longing for him to come to me." And there are several versions of how the Maggid came to the Baal Shem Tov, but I heard that his relatives pressed him to travel to the Baal Shem Tov.

When he came to the Baal Shem Tov, he found him sitting on his bed and studying, and he greeted him, and the Maggid asked him to heal him. The Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and said, "My horses do not eat matzah" [a cryptic rebuke]. And sweat broke out upon him [the Maggid] from weakness, and he went outside and sat on the bench in front of the house to rest. He saw a certain man, tender in years, and called him to him and said to him, "Please, go to the Baal Shem Tov and tell him, why does he not fulfill the verse 'And you shall love the stranger'?" That young man was R. Yaakov of Nepoli, and his mercy was stirred for him, and he entered to the Baal Shem Tov, but he was afraid to tell him. He acted with cunning and went to the far end of the house and turned to go out and flee, and as he went he said, "A certain man, [full of] black bile, is sitting in front of the house and asked me to tell your honor, why did you not fulfill 'and you shall love the stranger'?" and he went out of the house. At once the Baal Shem Tov gathered ten men and went out to him and appeased him and wished to heal him with words. I heard from R. Gershon of Pavoloch that the Baal Shem Tov went to him for about two weeks and sat opposite him and recited Psalms. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "I wished to heal you with words, for that is a lasting healing, but now I must busy myself with you with remedies." He gave him a dwelling and gave him twelve gold pieces every week for his expenses, and the aforementioned R. Yaakov and R. Eliyahu went to him at all times to visit him, and sometimes they would debate sharply in Gemara and Tosafot; and [the Maggid] could not go to the Baal Shem Tov because of weakness. After a short time he began to recover, and he would go to the Baal Shem Tov to his table.

Once he fainted, and they were rousing him until midday, and the Baal Shem Tov went three times to the ritual bath and sent to a certain nobleman three *parsah* away and bought from him a precious stone called a *diamond* for the sum of thirty gold pieces, and they crushed it and gave it to him to drink. Afterward the aforementioned R. Yaakov and his fellows came to visit him, and asked him why he had fainted, but he answered them nothing. They asked the members of the household whether he had gone out at night, and they said, "He went out and tarried and came into the house and began to faint." They asked him where he had been. He said to them, "The Baal Shem Tov sent for me by his beadle at midnight, and I came and found him sitting with a small cushion on his head, clothed in wolf hides turned inside out, and he asked me whether I had studied the wisdom of Kabbalah, and I said yes; and a certain book lay before him on the table, and he commanded me to recite before him from this book. In the book it was written in short statements, and each statement was a beginning [opening]. R. Yaakov [the narrator: 'Metatron the Prince of the Countenance recounted to me']. And I recited before him a column or half a column, and the Baal Shem Tov said, 'It is not so; I will recite before you,' and he recited before [me]. Meanwhile he shuddered and rose and said, 'We are occupied with the Work of the Chariot,' and I sat and recited standing, and in the midst of the words he laid me on the bed like a circle, and I no longer saw him; I only heard voices and saw lightnings and awesome torches, and this lasted about two hours, and I was greatly frightened, and from this fright I began to faint."

And I, the writer, say: it seems to me that this was the receiving of the Torah. For so I heard from the mouth of the pious rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, that he received from the Baal Shem Tov the Torah belonging to his soul amid voices and lightnings; and he would further say, in this language, "amid all kinds of [thunder and lightning], as is said in the holy Zohar." But I did not see in the Zohar that it mentions "amid all kinds of [...]" at the receiving of the Torah. Yet I heard from the rabbi this language: just as Israel received the Torah in general, so the Baal Shem Tov received it in particular. And after this, once his treasured men asked him about a certain statement of the Zohar, and he interpreted and told it to them; and they went and asked the aforementioned Maggid, of blessed memory, and the interpretation was exactly as the Baal Shem Tov said; and they told the Baal Shem Tov, [and] he said, "You are like bees," for [the Maggid] knew the Torah himself. And when the Maggid, of blessed memory, traveled from the Baal Shem Tov, the Baal Shem Tov blessed him; and afterward the Baal Shem Tov bowed his head that [the Maggid] should bless him, but he did not want to, and the Baal Shem Tov took his hand and placed it on his head and [had him] bless him.

---

## §35

I heard from R. Yoel, preacher of the holy community of Nemirov — and he was always with the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye in the city of Rashkov and in the city of Sharigrad, and in the holy community of Nemirov I heard from him: Once the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath in Sharigrad, and on the night after the Sabbath, after Havdalah, it was his way to recount what he had seen on the Sabbath. He said that he saw that R. Eliezer of the city of Amsterdam had died, and he said that he died the previous Sabbath, which was Sabbath of Bereshit [the Sabbath of Genesis]. I asked him what was the cause of his death, and he said, "Two men from Poland disgraced him on Simchat Torah." R. Yoel did not wish to specify their names; they said to him in these words, "Here you are a prince, but in the lands of Poland you would not be fit to be a rabbi even among the tailors." And [R. Eliezer] accepted upon himself death, "but I will avenge myself upon them through plague and through the sword, God spare us." I asked him, "What will you do with your wife, the rebbetzin?" and he said, "I will take her from them"; and I forgot to ask him whether she was alive, or whether he thought that she too would die.

In that year the son of the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye came, and his father asked him, "What is the rabbi R. Eliezer doing?" He said, "He has already passed away." He asked him whether he passed away on Sabbath of Bereshit, and he said yes. And he said, "From where do you know this, that no one preceded me [in telling]?" And he said to him, "We knew immediately, on the Sabbath of the portion of Noach, for the Baal Shem Tov told us," and he was greatly astonished. And he asked him about the wife of the aforementioned rabbi, and he said that R. Avraham Swerdlik came from Jerusalem and took her to Jerusalem. And he asked about the [former] adversaries of the aforementioned rabbi, and he said that they had been weakened by the blow of plague, God spare us, and they drove them out to the field, and Ishmaelites came and killed them.

[The Baal Shem Tov] further said, on that same Sabbath, that he saw that a new book would be printed that year, but he did not know the name of that book, for it had not yet been given a name. This book was composed by one of the sect of Shabbetai Tzvi — may his name be blotted out — "the lips of a strange woman drip honey" — may his name be blotted out — for the language is beautiful, and the people will desire it, and it will defile the world, Heaven forbid, because of our many sins. Afterward, when it came out into the world, the aforementioned rabbi [of Polonnoye] bought this book, for he did not know beforehand what its name was; and when the Baal Shem Tov came to him and saw that the book lay on the table, as the Baal Shem Tov opened the door he said, "An unfit book lies on your table." The rabbi asked which it was, and he pointed at it with his finger — and this was the book *Chemdat HaYamim*. And he took a rag in the corner and wrapped it and placed it under the table.

The aforementioned R. Yoel told me: Once the rabbi [of Polonnoye] saw in a dream that one of his prayer quorum had apostatized, and trembling seized him, and he hurried and sent to rouse the whole quorum, and saw that all of them, thank God, were whole. He asked them, "Perhaps one of you committed a transgression in secret, Heaven forbid?" and he recounted to them the dream. A certain righteous man, R. Eliezer of Tomashpol, answered, "I know of nothing, except that that night, before sleep, I looked into the book *Chemdat HaYamim*." The aforementioned R. Yoel also told me that a prayer leader came to the Baal Shem Tov to ask of him a rectification, and R. Yoel said, in these words, that the rectification was to bind soul to soul, spirit to spirit, breath to breath. He began to bind himself with him slowly, for he was afraid, since [the man] was a great wicked one. Once the Baal Shem Tov slept, and the prayer leader — may his name be blotted out — came and seduced him, Heaven forbid, and threw him a great throwing until he fell into the lowest depths. The Baal Shem Tov peered at his resting place and saw that he was with Jesus on one tablet, called a *table*. And he recounted that the Baal Shem Tov said that there had been a holy spark in him, and the *samech-mem* [the adversary] caught it in his net, God spare us. The Baal Shem Tov heard the tale of how his fall came about — through pride and anger. I am reluctant to write it; perhaps I will write it, so that one may know how deep pride is.

---

## §36

This tale too I heard from the aforementioned R. Yoel: R. Yitzchak of Drohobycz was accepted as preacher in the holy community of Greater Horychiv and sat there several weeks. There was an incident: a certain butcher remained in debt to the holder of the meat tax, and the tax-holder was not at home, and the tax-holder's wife took the butcher's pledges, even bolsters and cushions. The butcher's wife came to the aforementioned R. Yitzchak with a great complaint and great weeping, and the aforementioned R. Yitzchak sent for the tax-holder's wife and commanded her to return the pledges, but she did not heed him. R. Yitzchak cursed her, and an infant died with her at once. When her husband came from the road and she recounted to him the affair, saying, "A certain preacher has been appointed [here] and cursed me, and at once the child died" — and the aforementioned R. Yitzchak traveled home to the city of Ostrah, for there he was preacher to R. Yuzpa — the community sent wagons after him that he should come to the holy community of Horychiv with all his household. What did the Baal Shem Tov do? He sent a special messenger with a letter, in which was written: "If he has not yet moved from his place, let him remain in his place; and if he is on the road, let him return to his place, for even if he comes, he will be compelled to leave there." R. Yitzchak wrote an answer to him: "I will come to the aforementioned community at the time when they carry your bier toward me." And so it was. When R. Yitzchak came within the gate of the city, they were carrying the Baal Shem Tov [the dead man, ironically named?] — [rather: the tax-holder] — in a bier at the city gate, and they could not go out with the dead, and they had to carry the wagon to one side and go on with the dead. The [dead] tax-holder was the head of a family, and there was suppressed hatred in their hearts because they feared the preacher; and "this quarrel is like a breach of waters" [which keeps widening], until the dispute grew great, and whoever touched the preacher did not live. The rabbi, head of court, who was there — named R. Itzik, who became head of court in the holy community of Brod, and afterward was taken as head of court of the holy community of Hamburg — was also among the adversaries; but because R. Yechezkel, head of court of the holy community of Tomashpol, was a relative of the aforementioned rabbi [R. Yitzchak], he wrote to him for his honor's sake to make peace with him, for he is "a consuming fire," and he made peace with him.

Once there was a great thunderclap, and it struck the synagogue during the afternoon prayer and killed two men — one at the eastern wall and one in the vestibule. The people of the city came and said to him, "Our rabbi, you have killed the people of the Lord." He answered and said, "This one at the eastern wall was killed because of [the] dispute, and this one in the vestibule was killed because of a false oath that he swore in the holy community of Brod over three gold coins, and he held them in his hand during the prayer and amused himself with them" — and they found in his hand the aforementioned three gold coins.

---

## §37

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Two litigants came to be judged before the aforementioned R. Yitzchak, and he ruled an oath upon one of them, who accepted upon himself to swear; and his opponent at law cried out that he should carry out the ruling, for he was greatly angered, since he knew for certain that his opponent was pleading falsely. R. Yitzchak said, "Why do you urge that the ruling be carried out? This is my way: whoever pleads falsely before me, one of his offspring will die — perhaps he will regret it and confess, and live." And so it was, that one of his offspring died, and he regretted it and confessed the truth.

---

## §38

I heard from the rabbi of our community that R. Yonah of Kaminka, brother of R. Baruch, sent oxen to sell at a time when there was war with Greece [the Ottomans] in the villages, and they heard that the Greeks [Ottomans] had plundered R. Yonah's oxen. R. Baruch also sent oxen on the road and was afraid lest they plunder his oxen too. They sent R. Yosef of Kaminka to the Baal Shem Tov, and found him occupied with the washing of hands for a meal, and he commanded R. Yosef of Kaminka to wash his hands too; and after the "Who brings forth bread," the Baal Shem Tov said, "Why did they not speak with me when they set out on the road, that I might watch over them?" Afterward the Baal Shem Tov opened the Zohar and looked in it a moment and said, "I see R. Baruch's oxen, that they are intact and were not plundered." R. Yosef asked the Baal Shem Tov, "And is this written in the Zohar?" He answered, "This is what our Sages, of blessed memory, said on the verse 'And God saw the light, that it was good': they said, 'that it was good' — to hide it away; for with the light of the six days of Creation one could look from one end of the world to the other. And where did the Holy One, blessed be He, hide it? He hid it in the Torah. And what He said, 'for the righteous,' is interpreted: for the righteous who are destined to come into the world; and whoever merits to find in the Torah the hidden light, looks with it from one end of the world to the other. And do you suppose that I saw only the oxen? For in that very seeing I also saw an incident in the holy community of Amsterdam" — and he recounted the incident before him, but I did not hear it.

A tale: the Baal Shem Tov wished to travel on the road, and because it was the time of the blessing of the new moon, he delayed himself until night. At the time of his going to the blessing of the new moon, one of the companions went with him and wished to look behind him, but the Baal Shem Tov did not let him look, for he said, "Someone has stolen the bridle from the horses." For the horses were harnessed to the wagon in order to travel immediately after the blessing of the new moon, and [the thief] stole it because he had no expenses for the Sabbath. After the blessing of the new moon the beadle began to cry out, "Who stole the bridle?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Do not cry out; go to such-and-such a man and take eighteen *gedolim* [coins] and redeem the bridle, for the thief pawned it there; and do not publicize the matter."

---

## §39

A tale: there was in the land a certain slaughterer who was very frivolous and a drunkard. Once the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye called him, in the morning after the prayer, to a certain room and said to him, "I saw the Baal Shem Tov, and he repeated before me several times concerning the matter of the slaughterer, and I cannot explain how the matter was, although he repeated before me the matter of the gentile butchers. Nevertheless, the main strictness was upon the slaughterer, and he told me to watch to catch him at some matter." And so it was. For the rabbi, of blessed memory, was very careful not to deprive a person of his livelihood, and on account of this the slaughterer had a continued existence. And once I caught him, the rabbi commanded that a court be seated to take testimony from the butcher, and "if it is so according to Torah law, you shall remove him." The butcher testified to the matter, and they removed him.

---

## §40

A tale of when the rabbi R. Avraham, son of the great Maggid, was accepted as preacher in the holy community of Chvastov, and the broker [who arranged it] was the famous pious R. Menachem Nachum of Chernobyl. I heard from R. Yoel of Prohor, who was celebrating the festival of Passover with the aforementioned R. Nachum in the holy community of Pohrebysche: A messenger came to him the day after the Day of Atonement from the aforementioned R. Avraham, [saying] that he had grown weak on the Day of Atonement, and at the time of Ne'ilah ceased to speak; and they asked him whether to send for R. Nachum, and he nodded his head, yes, and they sent for him, and meanwhile he passed away. R. Nachum went to the ritual bath, for he said, "I will pray in my place on his behalf." Before the festival they heard that he had passed away, only they did not tell R. Nachum. On the intermediate days of the festival a certain man revealed it to R. Nachum in his innocence, and R. Nachum began to beat his head against the wall and wept a great weeping for about two hours, and they could not silence him, until they took R. Nachum on their arms and carried him to the sukkah and said, "Rabbi, remember that today is a festival," and he rested from his weeping. He began to recount his praises and said: "Once I was in the holy community of Anipoli and asked him [R. Avraham] whether he sees his father, and he said yes. I asked whether in a dream or while awake, and he answered, 'He comes to me in a dream and speaks with me, and afterward I wake from my sleep and he speaks with me while awake.'" And he recounted to R. Nachum that there was a certain wealthy man there who grieved him several times — and R. Yoel did not wish to publicize the name of that wealthy man — "and I complained before my father, and my father said to me, 'I will send to call him.' I asked, 'To where will you call him?' and he said, 'To your study hall.' And he sent after him my servant called R. Pilt." Now the aforementioned R. Pilt was at that time in the lands of Polesia, about sixty *parsah* from Anipoli. He came to the house of the wealthy man and knocked at the door and said, "Open for me." The wealthy man recognized him by his voice and wondered at this, saying in his heart, "Surely he is not in his [own] house [town]." And at once he resolved it for himself: "Perhaps he came to his house toward evening," and he greeted him with great joy, and they asked each other after their welfare. Afterward R. Pilt said, "There is no time to delay, for the rabbi calls you." He said, "What are you saying? Surely the rabbi has already died and passed away to his world." R. Pilt said to him, "Even so he calls you," and he went with him. When the wealthy man came to the study hall of R. Avraham, he saw the Maggid sitting at the head of the table, clothed in garments as he was in life. The Maggid said to him, "You suppose that I am not here, and you do whatever your heart desires; therefore I show you that I am sitting here." The wealthy man began to make excuses for himself and to offer pretexts before him, but he did not wish to hear, and said to him, "Go from me, go from me," and he rebuked Pilt, "Lead him to his house," and he led him to his house. When he came to his house, R. Pilt said to him, "Go to your house in peace, for I must return." When [the wealthy man] came to his house he fainted, and there was a great cry until his neighbors gathered and roused him. They asked him why he had fainted, and he recounted to them the affair. They said to him, "Let us go to R. Avraham," and they all went to R. Avraham; and when he saw them he laughed and said, "Presumably my father called him." They said to him, "From where do you know?" He answered, "For my father told me that he would call him."

---

## §41

The aforementioned R. Nachum further recounted that the aforementioned R. Avraham, in the city of Chvastov, was shut up and closed off, and they did not merit to see his face. R. Nachum praised him before the people of the city, and they longed to see him. Once there was a circumcision, and they gave R. Nachum all the honors of the circumcision, and to R. Avraham they gave the duty of *sandak* [holding the child]. After the prayer they waited for him until the day was far advanced, for he prolonged his prayer; and a great multitude gathered in the synagogue in order to see him. When they said, "R. Avraham is going to the synagogue," dread fell upon them, and there remained in the synagogue only about twenty men. When he came to the synagogue — for his walking was in haste — the rabbi of the community understood that he was going to his place, and he slipped away to the corner, and [R. Avraham] came and sat in the rabbi's place and laid his head on his arm on the lectern; and they brought the infant onto the platform while he leaned on his arm. They said to the beadle, "Go and inform him that they have brought the infant," but he did not wish to go because of the fear. They told others to go and inform him, but no one wished to go. Meanwhile he came himself onto the platform. R. Nachum said, "I was so frightened at his coming that the knife fell from my hand, and I caught the knife by the tip of the blade; and to this day I do not remember whether I recited the blessings of the circumcision, because of fear. And I gave him [the honor] to recite the blessings of the *metzitzah* [suction], and he recited them." When R. Nachum came home he sat silent and bewildered. His beadle brought him coffee to drink twice, but he did not want it, and pressed it upon him, and the beadle begged him to drink it; and he answered him, "Fool, how is it possible to drink coffee when one has heard that a man performs such service to the Blessed Name?"

---

## §42

(And from our master [may his rest be in Eden] we heard that R. M. of Chernobyl was the *sandak* and the rabbi R. Avraham, of blessed memory, was the circumciser; and when he recited the blessing of the circumcision in great fear, the knife fell from his hand because of fear and trembling before His greatness. Afterward the rabbi R. M. asked him about the great fear, what its nature was, and he answered, "I am astonished how it is possible to ask such a question.")

---

## §43
