I heard from the rabbi R. Menachem, who heard from the mouth of the daughter-in-law of the great Maggid, the wife of the aforementioned rabbi R. Avraham: When his holy son R. Avraham, of blessed memory, was widowed, [the Maggid] called two notables of the city and said, "My request is that you travel to the holy community of Kremenets and arrange a match for my son (may he live) with the daughter of the rabbi R. Faivel, author of the book *Mishnat Chachamim*, and bring her here." At once they did as he commanded, and prepared for themselves a good wagon with horses, and also garments of honor that would be a glory for the rabbi, and set their faces to the road, until they came to that holy community and stopped at a lodging. When they had rested from their journey, immediately after the prayer they went to the house of the aforementioned rabbi R. Faivel. Now the rabbi's way was not to engage in any occupation of livelihood in the world, but only to occupy himself with Torah all the days, including the nights; only his wife was the mistress of the house, occupying herself with the livelihood. When they came to his house, behold, his wife was coming from the synagogue. She asked after their welfare and said, "My brothers, whence have you come?" and they said, "From the holy community of Mezeritch." She said, "What is your business here — some commerce or another matter?" And they recounted to her the affair, how our great rabbi had sent them to speak about her daughter, that she should marry his son who had recently been widowed. It was a matter of laughter in her eyes, for she did not know the rabbi and had not heard his fame; and moreover, the maiden was a girl of twelve years, and it had never entered their minds to seek and inquire about matches to betroth their daughter. After much presentation of the matter, her words entered her ears [she came around], and she said, "Behold, with God's help, my husband is in my house; what do I care — let him do as is good in his eyes." They sent for him to the study hall, and he came home, and they presented their words before the aforementioned rabbi R. Faivel, until the matter was upright in his eyes; and at once they commanded that the scribe come to write the conditions, for good fortune.

When they began to write the conditions, the emissaries said, "As is good in your eyes, write concerning the king [the financial terms]; only, regarding the time of the wedding, they said it is impossible in any way to extend the time." And [the parents] argued, "Surely our Sages said that one gives a maiden twelve months [to prepare], and especially since it had not yet entered our minds to marry her off soon, and she has no garments or [trousseau] ready." They answered to this, "We are not particular [about the dowry], but this is impossible in any way — to extend the time of the wedding; rather, she will travel with us at once." And since the matter came from the Lord, they agreed to this too. And the agreement remained that the rabbi R. Faivel, so as not to neglect his study, would remain in his house, and the bride with her mother would travel to the wedding. They hired a good wagon with horses for her too, and they traveled with them. Now the whole way she marveled at herself — what was this that had happened to them, that they had been persuaded to this. Afterward they came to the holy community of Rovno, and all the people of the city came, from small to great, men, women, and children, to greet them and to give welcome to the bride of the great rabbi; and her mind was settled a little. Afterward, when they came to the holy community of Mezeritch, men and children came out toward them from outside the city, until they had to go slowly because of the great multitude that came out toward them; and when they came into the city, all the women came out toward them to greet them, until they came opposite the rabbi's house. He and his son came out to greet them and welcomed them, and afterward they traveled to their lodging. The wedding was seven days of feasting in great joy, and they said words of Torah as befits a king. And after the wedding the [bride's] mother traveled home in great gladness, for never had she seen or heard the like.

The rabbi drew his daughter-in-law very near. One day she dreamed, and behold, she entered a great hall, and there sat a Sanhedrin of elders, venerable men, and they sought to take her husband from her. She cried out before them and pleaded several pleas before the court. She awoke, and behold, it was a dream; but she told no one. Although she was in the rabbi's house, she did not tell him. On the second night she dreamed likewise and told nothing. On the third night she dreamed likewise, and she pleaded much before them, until at last they said to her, "See, because your pleas are very correct, therefore we give him to you as a gift for twelve years." The next day, when she came to the rabbi in the morning, the rabbi gave her great thanks and blessed her for her correct pleas, which had lengthened his [son's] time twelve years. And so it came to pass for him. After several years the rabbi ascended to heaven and departed life for all the living. Even so, in all their distress, or any matter that was needed, the rabbi would be revealed to her in a dream and would command her what to do.

Once, on the Sabbath eve [Friday] night, he came to her in a dream and said, "Tell your husband either to exchange his dwelling for yours, or at least to place his books in your room." In the morning she told her husband, but he paid no heed to this, and said, "Why did he not tell me, and tells you?" And, because of our many sins, his room with the books was burned at night. Afterward the rabbi R. Avraham traveled about the province, and when he came to the holy community of Chvastov they received him with great honor and accepted him as rabbi and gave him a fixed salary, and they sent a messenger after his wife that she should come there too for the Days of Awe. When she was coming, the Maggid came to her in a dream and said to her, "Do not travel this term to the holy community of Chvastov." In the morning she refused to travel, and there was a great commotion among the righteous ones, R. Meshulam Zusia and R. Yehuda Leib the priest, for it was their wish that she travel there to fulfill the will of the righteous one [her husband]; but she did not heed them. And the days were not long before, because of our many sins, the ark of God [her husband] was hidden away there in that holy community, and they sent a messenger to the holy community of Anipoli. And the righteous ones were greatly ashamed before her, that the woman saw better than they; but they hid the matter from her, only revealing it to her son, the rabbi R. Shalom, who was young, so that he might say Kaddish, in a way that would not be revealed to his mother. But she, in her great wisdom, marveled at her son's diligence in rising early in the morning to go to the synagogue, which was not his habit; and when she went from the synagogue, she leaned herself against the wall of the study hall to hear whether her son was saying the orphan's Kaddish. But she could not ascertain the matter, until after the prayer she coaxed her son with words and said, "Why do you hide your words? For I heard from behind the wall how you said the orphan's Kaddish." Until he was compelled to reveal the truth, and she mourned for her husband. And after the seven days of mourning passed, she traveled to the holy community of Chvastov to receive her husband's estate. When she came there, they received her with great honor, and in her honor they made on that Sabbath a feast at her lodging, and all the followers ate there in her honor in order to comfort her a little; but she would not accept comfort.

At the third meal almost all the people of the city gathered there and sang hymns as is customary, while she, in her bitterness of soul, sat sorrowful on her honored bed with the wife of the householder. She dozed and dreamed, and behold, a great hall, beautiful and splendid; and she opened a certain door, and her husband the rabbi R. Avraham came in, into a house bright as the sun at noon, very joyful; and after him entered several elders, venerable men. He said to them, "My masters, sit," and they sat around the table, and he said to them, "Behold, my wife (may she live) has complaints against me about several matters in which I conducted myself with excessive abstinence, and the right is with her, and I ask her forgiveness before you." She said, "I forgive you with a whole heart." He further said before them, "Behold, the Torah permitted her to marry another husband, especially since she is a young woman of twenty-four years, and I cannot prevent her; but if she agrees not to marry another, then all her needs are upon me, and her children — surely, before she comes home, each one of them will have a good standing." And she awoke; and at once her angry face was no longer upon her, for she was comforted by his words.

When she came home, at once matches presented themselves for her sons and her daughters, and the Lord sent blessing in the work of her hands, and she was supported with honor from the shop she had, in such a way that she lacked nothing of all her needs. And in time of need her father-in-law the rabbi would appear to her to tell her what to do. Now after a long time the wife of the great rabbi R. Menachem of Chernobyl died, and it pleased him to take the aforementioned woman as a wife. He took counsel with her son the rabbi R. Shalom (may he live), who was the husband of his [R. Menachem's] daughter, and it was right in his eyes too, for he was still tender in years. And he sent the aforementioned rabbi to speak with his mother. On the way the rabbi R. Shalom dreamed, and behold, a great hall, beautiful and splendid, and his father the rabbi, of blessed and holy memory, standing at the door of the hall with both his hands on the roof, and he cried out in a great voice and said, "Who is this, and who is the one whose heart filled him to enter my hall?" He awoke and understood how far the matter reached, and he returned home in peace.

---

## §44

The great rabbi R. Dov, of blessed memory, of Mezeritch once commanded his disciple the rabbi R. Aharon of Karlin to travel home by way of a certain known community. He did so according to his command, and when he came to the known city, he searched in his bag, and behold, he had forgotten the bag with the prayer shawl and tefillin. Now the rabbi never prayed with a borrowed prayer shawl and tefillin. He sent to a shop to take a prayer shawl and make fringes for it, and he himself went to the scribe to buy tefillin. When the scribe placed the tefillin in his hand, the rabbi at once cried out, "Yesterday he came upon a married woman, and today he sells tefillin! Wicked one, give acknowledgment!" And he confessed his sin. When he confessed, investigations and examinations were made, and they found several men and women, all of whom had been defiled with sins, so that more than a hundred bills of divorce were needed. And in one matter he had a question and did not know what to do, and was compelled to return to the great rabbi R. Dov.

When he came, he recounted to the rabbi R. Dov the whole affair, and also the matter of the question about which he was in doubt and how it was the law in practice. Before the rabbi R. Aharon traveled to that community again, the rabbi said, "Let the rabbi R. Menachem Mendel travel with you, for he knows how to speak in the Polish tongue; and also take with you the white Sabbath garments." And so they did. Now on their journey the rabbi R. Menachem Mendel said, "Behold, our journey is only to arrange the bills of divorce; if so, why is there need for Polish speech and also the white garments? This is nothing but that our rabbi sees, with the eye of the intellect, that the wicked ones have regretted their confession and have informed against [us] to the minister of the city." And so it was, for when they came to the city the minister set guards to bring them at once to the minister, and his wish was to do them harm, for the wicked ones had hidden themselves with the minister in another room. When they came to the minister, clothed in white garments, they found favor in his eyes, and he received them with great honor and commanded them to sit by him. As they spoke, the minister said to them, "What is the business of these rabbis here?" The rabbi R. Menachem Mendel said, "Since the rabbi R. Aharon is a man of God, and saw that there are great accusations against your city, upon which utter destruction has been decreed, because the people of the city are evil and sinners against the Lord exceedingly, especially in matters of adultery — and several women must be divorced — therefore we have come to you, for you are the minister of the city and must look after their welfare." As they spoke to him, he remembered the words of the wicked ones, and he commanded them to go out and speak with the rabbis. At once the rabbi R. Aharon thundered against them with a voice of commotion and anger, and they were compelled to confess all their sins before the minister, and the minister commanded them to carry out the divorces and gave them great backing.

---

## §45

In a certain holy community the people of the city had great suffering and oppression from their *commissar*, until they literally could not bear it. And there was a counsel among them that they had no other way except to send a messenger to the duke, who was far from them, about eighty *parsah*. They chose two notable men, one old and venerable and the second a man tender in years. The people of the community warned them, for his honor's sake, to hasten their journey, for the time of the contract was near, and if they delayed the time even one day, it would be a distortion that they could not repair. Now their route was by way of the holy community of Mezeritch, and they came to a lodging there on Sunday morning and prayed at the lodging. Meanwhile the householder spoke with them, what was their business and where their faces were set to travel, and they recounted to him the whole affair. The householder said, "My counsel is that you go to our great rabbi to ask his holy mouth what you should do." And although the old man refused a little, nevertheless the second, who was tender in years, his heart was drawn after his counsel, and he persuaded the old man to go to the rabbi; and so they did. When they came to the rabbi, they found there a great multitude wishing to enter inside, and they waited several hours until they found his beadle and told him that they wished to enter to the rabbi. The beadle went to the rabbi and answered them in the rabbi's name that they should lodge there that night, and the next day he would give them an answer. It was very hard for the old man, their delay, for they were in great haste on their way because the time was very short; nevertheless they had to lodge there.

The next day, after the prayer, they came a second time to the rabbi's house, and again they were delayed much until their turn came. The beadle went a second time to the rabbi and answered them in the rabbi's name that they should lodge yet another night there. The matter was very hard for them, and they greatly regretted their going to him; nevertheless they were compelled to delay until Tuesday. When they came on Tuesday, an answer came to them from the rabbi that they should not travel on the known road, but by another road, which was literally crooked. The old man was greatly angered at the second man for their delay until now over nothing. Nevertheless they heeded his words and traveled by the road the rabbi commanded them. Now in their journey, in bitterness of soul over their delay, the heavens clouded over and the rain came down upon them from the heavens — literally a flooding rain — and meanwhile they strayed from the road, and they had very great distress. They traveled thus until they came to a certain village, and they were very chilled from the rain and wind that there was, and they stopped there to lodge, and gave their horses to a gentile to graze them at night, and commanded him to bring the horses early in the morning. The next day they rose early and stood to pray, and finished their prayer, and they still had not brought the horses; and they had very great distress at their delay, for they feared they would pass the fixed term. Meanwhile the gentile came and said that the horses had been seized into the courtyard of the minister, because they were found doing damage; and they asked the householder to travel to the minister to ask for the horses. The householder said that he would surely not go to the minister, for besides that he would surely not return the horses, he would even strike him cruel blows, for such was always his way; and they wished to pay the householder for his trouble that he go there, but he would not in any way, for his body was dearer to him than his money. And they were compelled, in bitterness of soul, to go themselves to the minister.

Now when they reached the courtyard, they saw a certain servant, and it seemed to them that he was the servant of their own duke. And when the servant saw them, he recognized them too, that they were from the known community, for he had been several times with the duke. They asked the servant, "What is your business here?" and he answered, "I am here with the duke." And they were filled with joy and went with him to the duke. When he saw them, the duke rejoiced greatly at meeting them and asked them where they were traveling, and they recounted to him the whole affair, and obtained from him all that they asked, with good counsel; and he gave them letters. They further said before him, "Our horses have been seized by the minister." The duke sent to the minister that he return their horses to them at once, and they traveled in great joy and arrived several days before the appointed time. And they saw with their eyes that it was the word of the Lord from beginning to end.

---

## §46

What they heard from the holy rabbi R. Zusia of Anipoli, who heard from his brother the rabbi R. Elimelech, of blessed and holy memory: Once [the Baal Shem Tov] ascended on high and met the rabbi, author of *Chesed LeAvraham*, who asked him, "Rabbi, behold, I, with God's help, composed ten holy works, and they are very esteemed in the Garden of Eden. Tell me, why do all the worlds quake from your movements below? Tell me what your deeds are, for we cannot fathom their depth — how far the matters reach."

---

## §47

The holy R. Shmelke and his brother the rabbi R. Pinchas (may their rest be in Eden) were invited together to the seat of the rabbinate in the holy community of Frankfurt-am-Main and in the holy community of Nikolsburg. Now since the holy community of Frankfurt-am-Main was a greater and more honored community than the holy community of Nikolsburg, the people of the communities did not decide between them who would sit on the rabbinical seat in Frankfurt and who would be in Nikolsburg, and they said that they [the brothers] themselves would decide between them. Although the rabbi R. Shmelke was older than his brother, nevertheless, in his great humility, he said that his brother R. Pinchas was more fitting for the rabbinate of Frankfurt; while his brother attributed the greatness to his elder brother. And the two of them agreed that when they took leave of our great rabbi, the Maggid of the holy community of Mezeritch, they would ask his holy mouth, and as he decreed, so they would do; and they would further ask of him a certain statement of the Zohar that they could not fathom. When they came to the rabbi's house, the brothers began to dispute with each other as to who would go first, for each said to his brother that he was greater than him. At once the Maggid said, in these words, "The rabbi of Nikolsburg will go first, for he is the elder of the brothers." When they entered inside, the rabbi recounted to them a tale about a certain minister and his conduct with his servants and his palaces; and when he finished the tale, he commanded them to go in peace. They did not ask him the statement of the Zohar, and when they came to their lodging they settled the matter, for surely he had not told it for nothing, and they saw with an open eye that it was a parable and a figure, and from within it they understood the statement of the Zohar fully.

---

## §48

I heard from the rabbi of our community, R. Gedaliah, of blessed memory, that the pious rabbi R. Yechiel, head of court of the holy community of Kowel, sent a certain learned and wise man to take the measure of the Baal Shem Tov. He fell ill on the road and came to the holy community of Mezhibuzh, to a lodging, and lay at the lodging on the stove, and he had a great fever. The Baal Shem Tov sent his brother-in-law R. Gershon to him, and he sat by him on the stove and said before him words of Torah, and afterward said to him, "Come with me to the afternoon prayer." On the way [the man] felt himself well, and he supposed that this was the Baal Shem Tov. He said to him, "Are you the Baal Shem Tov?" He answered and said to him, "No, no; I am the rabbi [R. Gershon], and would that I might have the World to Come that our rabbi earns with the smoke of one pipe." And the rabbi R. Yechiel drew near to the Baal Shem Tov.

I heard from my brother-in-law R. Mordechai, who heard from R. Yechiel of Koliver: Once a certain man came from the land of Lithuania, whom the people of his land had sent to take his measure, and he lodged with the aforementioned R. Yechiel and ate at the Baal Shem Tov's [table]. When he heard, on the Sabbath eve, the prayer of the Baal Shem Tov, it pleased him greatly, and after the prayer he followed the Baal Shem Tov to his house. When the Baal Shem Tov came home, he began to cry out at his servant and was angry at him, that he should go quickly to the horses, for one horse was choking itself; and the servant pleaded with him, saying, "My lord, I will go at once." And he again grew angry at him and wished to strike him. The servant went and saved the horse. When the aforementioned guest saw the anger of the Baal Shem Tov, he was greatly astonished and said in his heart, "After such a prayer, a single horse is reckoned in his eyes [worth] being angry at his servant — and especially on the Sabbath." In the morning, when he heard his prayer, it pleased him; nevertheless this difficulty was fixed in his heart.

After the Sabbath, when he wished to travel home, he revealed the matter before the aforementioned R. Yechiel and went on his way. R. Yechiel recounted the words before the Baal Shem Tov, and he was angry and said, "What business have they to take my measure? I will tell you: a certain Jew was going on the road, and the Sabbath eve grew dark upon him, and he could not reach a settlement before the Sabbath. He took it into his mind and turned from the road into a field, to the side, to spend the Sabbath there. And a certain robber came and began to strike him and wished to kill him; and this Jew was very coarse [unspiritual], and I could not seize hold of him [to save him] except through the matter of the beast. Therefore I cast terror and fear upon my servant, and the more fear I cast upon my servant, the more fear fell upon the robbers, until they left him alive — and you will see that they will quickly bring him to the city." And so it was.

---

## §49

I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory, that the Baal Shem Tov said of himself that he was a reincarnation of Rabbenu Saadia Gaon. I recounted this before the rabbi of our community, and the rabbi said that he knew that the Baal Shem Tov was diligent in studying the books of the aforementioned R. Saadia Gaon. Once people asked him to preach to them after the prayer, and he preached, and meanwhile he trembled and shuddered as during prayer, and said in the midst of the sermon, "Master of the world, it is revealed and known before You that I do not say this for my own honor (but for the honor of [my people], and the house of my mother). Much have I known, and much have I been able to do, and there is no man such that I could reveal it to him."

---

## §50

I heard from the rabbi of our community, and also from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, that in the holy community of Izborz two demons appeared in the women's synagogue, and they frightened the women until they were compelled to abandon the synagogue. The rabbi R. Chaim, preacher of our community, went and sat in the synagogue and studied there and drove out the demons from there, and they went and harmed his two children. He sent for the Baal Shem Tov, who lodged in his house of seclusion with the scribe R. Tzvi. He commanded that they bring the children to him, to his lodging place, and they did so. When they lay down to sleep — and the Baal Shem Tov's bed was at the head of the table, and the scribe lay on the other side, at the Baal Shem Tov's head — before they slept, the two demons came into the house and stood by the door and mocked the Baal Shem Tov for the way he was singing "Lecha Dodi." The Baal Shem Tov rose and sat on his bed and said to the scribe, "Did you see?" And the scribe too saw, and thrust his head under the Baal Shem Tov's pillow and said, "Leave me be." When they finished speaking, they went to the children. The Baal Shem Tov leaped from the bed and rebuked them, saying, "Where are you going?" but they were not afraid and said, "What do you care?" And they began to mock the poor [the children] and to sing "Lecha Dodi" in the Baal Shem Tov's melody. He did what he did, and the two demons fell to the ground and could not rise. They began to plead before him, and he said, "Immediately you will see that the children recover." One of them said, "What is past is past, for we have harmed their inner organs, and now we came to harm them utterly; but their fortune is that your honor is here." He asked them how and what and why they had come to the synagogue. They answered that the cantor had been praying in the synagogue, and he had a chorister called a *bass*, and he was a great adulterer, and at the time of his singing his intention was to be pleasing in the eyes of the women, and he had [lustful] thoughts, and the women too had thoughts about him; and from these two thoughts we were created, two demons, male and female, and our life is in the synagogue. Then the Baal Shem Tov gave them a place by a certain well, a place where people are not found.

The pious R. Yosef of Zornitz, called R. Yosef the teacher, was in our city in the holy community of Linits; but I was not in good health in that city and could not sit with him — I only greeted him. I did not hear from him myself, but my fellows heard from him and recounted before me. In the hand of R. Yosef was always deposited [communal] money of a third party, and the money of the aforementioned rabbi [the Maggid] was also with the aforementioned R. Yosef. At that time there was a disqualification of coins, and there were quarrels because of this. R. Yosef said, "Let us go to the rabbi the Maggid, and he will make a compromise between us." They went to the rabbi the Maggid, and he made a compromise between them, and the rebbetzin went home, while R. Yosef still remained with the Maggid. The Maggid said to R. Yosef, "That rebbetzin is past despair [her case is hopeless]." The next day she felt in herself the pangs of childbirth and understood that she was having a difficult labor, and she sent for R. Yosef and said to him, "Please, let your honor forgive me; therefore go to the Maggid that he make for me a *pidyon nefesh* [redemption of the soul, an intercession]" — for she had heard from the Maggid as above. He said to her, "If you give me the key to the chest, that I may take money as much as I wish, I will go; and if not, I will not go." She gave him the key and gave him a satchel, and he took one hundred sixty gold pieces and came to the Maggid. The Maggid said to him, "Why have you come? Did I not tell you her state?" R. Yosef said, "She gave me the key and gave me authority over all the money she has." At once the Maggid sent for R. Michel, of blessed memory, and for R. Leib the priest, of blessed memory, and the aforementioned R. Yosef, that they should go to the ritual bath. The Maggid was still able to go on his feet, except that he could not go to the water; and the three of them went, and the Maggid also went with them, and the three entered the ritual bath while the Maggid stood over them. They lingered under the water, and when they came up from the water the Maggid said, "You have not yet accomplished it." And they immersed thus several times. R. Yosef said that the Maggid wished to teach him the intentions, but "it is better that I go in my innocence." They lingered in the ritual bath until it was very late, and R. Yosef grew chilled, for he was old, and said, "Master of the world, bring me the rebbetzin as a gift; and if not, I am in danger." And afterward [the Maggid] commanded them to leave the ritual bath and come up, for "thank God, you have accomplished that the rebbetzin will live and bear three sons." And he said how many males and how many females, but I do not remember. The Maggid had commanded, before they went to the ritual bath, that they cook a dish with much spice, so that he might warm himself after the ritual bath; and after he ate, the Maggid said that he should hide himself, so that they would not find him, for there would be a great commotion. He also shut the door behind him and let no one enter until she had borne the sons and lived.

---

## §51

I heard from R. Meir of Anipoli that the aforementioned R. Yosef the teacher dwelt in a certain city — I have forgotten the name of the city — and his house was near a house of idol worship. Once the Baal Shem Tov came to his city and saw his house near the house of idolatry, and said, "In this house dwells a righteous man." When R. Yosef came to greet him, he asked, "Why do you dwell near a house of idolatry?" He answered him, "I am a very poor man; if I had money, I would move my house to another place." And the matter did not delay, and the house of idolatry was burned, and the minister of the city did not permit another house to be built again in that place.

---

## §52

I also heard from the aforementioned R. Meir that there was in that city a craftsman called a *stocking-maker* (knitter), who prayed in summer and winter regularly in the synagogue; even when there were not ten in the synagogue, he would pray alone. Once the Baal Shem Tov was in that city, and while he was smoking his pipe before the prayer, he gazed through the window at the craftsman going to the synagogue, and he shuddered greatly. The Baal Shem Tov said to the householder, "Go out and see who this is who has gone with a prayer shawl and tefillin in his hand." The householder went out and saw, and said, "A certain craftsman is going to the synagogue." The Baal Shem Tov said to call him to come to him. The householder said to him, "I know that he is half-mad, and surely he will not want to come," and the Baal Shem Tov was silent. After the prayer he sent for the aforementioned craftsman to bring him four pairs of stockings. He came and brought the stockings with him. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "A pair of stockings for how much?" He said, "One and a half gold pieces each." He said to him, "Perhaps a pair for the price of one gold piece?" but he answered him nothing. The Baal Shem Tov commanded another to bargain with him further, and that man said to him, "Perhaps you will sell for less than a gold and a half?" He said to him, "If I were willing to sell for less, I would have said so the first time." And the Baal Shem Tov paid him for the four pairs of stockings. He asked him, "What do you do?" and he said, "I do my craft." He asked him, "How do you conduct yourself?" He said, "I make no fewer than forty pairs or fifty pairs at a time, and at one time I place them in a large trough that I have with hot water, and I tread them until they are made properly." He asked him, "And do you not sell them [yourself]?" He answered, "I do not go out the door of my house, and the shopkeepers come to my house and buy from me; and they bring me wool, which they buy for me, and I give them a profit for their trouble. And to your honor too I did not go except for your honor's sake; and I do not go from the door of my house except to the synagogue. If there is a quorum, I pray with the congregation, and if there is no quorum there, I pray alone in my place." The Baal Shem Tov asked him, "And when you have needed [money] for the weddings of your children, what do you do?" He answered, "The Blessed Name helps me, from it and within it [from the craft itself], from my craft, and I make weddings for my children." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "When you rise at the morning watch, what do you do?" He said, "I make stockings then too." He asked him, "And how do you recite Psalms?" He said, "What I am able to recite by heart, I recite." The Baal Shem Tov said of him that he is a foundation of the synagogue until the coming of the Redeemer, soon in our days, Amen, without end; and from this is seen [the merit of] one who enjoys the toil of his own hands in a kosher manner.

---

## §53

I heard from R. Aharon of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, who recounted before the rabbi of our community: Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to the holy community of Brod and lodged in a certain place near Brod, and at night the Baal Shem Tov trembled, and his knees knocked one against the other, until he woke R. Tzvi the scribe from his sleep because of the sound of the knocking. The scribe said to him, "What is this trembling?" and he recounted to him that the rabbi [from on high] had come to him and asked him, "Who is better, you or Abraham our father, peace be upon him?" "I asked him, 'What is the nature of this question?' He said to me, 'You will come to the holy community of Brod and they will do you great honor; if, Heaven forbid, you do not strengthen yourself, you will lose all that has been until now' — and I trembled exceedingly." And when he came to the holy community of Brod, the wealthy men came to greet him in garments of honor, and he began to play with the horses and to stroke them and pat them with his hands in the manner of those who play with horses. Now know how far the sin-fearing of the Baal Shem Tov reached.

---

## §54

I heard from the mouth of R. Zelig of the holy community of Letishev — although this tale is not about the Baal Shem Tov, nevertheless I write it because from this tale we can learn the fear of Heaven. A tale of a certain man who was tender in years, and he was a relative of the aforementioned R. Zelig: He saw in a dream that he was walking with two pious men in a field, and they said to one another, "Let us go and visit Gehenna and see what they do within it." They went and came to Gehenna and saw a great gate, and they opened it and saw that this was not Gehenna; and meanwhile they saw that one of them vanished. They opened the second gate and saw that this too was a vestibule, and the second vanished, and he remained alone. He weighed back and forth whether to enter or not, and resolved in his mind, "Since I am here, I will enter." He saw that this was Gehenna, and a certain man stood in prayer shawl and tefillin by the gate. The man asked him, "What are you doing here, and whence are you?" Now he was from Mezhibuzh, and the people of Mezhibuzh were always accustomed to say that they were from the holy community of Bar, which was near Bar. He said to him, "I am from the holy community of Bar, and I came to see Gehenna." And he saw bodies consumed by fire, like a brand plucked from the fire, in great heaps like sheaves on a threshing floor. That man said to him, "Since you are from the holy community of Bar, go to the other side of that heap and you will see a certain young man from the holy community of Bar lying there too." And he was greatly ashamed before him [the thought of being seen there], for they held him to be a worthy man, and he returned to the gate and saw that the bodies were lying quietly and were not being judged. He asked the man standing at the gate, "Why are the wicked not being judged today?" The man answered him, "From this I know that you did not say 'May there ascend and come' in the evening prayer; therefore you forgot that today is the New Moon." And indeed it was so. Afterward he asked him to let him go out, and [the man] said, "I will not let you in any way." He asked him again and said, "Let me go, for I am a worthy man." He said to him, "What is your worthiness? You went to collect alms and had base thoughts," and he detailed for him the thoughts. He answered again, "I must pay for the tuition of teaching [my children]." That man began to taste it with his lips and said, "Very fine and very good — nevertheless it is impossible to let you go out." He began to ask and plead with him, and [the man] said, "I will counsel you: hide under my prayer shawl, and soon the prince of Gehenna will come here in a great storm, and they will open the gate, and you will slip out of the gate and go forth." And when from afar the great noise of the prince of Gehenna was heard, the bodies began to tremble a great trembling, and he hid himself under his prayer shawl, and when the prince of Gehenna came and opened the gate, he slipped himself out of the gate and went out. And a bit of the storm wind entered into him, and he began to cough — what is called a *cough* — and from the coughing he woke from his sleep. And from that day onward he had the weakness of the cough for a full year, and he died of that illness. And that man whom he saw in Gehenna, who was from the holy community of Bar, lived two years after this incident.

---

## §55

I further heard from the aforementioned R. Zelig, who heard from R. Shneur, son of that righteous man R. Moshe who was preacher in the holy community of Rashkov, that he saw in the holy community of Ladmir a manuscript of a certain rabbi who composed a book [titled with the numerical value 419], and there it is said that the author saw in a dream that he entered a certain palace in the Garden of Eden, and saw that it was exceedingly splendid, and his eye was not sated with gazing at it, and he lingered there because of the greatness of its beauty. Meanwhile he saw there that the *samech-mem* [the adversary] entered, in the form of a dog, into this palace, and there a court sat, and he informed against a certain villager before them, saying, "It is true that he studies regularly and is charitable, but I have a claim against him: that he has dwelt in this village several years, and every year he robbed his gentiles," and he made an accounting before the court of a great sum. The court rebuked him as one rebukes a dog, and he fled. When the rabbi [the dreamer] heard the informing, his heart grieved for [the villager], and he did not wish to leave there until he heard the end. After about half an hour the *samech-mem* came a second time and again made his claim, and they rebuked him a second time, and he fled, and returned a third time and stood before the court and said, "Master of the world, I have come with a claim before the court, and they do not heed my words." A proclamation went forth from above that they should issue him his judgment, and they wrote him a ruling: that the lord of the aforementioned village would take all that [the villager] had and take him and his household into imprisonment, or that one of his offspring would apostatize — and the choice was in the hand of the tax-farmer [the villager]. And [the adversary] took the ruling in his mouth and went off. The rabbi woke from his sleep and was greatly distressed over the tax-farmer, for he loved him much, and especially since he had heard that the *samech-mem* himself praised him; and he sent for the tax-farmer. He recounted to him the affair and clarified for him this [choice]: that the lord would take all that he had and imprison him and his household, and that, Heaven forbid, one of his offspring not apostatize. And so it was, that the lord took all that he had and imprisoned him and his household. And the rabbi had to bestir himself and collect money for the redemption of these captives, and he redeemed him and his household. From here a person must see how far to keep himself from robbing a gentile, as it is written in the books that, Heaven forbid, the *samech-mem* collects this from the holiness [later].

---

## §56

I heard from the rabbi of our community that R. Leibush of the holy community of Mezeritch was with the Baal Shem Tov during the Days of Awe and fell ill before Rosh Hashanah. The Baal Shem Tov busied himself with his healing the whole night of the eve of Rosh Hashanah, and also the night of Rosh Hashanah, and he saw no sign of death, Heaven forbid, at all. When he went to the study hall at the time of prayer, the aforementioned R. Leibush grew confused and very weak, and they informed the Baal Shem Tov, but he did not hear, for they were afraid to cry out to him aloud. When R. Yitzchak of the holy community of Mezeritch saw that the Baal Shem Tov did not turn, he cried out to him in a great voice, and the Baal Shem Tov answered and said, "Why did you not tell me?" And he ran to his house and found the Angel of Death standing over him. He rebuked it with a rebuke, and the Angel of Death fled, and the Baal Shem Tov took R. Leibush by his hand, and at once he recovered, and he led him to the study hall, for he feared to leave him in his house lest the Angel of Death return, Heaven forbid. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov said that for this matter they wished to thrust him out of this world and the World to Come; and he said that he had done this only because the matter came upon him suddenly, for he had seen no signs of death in him beforehand, and "when I suddenly saw the Angel of Death, my heart grieved for him and I did this."

---

## §57

I heard from the rabbi of our community: There was a certain man from the holy community of Mezeritch with some wealth. When he came near the days of old age, he thought in his heart, "What is this that I have done all the days of my life, and what will become of me, that I have consumed my years in vanity and emptiness, without Torah and without good deeds?" And he did not know what to do. It came into his mind to give charity in secret, and he placed every day in the tefillin bag of R. Yitzchak one *thaler*. The aforementioned R. Yitzchak found the money once and twice and said in his heart, "Who is this who puts money into my tefillin bag?" And he asked the beadle of the study hall to set his eye [to see] who was handling his tefillin bag during the prayer, and not to reveal it to anyone but him. The beadle watched for this until he saw that this man was handling the prayer-shawl-and-tefillin bag of R. Yitzchak; and after the prayer that man revealed [himself] to R. Yitzchak. R. Yitzchak began to draw him near and to study with him the six orders of the Mishnah; and every chapter of Mishnah that he studied with him, he reviewed with him several times until it was fluent in his mouth. And he returned and studied with him another chapter, until he finished with him all the chapters of the Mishnah. And he studied with him *Ein Yaakov* too in this manner. When he finished the Mishnah and *Ein Yaakov*, he became somewhat learned, and he studied the books of the God-fearing and the books of moral instruction and the book of the Zohar, and he acquired for himself [membership in] a Mishnah society, and he studied until the day of his death. When he fell into his deathbed, before his death, R. Yitzchak went to visit him and compelled him to give him a handshake [a pledge] that he would appear to him at all times.

The custom in that city was that at the time of expiry [death], all the Mishnah society would come at the time of the soul's departure and study Mishnah; and throughout the thirty days [of mourning] they studied and finished all the Mishnah for his soul. After the thirty days he appeared to the aforementioned R. Yitzchak, who asked him where his rest was. He said, "My rest is among the righteous in the Garden of Eden, and in truth I rectified everything in [my] life; only one matter remained upon me that I did not rectify, and the society, in their study on my behalf, rectified the matter." And he kept coming to him at all times, and R. Yitzchak would ask him statements of the Zohar, and he revealed to him how they study the statement in the Garden of Eden.

Once he revealed to someone a certain statement that he [the deceased] had told him, and [the deceased] did not come to him for a long time. Nevertheless he was compelled to come because of the handshake. When he came, [R. Yitzchak] asked him why he had delayed coming, "now that I revealed [it] to no one." He said, "Know that they raised me to very lofty worlds, and it is very bitter for me to descend from those worlds to this world; therefore I delayed coming, and I was compelled to come because of the handshake. Therefore I ask your honor to forgive me the handshake." And he forgave him the handshake.

---

## §58

I further heard from the rabbi of our community: Once the son of R. Yosef Yuzpa of the holy community of Ostrah was accepted as head of court in the holy community of Polonnoye, and he brought with him a certain judge [dayan] so that he could act brazenly against the preacher, of blessed memory. On the Sabbath the preacher prayed before the ark, and R. Yitzchak heard that the judge was cursing the preacher during the prayer, but he answered him nothing. After the prayer, on Sunday after the Sabbath, R. Yitzchak said, "I heard well that you cursed our preacher," and there was a quarrel between them. In the midst of the quarrel he looked at him and said, "You are clothed in unfit tefillin." They took the tefillin from the judge's head and removed the parchment portions, and found that they were unfit. The judge strove to render them fit according to the Shulchan Aruch, but R. Yitzchak showed him that he was an ignoramus and did not know the law. And out of shame the judge fled the city.

---

## §59

It also happened that R. Yitzchak recognized [unfit] tefillin on the head of a certain man. For before his death [R. Yitzchak] was shut up in his house because of weakness, and he made a prayer quorum in his house, and a certain man of the city prayed there, and after the prayer he would study; and it seems to me that his name was R. Berekh. R. Yitzchak looked at him and said, "Berekh, it appears to me that there is a disqualification in your tefillin." And he removed them from his head and gave them to him to check, and he found that they were unfit from the time of their writing. The tefillin had been with him from the time he became bar mitzvah, for they were from a good scribe, and he held fast to them; and he was about sixty years old at that time. It was very bitter to him that he had not fulfilled the commandment of tefillin all his days, and he urged the aforementioned R. Yitzchak to write him tefillin, and R. Yitzchak promised him. But because of his weakness he put him off from day to day, until he wrote him the parchment portions of the tefillin. Afterward he went home and said to him, "Do not say that I never wrote such tefillin in my life — rather, even from the days of Ezra onward there were no such tefillin." And about two hours after this he died and departed from the world. And this R. Yitzchak was the brother of R. Yosef of Kaminka.

---

## §60

I heard from the rabbi of our community a tale concerning the mother of the brothers R. Yosef and R. Yitzchak, whose dwelling was in the holy community of Shatanov, and her name was *the pious Rivele*. Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to that holy community, and near the city he saw a certain light upon one of the people of the city, and he stood in his place and observed that this light was resting upon a certain woman. When he came to the city and esteemed men came to greet him, the Baal Shem Tov said to them, "Be ashamed and abashed, for I saw a light upon a certain woman, and not upon a man." He asked the people of the city, "Do you know upon which woman this light that I saw could be resting?" They said to him, "Surely it is the woman called the pious Rivele." It was in his mind to send for her, but the people said, "There is no need to call her, for surely she will come of herself to your honor to ask of you a donation, for she is active in commandments." The next day, after the prayer, the Baal Shem Tov said, "I see that she is coming, and she has fixed in her heart to take from me no less than forty gold pieces. You will see what I will do to her." This woman came and said to the Baal Shem Tov, "There are here several sick poor folk; therefore let your honor give a fitting donation." He placed before her one *zekser*, and she said nothing, but only made motions as though she did not wish to take it; and he placed before her another *zekser*, and she did not take it. He said to her, "Who appointed you a charity collector, to steal from the charity box?" But she answered nothing and was not offended at this, and these words made no impression upon her, because she held to the qualities of humility and lowliness exceedingly, literally like dust. And she did not go from him until he was compelled to give her forty gold pieces.

Toward evening she came to him and said, "There is a certain doctor [healer], and he is very ill; therefore pray for him that he live, for he is in great danger." He said, "And let there be one adulterer fewer in this world." She said to him, "Who says that it is so?" He said to the men standing there, "I speak the truth." They said, "It is true." She answered and said, "Firstly, they did not see [the act] clearly; and moreover, he is an ignoramus and does not know the gravity of the sin — and had he known the gravity of the sin, he would not have transgressed." And in truth the Baal Shem Tov had heard the accusation against [the doctor] above, and he repeated the accusation before her in order that she might plead merit for him; and the plea that she pleaded on his behalf was accepted above, and the doctor was healed of his illness.

When she walked over her husband's grave, she would knock with a staff on the grave and say, in these words, "Hirshel, Hirshel, go before the Throne of Glory and pray for your sons, that they be poor and destitute, so that they not turn from the ways of the Lord."

Once her two sons R. Yitzchak and R. Yosef came to her for the Sabbath to fulfill the commandment of honoring one's mother, and they came there on Thursday. The people of the city came to greet them, and they recognized one another, for they had been companions before, and the old love between them was awakened. The next day, after the prayer, on the Sabbath eve, she set the table and gave them to eat and sat with them, and said to them, "My sons, I ask of you one thing, that you do for my sake." They said, "Surely all that you command us, we will do and obey." She said to them, "Give me a handshake [pledge] that you will do my will." They said to her, "Our mother, it is astonishing to us that you would suspect us in this." She said to them, "Presumably it is in my mind to ask a great thing, and I feared lest you transgress my will." They appeased her not to require a handshake and promised to do as she wished. She said to them, "I ask of you that you travel at once on your way and not rest [spend the Sabbath] here, for the Blessed Name gave me sons that I might have satisfaction from them in the world of truth, and not in this world." They said to her, "And is it possible that the people of our city will let us travel from here on the Sabbath eve?" She said to them, "Your wagons — send them at once outside the city, and I will lead you through the back of the street so that the people of the city not see you." And so they did.

---

## §61

I heard from R. Shmuel the magnate, who recounted before the rabbi, that he and R. Ber son of Bonim traveled for the redemption of captives before Rosh Hashanah, and came to the great rabbi the Maggid; and the Maggid wished to detain them for Rosh Hashanah, but they did not wish to, for they said, "We must redeem the captives by the coming Rosh Hashanah." They traveled on their way and came to R. Pinchas, and he detained them, but they again refused, for the aforementioned reason. R. Pinchas said, "Let R. Shmuel return home to redeem the captives, and R. Ber will be here." But R. Shmuel did not wish to leave R. Ber. R. Pinchas said, "R. Ber must be here, for he has no children." R. Shmuel said, "I too need that thing." He said to him, "You will come for the coming Shemini Atzeret." And R. Ber remained there for Rosh Hashanah. Before Rosh Hashanah R. Ber fell ill and could not go on Rosh Hashanah to the synagogue to pray. R. Pinchas came to his house to read the Torah, and he called R. Ber to come up to the Torah, and they dressed him and led him to the Torah scroll; and after the second blessing he recovered, and a daughter was also born to him that year, and she became the daughter-in-law of R. Pinchas. And R. Shmuel came for Shemini Atzeret, and a daughter was also born to him that year.

---

## §62

I heard from my father-in-law, and also from the rabbi of our community: When the Baal Shem Tov was still dwelling at the inn in the village, they once needed rain, and he prayed for rain. There was there a certain sorceress who had a demon, and she made witchcraft so that the rain would not fall; and the Baal Shem Tov, in his prayer, spoiled her witchcraft. The demon told her that he was spoiling her witchcraft. The sorceress came to his mother and said to her, "Tell your son to leave me and let me be, for I will work witchcraft against him." His mother supposed that [the sorceress] was quarreling with him over a debt for brandy, and his mother said to him, "My son, leave that gentile woman, for she is a sorceress." He said to his mother, "I am not concerned with her," and he did his own [thing] and prayed for rain. The sorceress came a second time to his mother, and afterward she sent her demon against him. When [the demon] came to him, he could not enter within his four cubits. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "You dared to come to me — immediately you will go and harm the gentile woman through a small window of a certain dovecote." And so he did. And afterward they imprisoned him [the demon] in a prison in the forest, and he cannot move from there forever. When the Baal Shem Tov became famous, he traveled with people through a forest; he stopped and went to the city and peered at [the demon] sitting imprisoned, and laughed greatly. They asked him, and he recounted to them the aforementioned tale.

---

## §63

I heard from R. Yoel, who was preacher in the holy community of Nemirov: Once the Baal Shem Tov was traveling on the road and heard a proclamation: "One of these two things I impose upon you: either the Haidamaks will fall upon you and rob you of your money" — and he had with him money in the sum of two hundred gold pieces — "or you will have a fever illness." (And the third option I have forgotten.) And he had pity on the money, because he had a great creditor at home, and he chose the fever illness; and so it was. He came on the Sabbath eve to the holy community of Sharigrad, very weak from the aforementioned fever; nevertheless he went to the study hall and prayed before the ark, and the prayer was a flame of fire. After the prayer they led him by his arms, because of his weakness, to his lodging, and he commanded that they bring him very good wine, and he drank one cup and recovered and was healed at once. And he said, by way of jest, "It would be good to have the fever yet another hour, and to drink yet another cup of wine, for it is very good."

---

## §64

I heard from R. Falk, head of court of the holy community of Tytschilik: Once, on the night after the Sabbath, the Baal Shem Tov commanded his stepson-in-law R. Yosef Ashkenazi to recite *Ein Yaakov* before him, while he lay on the bed and heard from his mouth the *Ein Yaakov*. In one place the Baal Shem Tov said Torah on a certain statement of *Ein Yaakov*, and again [R. Yosef] recited *Ein Yaakov* before him. In the midst of the recitation, the aforementioned R. Yosef saw the *maggid mesharim* [a heavenly mentor], named R. Yosef, who had passed away about three-quarters of a year before this, and he saw him enter the house clothed in Sabbath garments with a hat on his head — called a *hut* — and he said in a loud voice, "A good week to our rabbi," and walked as in life, with a staff in his hand. When he saw him, he trembled greatly and the *Ein Yaakov* fell from his hand. The Baal Shem Tov passed his hand over his face, and he no longer saw him. The Baal Shem Tov said, "Take in your hand one candle and go to the side" — for he used to say that one candle is harmful to the eyes, and likewise three, but two are good for the eyes. And he did so, and [the Baal Shem Tov] lingered with him about half an hour or more, and he saw that the Baal Shem Tov was speaking with him [the visitor]. Afterward the Baal Shem Tov called R. Yosef and said, in these words, "The German [R. Yosef the maggid] has come to me," and he came and recited before him *Ein Yaakov* again. In the midst of the study the Baal Shem Tov began to complain against him and said to him, "Why were you afraid? Did R. Yosef the maggid, the German, slaughter people like you in his life, that you feared him after his death?" [R. Yosef] asked him, "By what merit did I see him?" He said to him, "Because you recited before me and I recited before you, and I was purifying you with my words, and the two of us were bound together and became one; and on account of this you saw him. And had you strengthened yourself in your mind, you would have heard what he spoke with me, and you too could have asked him whatever your heart desired, and he would have answered you. And not only that, but you would have had an acquaintance with him and would have seen him continually." And R. Yosef was greatly grieved over this matter, for it is a level of prophecy when one sees the soul of a righteous man, as is said in the book *Sha'arei Kedushah* of R. Chaim Vital. And he asked why [the maggid] came and what was his need, and he said that he came for his own need.

---

## §65

I heard from R. Yaakov, head of court of the holy community of Smilna, who heard from his father R. Meir Charif, of blessed memory: Once the Baal Shem Tov came to the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye — and I am uncertain whether the tale was in the holy community of Nemirov or the holy community of Sharigrad — and he came several days before the Sabbath and did not eat in all those days. The aforementioned rabbi urged him greatly to eat, and he answered, "Let the rabbi exempt me from eating, for here there are great sparks [of holiness], and it is hard to rectify them, and there is danger in the matter — when one does not rectify them, they kill the person; therefore I do not wish to eat." On the Sabbath eve in the morning he commanded that they give him to eat, and he ate. After the meal he called the rabbi privately and said, "Know that they revealed to me from heaven that in the villages around the city there are two teachers who have a portion in these sparks that I must rectify, and they made it from heaven so that it would seem to them that they are sick, in order that they come to the city, and that they come to heal them. Therefore station your servant at the city gate, and he will bring them to you, and have mercy on them and accord them no honor at all; and let your honor promise them to plead on their behalf before me and to heal them, and speak to their hearts that they go to the ritual bath — for because it seems to them that they are sick, they will not wish to go to the ritual bath. And say to them that I love it when they come before me pure from the ritual bath; and ask them for [their] Sabbath shirts and overcoats, for they are poor. And let them eat on the Sabbath at a householder's, not at your honor's. And when they come to the study hall, even though I will be standing at the Amidah, whisper in my ear that they have come." And so they did. After the meal they came to the rabbi's house, and they did not give them even a flask of honey; and likewise in the morning. And at the third meal they ate there and were given honey. After Havdalah the Baal Shem Tov was filled with joy and took his pipe and went to the small house where he was lodging. The rabbi too wished to follow him, and the two aforementioned teachers came and asked him to bring them in to the Baal Shem Tov. He said to them, "Come with me," and they entered with the rabbi. The Baal Shem Tov said to the rabbi, "They do not need healing, and the shirts and overcoats — give them to them as a gift, and let them go in peace." And the teachers knew nothing [of what had transpired].

---

## §66

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov did not wish to be in the holy community of Anipoli, for he said, "I have no business there, for R. Shimshon of that holy community already rectified there all that was needed." Once his brother-in-law R. Gershon spent the Sabbath in that holy community and came from there and said to the Baal Shem Tov, "I attained there a great light from the aforementioned R. Shimshon." He said, "You are deluding yourself." Once they sent for the Baal Shem Tov to come to that holy community, and at the meal he sat opposite the window and opened the window and kept gazing through the window every moment. R. Yosef of Kaminka wondered at this and wished to know the reason why he gazed through the window every moment. What did he do? When the Baal Shem Tov turned from the window, he shut the window; and when the Baal Shem Tov wished to gaze, he opened it, and again R. Yosef shut it a second time. And so he did a third time, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and said, "Who is this who shuts the window?" R. Yosef said, "It is I, for the cold enters the house through the window; and why do you open it at every moment?" He said, "Do you not see how the pillar of Torah of R. Shimshon shines from the earth to the firmament?" And they told him that in this place there had been a refuse heap at that time, and they said that his study hall had been in this place; and he said, "Today it has become known to me what your brother-in-law R. Gershon said."

---

## §67

I heard that there was a certain tax-farmer near the holy community of Mezhibuzh, and a certain man came and made a [higher] bid on the lease, and the tax-farmer was driven out of the village. He remained in debt to the lord of that village and had nothing with which to pay. The lord, and also the new tax-farmer, commanded that they not let him travel from that village to any place. Nevertheless the former tax-farmer traveled to the Baal Shem Tov, and when this became known to the new tax-farmer, he informed against him to the lord, saying that he had traveled to a sorcerer to work witchcraft against you. The lord was angry at him and took his son into imprisonment.

This tax-farmer did not find the Baal Shem Tov at home, for he was in the holy community of Kaminka, and he traveled after him to the city, but did not find him there, for the Baal Shem Tov had traveled home, and he traveled after him and overtook him on the road. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "What can I do for you on the road? Continue your journey after me to my house." And on the way the Baal Shem Tov saw that the lord had seized his son into imprisonment. He said, "There is no time to wait," and they came to a certain river, and he immersed in it. On his coming out of the river he said to him, "Go home, and at once when you come to the village go to the fortress [the lord's residence]." And he did so. When the lord saw him from the window, he was afraid lest he harm him with his witchcraft, and he drove his son out of imprisonment and sent his servant to drive [the tax-farmer] out of the village with all that he had. Afterward the lord sent a letter to the commissar of the holy community of Mezhibuzh with a great complaint against the Baal Shem Tov, in these words: "Inasmuch as you keep a sorcerer in your city." And the [commissar?] answered the lord, "See with all your might to strive and to appease the Baal Shem Tov, for he is no sorcerer; and if you do not appease him, you will be punished from heaven." And at once the lord sent to appease him, and sent him a gift, a wagon with all kinds of flour and fowl.

---

## §68

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye that the Baal Shem Tov could not speak with people because of his cleaving [to God], and he would speak out of [normal] manner. His known master [in heaven] taught him to recite every day the chapter "Happy are those whose way is blameless" [Psalm 119] and several other marked chapters of Psalms, and showed him a [point of] wisdom, and by means of this he began to speak with people; and his cleaving was not nullified, and he would recite every day those chapters of Psalms.

---

## §69

I heard from R. Pesach, son of R. Yaakov of Kaminka, that the Baal Shem Tov was on the road and came to a certain city, and heard a proclamation that he should lodge in the house of so-and-so. He came to that house, but they did not wish to receive him at that lodging, because the householder's child was very ill. He sent the scribe to the house, and the woman said, "How is it possible to lodge here? Surely you see the sick child, and I am in great distress," and she cursed the Baal Shem Tov. The householder could not be brazen, and he went out to the Baal Shem Tov to ask of him, and said that it was a thing impossible. The Baal Shem Tov swore to him that if he lodged with him, the child would live. And [the householder] received him into his house, and at once the Baal Shem Tov went to the ritual bath and saw that it was not well with the child, and he commanded that no one be in that house, and they all went to another house, and the scribe too he commanded not to be in that house until he called him to give him wine for Kiddush — for this tale was on the Sabbath eve. And [the Baal Shem Tov] remained alone with the child and prayed the afternoon prayer by the child, and he lingered much into the night, until the scribe feared lest, Heaven forbid, the Baal Shem Tov endanger himself because of the greatness of his exertion in prayer over the sick child, for the matter was dangerous. The scribe went and opened the door quietly and heard him saying to the child's soul, "Enter the body, for you will be compelled to enter, for I will not swear falsely." And he did not know whether the child was dead or still alive with some life-force in him. The scribe went from there, and after a short time he returned and entered and found him lying on the ground with hands and feet outstretched. And he rose and said, "Did I not tell you to enter the child's body?" and he called out, "Hirsh, give wine for Kiddush," and he ate with the scribe and did not sleep the whole night. In the morning he handed over the remedies and instructions to the scribe, and he went to pray in the study hall. The child's mother understood that the child had recovered, and she began to weep a great weeping. The scribe heard and asked her, "Why are you weeping?" She said to him, "And how should I not weep, that I cursed such a righteous man?" He answered her, "Do not weep; my rabbi is a good man and will forgive you." When the Baal Shem Tov came from his prayer, he too heard that she was weeping. He asked the scribe, and [the scribe] told him, and he sent the scribe to her and said, "Tell her not to weep, and let her make a good third meal, and I promise her that the child will sit with us at the table for that meal." And this — that the Baal Shem Tov fell to the ground with hands and feet outstretched — was because he took upon himself to receive the *pulsa denura* [a fiery lash] for the oath, and the soul was compelled to enter the body. And he accomplished for the child that he would live more than sixty years and have children and a good livelihood all the days of his life; and from this it appears that the time of the end had come for the child, and therefore he had to pray for him, that he live, and for livelihood, and for children.

---

## §70

The aforementioned R. Pesach further recounted to me: Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled from Kaminka to his home, and R. Baruch of Kaminka traveled together with him in a separate wagon, and the scribe sat with him, and the flour wagon also followed after them. The Baal Shem Tov said, "At the place of our lodging we will pray the afternoon prayer." This was in the winter days, and there was great cold; and that place was several *parsah* away, and they grew very chilled and the cold entered their bodies, and they said, "It is impossible to reach that place, for the place is still far, and the cold will overcome us all." His servant too said that he had grown very chilled. They were traveling through a forest, and the Baal Shem Tov commanded them to stop, and he touched a certain tree with his finger, and the tree kindled and burned, and they warmed themselves by the fire. His servant too drew off his boot and dried the cloth on his feet; and afterward they traveled to that place. As they traveled from this place, R. Baruch kept looking [back] to see what would become of this tree, and the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him not to look.

---

## §71

I heard from the rabbi R. Falk the priest that his father-in-law R. Shemeril had no male child. The Baal Shem Tov used to lodge with him, and there was no special house for him, and he lodged in that house, and the women removed from there to their neighbor's and cooked there, and at mealtime brought [the food] here. Once, one day before his departure, [R. Shemeril] wished to ask him to pray on his behalf that he have a male child, and he circled about him but could not speak because of shame; and the next day, before the departure, he again circled about to speak to him but could not, and was ashamed before him. Suddenly the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Send for your wife." She came, and the Baal Shem Tov took his staff in his hand and said to her, "See that you bear a male child, for I will break your bones with this staff." And so it was, that she bore a male child that year, and his name was R. David, who was head of court in the holy community of Tytschilik. Now R. Shemeril's custom was, even if his wife bore a daughter, to travel to the Baal Shem Tov and take from him an amulet, and give him two gold pieces or more; for at that time he was a merchant and had about three thousand gold pieces. When the aforementioned son was born to him, he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov to take from him an amulet as was his custom from before. He said to him, "Your wife bore a son?" He said, "Yes." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Know that you will no longer have money, for they gave you a choice — either money or a son — and I chose for you the good [option], that you have a male child." And so it was: all the merchandise he bought, he lost on it, until he had lost all his money, and afterward he was accepted as preacher in the holy community of Vyerkhovka. And I, when I knew him — when he was frequent at the [house of the] rabbi of the holy community of Kosov — was at that time judge of the holy community of Sagran.

---

## §72

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye and from the rabbi of our community that R. Nachman of Kosov had a certain relative named R. Yudel of Chidnov, and his father R. Yosef was a preacher. Now R. Yudel's way was that he did not wish to derive benefit from others, but only from the toil of his own hands, and his commerce was in iron. Once he traveled to a certain *rudni* [foundry/forge settlement] and wished to spend the Sabbath there. The householder said, "And what will your honor eat? Fish I have none, and meat he will not eat because I did not inspect the knife. Therefore my counsel is: near here there is another *rudni*, and there dwells a well-off man, and surely he has fish and a slaughterer; let your honor travel to him." He traveled, and when he came to the water-channel near that *rudni* — the road passed through the water-channel, for the water was not deep — and now there had been great rains, or the time of the thaw of the snows, and that channel had grown swollen, and they did not understand this and wished to cross through the channel. A certain dog ran ahead, before [them], into that channel and drowned in that water, and the dog kept rising and sinking in the water and cried out in a bitter voice, until the mercy of this pious one was stirred and his eyes shed tears. He saw that it was impossible to cross the water, and he returned to that *rudni* and asked the householder to strive that there be fish for the Sabbath. The householder went and urged the fishermen, and they fished and brought a large fish called a *pike*. The householder said, "I have dwelt in this village several years and never had such a fish present itself to me." And they made him several dishes from it. On the Sabbath night he was sitting at the meal and singing hymns, and he dozed off at the table. His father appeared to him in a dream and said to him, "Know that I am reincarnated in this fish, and the informer whom I pursued in my life was reincarnated in this dog that drowned. And this was his rectification — that he drowned on your account, my son, to save you. And I had to be reincarnated in this fish because I pursued him, and you rectified me in that your eyes shed tears over the drowning of the dog. And see, my son, how you eat this fish." When he came to the Baal Shem Tov, the Baal Shem Tov said to him that thus he used to say: that R. Yudel was a reincarnation of Samuel the prophet.

---

## §73

I heard from R. Shneur, grandson of R. Nachman, that R. Yudel was with R. Nachman in the holy community of Ladmir, and R. Nachman built there a study hall literally on the water, and the bathhouse was near the study hall, and they went on the Sabbath morning to the ritual bath. R. Nachman was very quick, and R. Yudel was a bit lazy, and while he was [still] taking off his garments, R. Nachman was standing before the ark and praying. When R. Yudel came out of the ritual bath, he heard R. Nachman singing "The majesty and the faithfulness," and he was inflamed and ran to the study hall in his shirt alone, without garments, and danced there about two hours in the study hall.

---

## §74

I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory, that when R. Nachman groaned a groan in prayer, it broke the heart of the listener, and it seemed to him as though he were cut in two. The Baal Shem Tov said, "As far as R. Nachman traveled, they know what prayer is; and in a place where R. Nachman was not, they do not know what prayer is."

---

## §75

I heard: Once R. Nachman, of blessed memory, was going through the holy community of Zalrava with merchandise at the time of the morning prayer, and he stopped with his wagon opposite the study hall, and took the prayer shawl and tefillin and entered the study hall, and donned prayer shawl and tefillin, and stood before the ark to pray without permission. And it grieved them how this guest dared to stand before the ark without permission. But when they heard the words coming forth from his mouth, they were sweeter than honey and the dripping of the honeycomb, and they enjoyed and kept silent. Nevertheless it angered them, and after the Rabbis' Kaddish he began "Give thanks" before "Blessed is He who spoke," and they were all filled with anger and fury, and it was in their mind to drag him from the ark. But because they greatly desired to hear his words and his prayers, they could do nothing, and they kept weighing the matter back and forth until he finished his prayer. When he finished all his prayers, all the people of the study hall fell upon him and said to him, "How did your heart fill you to stand before the ark without permission and to alter the text that our fathers and our fathers' fathers, who were the great ones of the generation, did not pray?" He answered and said, "And who says that they are in the Garden of Eden?" When they heard his answer, they were filled with even more fury against him. And the author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef* was there, and he was very old, and he had given his greatness [position] to a certain disciple to be preacher in his place, named R. Zalman, and R. Zalman fell upon him [in attack] more than all of them. And R. Alexander, a disciple of R. Zalman, came and said, "Leave the man, for he is always with the Name; and since it has come to our hand, let us hear this matter."

---

## §76

I heard from the rabbi [of Polonnoye] that he used to say, in these words: It was a saying in the mouth of the righteous R. Yaakov Yosef, of blessed memory, "It is easier for me to say ten [Talmudic] distinctions than to pray one Amidah." And I heard from R. Zusia of Anipoli: When he traveled from his brother R. Elimelech by way of Zolkva, "I stood in the study hall in a certain place to pray, and the prayer was pure and clear, of the order of the prayers of the Baal Shem Tov, and I did not know from what cause." And they said that in this place had been the place [seat] of the author of the book *Toldot Yaakov Yosef*.

---

## §77

I heard from the rabbi of our community that when the Baal Shem Tov was ill before his passing, he did not lie in bed, but only became emaciated and his voice was stricken. He would sit in his house of seclusion, and he had not the strength to call his servant when he needed him. So he hung a bell in the large house with a cord drawn to his small house, and the Baal Shem Tov would pull the cord, and the sound of the bell was heard, and the servant came to him. Once R. David Forkes, of blessed memory, preacher of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, came to him and asked him about a certain matter. And from a certain man of the holy community of Bar I heard that the question was how to pray for a sick person by means of telling tales. He began to tell him, and as he spoke he became greatly inflamed, and his face was burning like torches. Fear fell upon the aforementioned R. David, and he wished to flee, but because the Baal Shem Tov was speaking with him face to face, he could not go out. Meanwhile his daughter Mistress Adel (may she live) came and said to her father, "It is time to eat, for the day has turned." And at once the redness departed and the pallor came, and he lay on his bed to rest, and said to her, "My daughter, my daughter, what have you done to me?" And R. David said, "The right is with her, for it is time to eat." The Baal Shem Tov said, "You do not know who was here. On one side stood Elijah, of blessed memory, and on the other side stood the rabbi [from on high], and they were telling me, and I was telling you; and when she came and confused me, they went." And the Baal Shem Tov said that he took the soul of his daughter from the Torah, from the verse "a fiery law unto them" [*esh dat lamo*], the initial letters [of which spell] *Adel*.

---

## §78
