I heard from the rabbi that he said of R. Leib that he was among the "masters of reckoning" of all the day [that he had done], and likewise every single night. And on the day that he passed away, he went to the ritual bath, and on his coming out of the bathhouse he met the Baal Shem Tov, who said to him, "Hurry and do your deeds" — for his way was to hear the Kedushah and Barchu in all the synagogues, and he took a horse and rode upon it from synagogue to synagogue, for it was a muddy season and he could not go on his feet. And on that very day he died and departed from the world.

---

## §79

I heard from the rabbi of our community that there was in Kotov a certain man named Tektiner, who always spoke against the company of the pious ones. Once R. Aharon, brother-in-law of the Baal Shem Tov, heard [him] and was quarreling with him, and this man leaped up and tore at the beard of the aforementioned R. Aharon. R. Gershon, his brother, rose and placed him under ban. In a few days that man died, and after some years R. Aharon died. His brother R. Gershon saw him in a dream during the seven days [of mourning], and heard that he was expounding [Torah], and it pleased him, and he ran to listen; and when he came near, he recognized in him that it was a husk [a demonic shell]. He was angry at it and said, "Wicked one, how did your heart fill you to clothe yourself in the likeness of my brother?" and he spat at it, and it vanished from his eyes. And afterward R. Leib the preacher of Polonnoye also saw him during the seven days, and looked at the sign and saw that it was R. Aharon himself. He asked, "What is this — that a husk clothed itself in your likeness?" He said that a trace of the sin had remained, and through that trace it had the power to clothe itself in his likeness. "May my brother R. Gershon live and have length of days, for by his spitting he annulled the trace from me." He asked him, "How are you now?" He said, "In the Garden of Eden." He asked him about the man Tektiner, what he was doing. He said to him, "He too was in the Garden of Eden, but my mind and thought were not upon him; and when he saw me in the Garden of Eden, he said to me, 'I will summon you to the heavenly court, for through you was my death.' And I was very afraid, and I took the hairs that I had wrapped in a cloth and placed in my chest, and I ran and took them and laid them before the court — and he caused evil to himself, for they drove him out of the Garden of Eden to Gehenna." And he asked him, "How and what are you in the Garden of Eden?" He answered, "The lower Garden of Eden is somewhat material, and when the Sabbath eve came I went to the synagogue, and when I came to the door I saw none of my acquaintances, and I stood by the platform — called the *almemar* — and prayed the afternoon prayer. And before the receiving of the Sabbath a proclamation went forth, 'R. Aharon will receive the Sabbath before the ark,' and I went before the ark and said 'Come, let us sing'; and when I came to 'A psalm, a song for the Sabbath day,' they all vanished from me, and I remained alone, and I did not know where they had gone, and I had great distress from this the whole Sabbath, that I had no company. Therefore I ask your honor to pray for me that I may ascend with them the coming Sabbath." And the preacher said to him, "You left your wife in great poverty, and you did not leave her even the food of one meal." R. Aharon said, "I have two *thaler* hidden in the chest that I hid for a certain need; now take them for her." When he woke from his sleep, he went in the morning to the widow, the wife of R. Aharon, and said to her that she should search in the chest and find there two *thaler*. She answered, "Surely there is no money in the chest." But he urged her, and she searched and found the two *thaler*, and also found the hairs wrapped in a cloth. And from this you may see how clear and accurate their dreams were.

---

## §80

I heard from my fellows, who heard from R. Nachum of Chernobyl: Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Khmelnik, and there was there a certain proofreader [of sacred texts], whose wife was ill; and the Baal Shem Tov's lodging was near the head of court, the son-in-law of the head of the rabbinical court. The proofreader came to the Baal Shem Tov to ask him to give a remedy to his wife, but he did not wish to; and not only that, but he was angry at him and spoke to him words that were not good. The matter was difficult for the aforementioned head of court, and in the evening, when they had all gone, the rabbi asked the Baal Shem Tov why he had not wished to heal the aforementioned woman. He answered that she was a righteous woman, and because of her weakness she protected the city from the robbers in the forest; and when the robbers are gone from the forest, she will of herself be restored to health. He asked him why he was angry at the proofreader, and he answered that he saw judgments resting upon him, and with that anger he annulled the judgments from him.

---

## §81

Once the aforementioned rabbi saw that a certain man came to the Baal Shem Tov, and the Baal Shem Tov drew him near and spoke to him; and this man was famous for adultery. He asked, "Why did you draw near this man famous for adultery?" The Baal Shem Tov answered him, "Are you better than him?" The rabbi examined himself and searched his deeds and found nothing. He said, "I swear that you speak falsely." The Baal Shem Tov said, "I see adultery upon you, and tomorrow I will inform you in detail." The next day he said, "You took upon yourself abstinence and transgressed that acceptance. And I heard this night that Maimonides ruled that this is as though he forbade his wife to himself and said, 'She is to me as a mother'; and look in his book — perhaps you will find it there." Then [the rabbi] admitted to his words, and in Maimonides he found that he ruled so. And such an incident happened to the Baal Shem Tov a third time. (See above, §27.)

---

## §82

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled on the road and lodged in a certain place, and heard [a proclamation]: "Return quickly to your home, for R. Yosef is very weak." He told his servants to turn back, but the servants did not wish to, for they said, "Your honor has a creditor at home, and why should we turn back?" But they turned back, and he came home on Friday morning. He went to the ritual bath before the prayer, and after the prayer he called a certain doctor and went with him to the sick man, and saw that he was very weak and his speech was stricken. He told the doctor to let blood from him, but the doctor did not wish to, saying that it was very dangerous to let blood from him. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "How do you understand it — that if you do not let blood from him, he will live?" The doctor said, "No." "And why were you afraid to let blood from him?" And the Baal Shem Tov commanded at once to let blood from him, and all the people murmured against the Baal Shem Tov about the bloodletting. The Baal Shem Tov too went home, and after about half an hour the sick man asked that they give him water to drink — and thank God, he recovered.

---

## §83

I further heard from him: Once, on the Baal Shem Tov's journey to Khmelnik, he heard a proclamation about R. Yosef the teacher of that holy community, that he would die. When he came to the city, the people of his city came to him and wished to pay him to heal [R. Yosef], but he did not wish to heal him and did not reveal his reason. After the Baal Shem Tov traveled from there, a certain doctor came and healed him, and he recovered. The doctor was still there, and meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov returned to that city, and they told him that the aforementioned R. Yosef had recovered through the remedies of the doctor who healed him. During the Baal Shem Tov's stay there, R. Yosef asked the doctor to make him some salve to drive the lice from his head, which had greatly multiplied because of his lengthy illness. The doctor gave him an ointment to smear on his head, and he smeared his head with this ointment and died. Then the Baal Shem Tov revealed his reason — that he had not wished to heal him because of the proclamation he had heard.

---

## §84

I further heard from the aforementioned rabbi that in the days of the aforementioned preacher of the holy community of Polonnoye, there was in a certain city an informer who greatly tormented the people of his city with his informing. Once he fell ill and died, and the preacher, of blessed memory, commanded the attendants of the burial society to inform him of the time of his funeral. They informed him, and he put on his coat and went with R. Meir Milkes of that holy community. When all the people saw that the preacher was going to escort him [in burial], literally the whole city went, men and women. The son of the informer rejoiced greatly that the preacher had done him [his father] honor and went himself too to escort him. The preacher went up to the cemetery, and when they came with the dead and laid him by the [burial] tent as is customary, the preacher drew near and stood by the dead to say a eulogy over him. And the son of the informer was greatly pleased.

Then the preacher opened his speech with a parable and said, in these words: "Once a certain lord had a dog, and it was called by the name Britan." As soon as his sons heard the opening of the parable, they understood that he would speak words of disgrace about him, and they turned back to go to the fortress and take from there the lord's servants and also idle fellows, to do the preacher contempt or to strike him. The aforementioned R. Meir trembled greatly and seized the preacher by the hem of his garment and whispered to him that the sons of the informer had gone to the fortress to inform against him. But [the preacher] answered him nothing, and kept him back four cubits behind, away from the ark, and did not interrupt his words, and concluded, saying that this dog was very esteemed by the lord, for it always caught all kinds of beasts when the lord traveled to hunt game, as is the custom among the nobles. "One day this dog died" — and the preacher pointed with his hand at this dead man — "and there was great joy among the beasts, that they were freed from the snares of this dog. And the cunning fox among the beasts said to them, 'O fools, why do you rejoice? Had he died before the other dogs learned from him to catch beasts, it would have been fitting to rejoice; but now, that the rest of the lord's dogs have already learned from him to hunt beasts, it would have been better for you that this dog still live. For when the other dogs saw that they were hunting beasts, and the dog Britan came and snatched from them in his mouth what they had already caught and brought it to the lord, and it was all called by his name — then all the dogs left off hunting, for they said, "Why should we trouble ourselves, when it is not called by our name, and the lord accords us no esteem for it?" And all the dogs left off hunting. But now that this Britan has died, all the dogs will pride themselves in hunting in order to find favor in the eyes of the lord, that he treat them well. It turns out, then, that there have been added upon you many more hunting dogs — so why do you rejoice?'" Thus the words of the parable.

And the meaning is: the dog Britan is this informer, who was esteemed by the lord [the authorities] through his informing against the people of the city. And the other dogs are the other informers who learned his way of informing from him. When the informers saw that they were not esteemed by the lord, because this informer snatched in his mouth and bore to the official all the informing by himself, then they let their hands fall from informing. But now that this dog has died, and all the informers know that it will be called by their name and they will be esteemed by the lord, there will now arise many more informers against you. When his words were finished, all the people understood that he spoke truly, and there was great weeping among all the people who stood there. Afterward the preacher turned the face of the dead to the ground and struck him with his palms on his backside and immediately turned away. He went with R. Meir to the wagon that was ready for him near the cemetery, and traveled and fled. On the way R. Meir heard that the preacher was arguing and answering the informer, though he saw no dead one. The preacher showed him that one of the wheels of the wagon was not turning because the informer was holding it back with his hand and speaking with him, and was grateful to him for the contempt he had done him; and [the informer] asked him that at least from now on he not press hard against him with such harsh words. And the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, said that there was a dog ready, called Britan, into which, immediately when [the informer] was buried, his soul would be reincarnated; and the contempt that the preacher did him in that eulogy delivered him from this punishment. And the main point I have forgotten: after the preacher concluded the meaning, the aforementioned preacher said, "This is the intent of the verse, 'And I praised the dead who have already died' — for had he died before the others learned from him, he would be praiseworthy in his death; but it is not so now, that there is no joy, 'more than the living who are yet [alive]' — meaning, because of the other informers still remaining alive."

---

## §85

I heard from the aforementioned rabbi that some time later, because of our many sins, when the generation became unrestrained, he heard the preacher, of blessed memory, say, "Would that the informer were alive today; I would have him as a club for the pious against the wicked of the generation of this time."

---

## §86

I heard from a certain man who heard from the mouth of the scribe R. Tzvi, of blessed memory, that the Baal Shem Tov made a match with the famous R. Zev Kitzes [for] an orphan boy and an orphan girl who were being raised in their houses: the orphan girl was raised in the Baal Shem Tov's house, and the orphan boy was raised in the house of the aforementioned R. Zev. And the Baal Shem Tov set two hundred gold pieces as the dowry. When the wedding arrived, and before the *bedecken* [veiling], R. Zev, of blessed memory, said to the Baal Shem Tov that he would not go with the groom to the veiling until [the Baal Shem Tov] paid the two hundred gold pieces he had set. And it was as a jest in the eyes of the Baal Shem Tov, and he said, "Do you not trust me for this sum?" He answered him, "By my faith, [I trust you] that you will not go back on your word; but I need to keep the groom with me to study with him, and 'if there is no flour there is no Torah.'"

Meanwhile R. Leib of Kreminer came to the Baal Shem Tov's house, and the Baal Shem Tov seated him with honor and commanded that they give him a flask of wine to drink, and he blessed over it, and the Baal Shem Tov answered Amen. Afterward the aforementioned R. Leib began and recounted to the Baal Shem Tov about his coming: that the commissar of the city had sent for him and shown him a letter from a certain lord, eight *parsah* distant from the holy community of Mezhibuzh, who wrote to him that the Baal Shem Tov owed him a thousand gold pieces from the redemption of captives, according to the promissory note in his hand, which he should pay him at once, immediately and without fail. And the commissar said that if he did not pay him at once, he would bring him down in iron chains to that lord, and not a word would fall to the ground, and they had sent to inform him to pay at once. When the Baal Shem Tov heard these words from his mouth, he immediately called his servant in a loud voice to harness his carriage, and said to R. Leib, "At once travel to that lord and pay the lord that sum, and hasten yourself to come to the veiling" — and he did not let him go home, and it was a wonder in his eyes, but he did not turn him from his words. He traveled at once to that lord, and on the way, at a certain inn, he had an acquaintance and friend, and showed his face to him and recounted to him the matter of his journey; but he was in great haste on his way.

It was a wonder to the innkeeper. And he traveled and came to the lord at noon, at the fixed time of their meal, and waited until the meal was finished, and came into the lord's house. The lord asked at once whether he had brought him money from the Baal Shem Tov, and he answered him yes. He received him with honor and gave him honey-water to drink, and [R. Leib] wondered in his heart in what manner the payment would be made, and said in his heart, "Perhaps the Baal Shem Tov's intention is that a good horse of [the lord's] horses should please me, and I should accept it for that sum." Meanwhile it came into his mind, and he said to the lord, "I need to speak with you a secret matter," and went with him to a certain room, and said to the lord, in these words, "I know that you are a righteous and very faithful man, and would not desire even a penny's worth of others' money; and now I wish to pay you that sum. Perhaps, according to the reckoning, that much is not owed from the captives, and you would stumble, Heaven forbid, in robbery." The lord answered him with great thanks, "I have loved you with great love for this, that you save me from robbery and from the punishment of the World to Come."

And he immediately searched out the receipts of the tax-farmers who had been redeemed and made reckonings, and took the Baal Shem Tov's promissory note and threw it onto the fire, and said to R. Leib that, on the contrary, two hundred gold pieces were owed to the Baal Shem Tov. And he gave him eleven gold coins, and R. Leib said to him, "Two more gold pieces are owed," and he added to speak with him in love and affection, because he did not show him favoritism [out of greed], and his soul was precious in his eyes, that he had saved him from robbery. And he gave him the two gold pieces, and R. Leib traveled on his way; and when he came to the aforementioned inn, he leaped from the wagon to recount these wonders. And the Baal Shem Tov was rebuking [those] in his house, saying, "That fool is tarrying on the road and recounting the tale." Afterward the aforementioned R. Leib came to the Baal Shem Tov and wished to recount the affair, but he answered, "I know; quickly give me the money, for the day has grown late to perform the veiling," and he was angry at him: why had he stopped on the road on his return? And afterward he said, "Now is the time of the wedding" — and he acted like one of the jesters [in merriment].

---

## §87

I heard from the rabbi of our community that once R. Pinchas of Korets went with the rabbi of the holy community of Rovno for the redemption of captives, for a certain relative of both of them had been seized into imprisonment. R. Pinchas said to the aforementioned rabbi, "I have heard that the Baal Shem Tov is spending the Sabbath in the holy community of Kaminka; let us travel to him — perhaps there will be salvation and we will not need to bestir ourselves with the wearisome travel of a man." They counted and concluded [agreed], and hurried to reach him before he went to the ritual bath, for after the ritual bath it is impossible to speak any word with him. Nevertheless, they found him [already] come from the ritual bath and clothed in Sabbath garments. In the study hall his men said to them, "Show yourselves, and he will surely know your need even though you do not recount it to him." And so they did, and came to the door of the study hall; and when he saw them, he waved his hand that they should go.

Afterward R. Pinchas heard that his father R. Avraham Abba had come from across the river to the holy community of Maripoli, and he went to him, for he feared lest [his father] wish to spend the Sabbath with the Baal Shem Tov — and to him the Baal Shem Tov counted for nothing, and he was always saying to his son, "Why do you travel [to him]?" And he feared lest they not accord [his father] honor in some matter, and [his father] would surely act brazenly before [the Baal Shem Tov], and he would have grief and shame, Heaven forbid. He asked him where he would spend the Sabbath, and he said, "Why should I go to the Baal Shem Tov? I will spend the Sabbath here and preach here." In the morning, when the Baal Shem Tov began "Give thanks," [the father] came to the study hall of Kaminka and stood by the holy ark, while the Baal Shem Tov was praying before the ark, and his father was praying in a loud voice. R. Pinchas feared lest, Heaven forbid, he confuse the Baal Shem Tov, and [the Baal Shem Tov] rebuke him. But the Baal Shem Tov said nothing to him until after the morning prayer. It was the Sabbath of the New Moon, and they had to take out two Torah scrolls, and the Baal Shem Tov took one scroll, and the second scroll his [R. Pinchas's] father took, and went after the Baal Shem Tov to the table. The Baal Shem Tov saw that he was holding the Torah scroll, and rebuked [him] and said to someone, "Take the scroll from his hand." But [the father] was silent and answered him nothing. R. Pinchas wondered at this, and after the prayer he went to the city across the river, to the head of the rabbinical court, and ate there and slept a little, and went to the synagogue to preach.

At the time of the third meal he came to the Baal Shem Tov. Now the custom of the people of Kaminka, when the Baal Shem Tov was with them, was to set up a table for them at the side, and the guests sat with the Baal Shem Tov at the table in the [main] room. When they saw [R. Avraham] come, they called him to them, for they recognized that he was learned and strong-minded, lest he confuse the Baal Shem Tov or interject into his words. And they called him, "R. Avraham, come and sit with us," and gave him flasks of honey-water. When the Baal Shem Tov began to say Torah, [R. Avraham] went out from his place and stood opposite the Baal Shem Tov in the middle of the room and gazed at his face. R. Pinchas feared to tell him not to look at the Baal Shem Tov's face, out of [the duty of] honoring one's father, and [his father] stood opposite the Baal Shem Tov, and R. Pinchas was in distress. When the Baal Shem Tov ceased saying Torah, [R. Avraham] returned to his place. Afterward they kindled candles and ate meat, and the Baal Shem Tov resumed and said, "We must say one more thing; and it was not in my mind to say that thing, except that I heard this thing in the Garden of Eden in my name." And he began to say it, and the aforementioned R. Avraham went out a second time from his place and stood opposite the Baal Shem Tov until he finished, and returned to his place. They recited the grace after meals and prayed the evening prayer. After the prayer [the Baal Shem Tov] said to R. Pinchas, "It is he and no other, it is he and no other." R. Pinchas said, "What did you see in him?" He said to him, "You suppose that I kept silent toward him when he rebuked [me] during the reading of the Torah; but I waited until the end. And I will tell you, my son: I saw this night a dream, that I entered the Garden of Eden, and the Prince of the Torah came and said a word of Torah in the name of the Baal Shem Tov; and when I woke from my sleep I remembered it well, and I came here for this thing — and just as I heard, so I heard from his holy mouth." And concerning the captive I did not hear from the aforementioned rabbi, and I forgot to ask him.

---

## §88

I heard that there was a certain priest in the holy community of Mezhibuzh, with the commissar, and he caused great troubles to the Jews of that city. The people of the city pressed the Baal Shem Tov to pray to drive him from the city, but he said he did not wish to provoke him, for he was a great sorcerer, and when he began with him [the priest] would sense it at once; and he occupied himself nothing with this.

Once [the priest] pressed the people of the city greatly, and they came to the Baal Shem Tov with an outcry and a great complaint. He answered them, "What shall I do with you? I will begin to occupy myself with him, but quietly and gently, so that he not sense it." And he began the prayers, and at once the priest sensed it. Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled over a certain hill and met [the priest] descending the hill, and he said that [the priest] would have a downfall — "and had it been, Heaven forbid, the reverse, it would not have been well for me." And so it was, for [men] came from the minister of the city and took from him his possessions and his money and brought him down from his position, and he was compelled to pay every single Jew in the city his claim, until he became poor and traveled from the city on one horse with a contemptible wagon.

And from R. Leib of Sinami I heard another version: that [the priest] worked witchcraft on the Baal Shem Tov. When the Baal Shem Tov traveled over the bridge by the mill, [the witchcraft] lifted up the wagon with the horses; and the Baal Shem Tov stopped the wagon and set up the [supports?] and laid down the boards, and the men got out and hired gentiles, and they took out the horses with the wagon. Then the Baal Shem Tov said, "Since he knows [witchcraft], he will surely have a bad end." And this R. Leib was a learned and distinguished man, and was the brother of R. David the scribe of Anipoli.

---

## §89

I also heard from him, and afterward I heard from the rabbi of our community, that once a shopkeeper came to the city of Sdeh Lavan from Breslau with a large wagon [*bricke*] of merchandise — cloth — and he came at night and did not wish to open the shop at night. He commanded that the horses be removed from the wagon, and said in his heart, "Who can steal a wagon — that is, such a large *bricke*?" And he left the *bricke* with the merchandise outside. Afterward a thief came and stole [it], and harnessed other horses to that wagon and fled with the wagon and all the merchandise. In the morning they saw that the wagon with the merchandise had been stolen, and they began to run and search in all the streets and at the crossroads, but did not find it and did not overtake the thief. The merchant sent a letter to the Baal Shem Tov; and when the messenger came to the Baal Shem Tov, he found him standing by the mezuzah and about to travel to the holy community of Drozne for a circumcision, for they had honored him with the commandment of circumcision. He received the letter from the messenger and said to him, "Wait until I return."

The messenger waited, and the Baal Shem Tov lodged on the road, and when it was morning he traveled to the city. When he came near the city, the Baal Shem Tov saw from afar that the wagon with the merchandise was also coming to the city by another road. He said to R. Tzvi the scribe, "See and look at the wagon traveling with two horses." And he said, "I see [it]." And he said, "The messenger who was sent to me came concerning this very wagon with the merchandise in it, which was stolen. When we come to the city, ask for his lodging, and tell him to give you the wagon with the merchandise, for it is stolen [property] with you [the thief]." When they came to the city, R. Tzvi the scribe immediately went to his [the thief's] lodging and saw him standing and praying, and he could not be brazen and tell him as above, for he was praying like an innocent, upright man. The scribe came to the Baal Shem Tov and reported to him as above, and he said to him, "Go at once, for he will travel at once with the merchandise and the wagon with the merchandise will be lost." The scribe went a second time and found him eating his morning meal. He asked the householder whether [the thief] had drunk much brandy in the manner of thieves, and he said that he had drunk only for two *gedolim*, in the manner of all the world. And he went from him again empty-handed to [the Baal Shem Tov]. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Go and tell him all the signs — that he stayed in the forest three days until all the searching runners had passed, and afterward lodged in such-and-such a place," and tell him all the lodgings he lodged in until the city of Drozne. The scribe went and told him all the signs; then the thief's hands grew slack, and he said, "It is true, so it is; take the wagon with the merchandise." And [the Baal Shem Tov] commanded the householder to guard the wagon with the merchandise.

When they came to eat at the circumcision, the thief said, "Now I am poor like the rest of the poor; I will go and eat at that circumcision." When they ate and drank honey-water, the thief stood by the Baal Shem Tov and said, "I have a great difficulty regarding your honor: since your honor knows and sees how thieves steal, and their place and their lodging, presumably you could see better things than these — why do you look at lowly things? It would be better to look at good things." And the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, said Torah on this difficulty until the afternoon prayer, and did not finish that Torah.

---

## §90

I heard from the rabbi of our community that at the time of the Baal Shem Tov's funeral, R. Nachman of Horodenka greatly wondered why it was that he saw nothing [no visions]. And when he came from the cemetery, he saw great awesome things, and said to R. Zev Kitzes that surely it had to be so.

---

## §91

I heard from the rabbi of our community: When R. Nachman wished to travel for the redemption of captives to the Holy Land, he went to the grave of the Baal Shem Tov with R. Yosef of Kaminka. When he returned from the cemetery he rejoiced with great joy and said, "The Baal Shem Tov commanded me to travel to the Holy Land." R. Yosef greatly wondered and said, "And where did he speak with you?" R. Nachman wondered at him and said, "Did you not see that he spoke with me and was standing by me?" On his journey he spent the Sabbath with the preacher, and I, the writer, was also there, and I heard that there were sharp [witty] words between R. Nachman and the preacher. For R. Nachman spoke sharp words to the preacher and laughed, and the preacher, of blessed memory, was offended at R. Nachman and said, "A person cannot be saved from an alien thought. Even if he prays without an alien thought, near the conclusion of the prayer a thought falls upon him that he is finishing his prayer — and this is an alien thought, and a very subtle one. And from every transgression an accuser is created, God spare us; and because it is a very subtle thought, that thought clothes itself in a Torah scholar and torments him." And all the people of the city escorted him [R. Nachman], and I was also among them, and also the rabbi and the preacher. The preacher wished to travel up to the city of Ladyzhin to escort him, but because the preacher had been offended — how is it the proper way to leave him? — he did not travel. They prayed the afternoon prayer in the field, and the rabbi put on tefillin and prayed before the ark, while R. Nachman prayed without prayer shawl and tefillin; and I did not see whether the preacher prayed there, and it seems to me that he went to the city to pray.

The next year R. Nachman, of blessed memory, died, in the month of Tammuz; and he was in Nemirov also in that month, and R. Mendel of Premishlan attached himself to him and received from him, and greatly wondered at the wisdom of the Baal Shem Tov. And I heard that R. Nachman died on the Sabbath, and R. Mendel sat with him at night and made Kiddush over wine and drank the [required] measure and sat in Sabbath garments. R. Mendel went to eat the Sabbath meal, and at the time of eating the turnip [dish] [R. Nachman] sent for him, and he found him expiring, and he studied Zohar at the time of the soul's departure; and he died at the afternoon prayer and went forth to his rest.

---

## §92

I heard that the rabbi greatly praised the prayer of R. Mendel, who came from the holy community of Tzikunaunki to take leave of the rabbi before his journey, and spent the Sabbath there and was delayed there about five days. For he came on Wednesday at the time of prayer, when the rabbi was praying the afternoon prayer, and R. Mendel's wagon with the horses stood [there], and nothing had been unloaded from the wagon. I was a teacher to a householder, and it was a long way, and I tarried in coming; and when I came and asked his men why they were not unloading the wagon, they answered that he did not wish to lodge with the rabbi, because the rabbi must pray before the ark, and he too wishes to pray before the ark; therefore he wishes that they give him a lodging near the rabbi, so that he can see the rabbi. And they did not let me pray, for he was still smoking the pipe; and also, in the house where they were praying, they would not let anyone in, for the rabbi had not yet finished his prayer, and whoever tarried they did not let pray, so that there would be a quorum for R. Mendel.

When the rabbi finished his prayer, he went out with his quorum from the house, and R. Mendel entered with us to pray before the ark, and he stood in another place as is proper [for a guest], and prayed the afternoon prayer. After he finished his prayer he sat near his place and wound up the tefillin, and the rabbi entered and greeted him. He asked him why they were not unloading from the wagon, and [R. Mendel] stammered in his answer, for he had not spoken for twelve years except by writing; and when he spoke, he would seize the word and settle himself before he brought the word out of his mouth — and especially since this was not the proper way to give such an answer. And he stammered, and no answer was heard from him, until I could not bear it; though I was very ashamed before the rabbi, nevertheless I was compelled to say, on account of the prayer, that your honor must pray before the ark the afternoon prayer, and on the Sabbath, and he too must pray. The rabbi answered, "For this there is no hindrance; in a place where there are no men, strive to be a man." After this he commanded that the belongings be brought in from the wagon, and gave him his house of seclusion, while he himself sat in the sukkah; and it was still a cold time, and [R. Mendel] came on Wednesday and traveled on Monday. The rabbi sat in the cold sukkah and gave him the small house where they prayed, and he lodged there, and the rabbi, of blessed memory, never slept there.

When he traveled, R. David of the holy community of Linits came, for he was planning to travel to the Holy Land and first wished to see R. Mendel, but he did not find him in the holy community of Nemirov, and the rabbi [R. David] lodged with the rabbi. And I heard that the rabbi greatly praised the prayer of R. Mendel and said, in these words, "I too became like one of the common people and forgot the way of prayer, and they reminded me of the way of prayer" — and he said it in the plural, of him and of R. Fridel. For R. Fridel had prayed with the rabbi before R. Mendel's coming, on Monday morning, before the ark, and he said, in these words, "When they say 'Blessed is He, blessed is [His Name]' — did you see R. Fridel of Brod, the householder, how his prayer goes?" And I also heard that once he let R. Pinchas of Korets pray the Sabbath prayer in the holy community of Polonnoye; and the rabbi of our community, when I recounted to him the tale, greatly wondered and said, "I heard from him that he used to say, 'I will forever regret that I left them the prayer.'" And regarding R. Mendel, who was at that time thirty-six years old, and the rabbi was close to sixty years, and perhaps more — see how the early righteous ones conducted themselves with such great submission.

---

## §93

I heard that R. Mendel complained about the rabbi over the honor they accorded him, for I heard that in the holy community of Konstantin Yashan the whole city went out to meet him, and he said to the rabbi that the honor was like a heavy burden upon him. The rabbi answered him that the Baal Shem Tov was always praying that they should speak disgrace about him, and R. Mendel said that he too had thought of such a counsel.

---

## §94

Once I heard from R. Nachman of Horodenka, who said, "When I was a great pious one, I went every day to a cold ritual bath such as no one in this generation could bear; and when I came home and found the ritual bath very hot, so that the walls almost burned, I did not feel the heat for about an hour; and even so, I could not free myself from alien thoughts, until I had to resort to the wisdom of the Baal Shem Tov."

---

## §95

I heard from the mouth of R. Mendel of Bar, who said, "In the days of my dwelling [in seclusion], at the time of inflammation during prayer, it came into my mind: how did my heart fill me to pray before the Blessed Name, [I who am] full of transgressions? — and my heart broke within me. And this went on a long time, and I could not free myself from this thought, for it seemed to me that it was a good thought. But afterward I settled it in my heart: why does this thought not fall upon me in my forehead and my heart when I sit to eat by the bowl? Then I thrust away this thought."

---

## §96

I heard in the rabbi's house in the holy community of Nemirov from the mouth of the aforementioned R. Moshe, who heard from the preacher [of Polonnoye]: a tale of how the preacher dreamed that they taught him a sermon, and he remembered some of the words, and recounted it to the Baal Shem Tov. He said to him, "Tell me some of the words you remembered," and the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Preach at first in my house from the book," and afterward he knew how to preach well. "And the Baal Shem Tov commanded me to travel to the holy community of Lemberg, as he had commanded me in the dream. And the Baal Shem Tov commanded me to preach specifically in the Great Synagogue and to lodge in the house of the head of the rabbinical court." "And I set my way to travel, and I preached in all the places.

"Once I preached in a certain city, and my Torah flowed like an ever-strengthening spring, and there was there a certain magnate from the holy community of Lemberg, and he heard the sermon and it pleased him greatly. And I forgot this sermon and traveled to another city, and in the street the youths mocked me, saying, 'A sermon, a sermon!' Meanwhile that magnate came and rebuked them and said, 'I heard that he is a great preacher,' and they left him be.

"When I came to the holy community of Lemberg, before the house of the head of court, the head of court was not at home, and I wished to lodge there, but the servant of the house would not let me, saying, 'Here the custom is to lodge in the communal house, where all the preachers lodge.'" But [the preacher] pressed himself, wishing specifically to lodge with the head of court. Meanwhile the head of court came and saw that he was pressing to lodge specifically in his house. He said, "Here it is not the custom to lodge with the head of court; but since you specifically wish [it], I will give you a place." And he gave him a small house among his rooms, and he placed his belongings there and sat there. The day was still long, and he took a book of Psalms and recited Psalms, while the householder was occupied with his affairs in his house. When he heard from him the recitation of the Psalms, it pleased him greatly and he found favor in his eyes, and he came in to him. He asked him in which synagogue he wished to preach, and he said, "In the Great Synagogue." He answered him, "Here the custom is not to preach so, for at first one preaches in the small house of study [klaus], and afterward in the great study hall; and when it pleases the people of the city, then he preaches in the Great Synagogue." But [the preacher] said, "No, for I wish to preach specifically in the Great Synagogue first." And the head of court said, "First you must obtain permission from the rabbi of our community."

Meanwhile the magnate who had heard him in the other city came and told the head of court that he was a great preacher. He said, "I will appear before the rabbi to let you preach in the synagogue." Now the preacher had no garments of honor, only a single overcoat, and the head of court asked him, "In what garments do you wish to go to the synagogue to preach?" And he said, "I have only this garment, this overcoat; if you wish to lend me garments, I will wear them." At once the head of court brought out from his chest his own jacket and woolen coat. [The preacher] said, "I do not wear woolen garments," and he brought him silk garments, and he put them on. The head of court went to the rabbi and asked permission for the preacher. The rabbi said, "Let him preach, and I too will go to hear the sermon." And he asked the beadle to inform him when [the preacher] went to preach; and at once, when the preacher went to preach, the beadle informed the rabbi, and the rabbi ran to the synagogue, and the sermon pleased them greatly.

After the evening prayer the rabbi asked the preacher to be his guest. He said, "I am not at my own disposal." The rabbi went to the place of the head of court and asked him [to allow] that [the preacher] be his guest, and [the head of court] could not be brazen before the rabbi, and let him have the guest. The rabbi asked him, "Are you a disciple of the Baal Shem Tov, and is your name R. Leib?" He said, "Yes." And he asked him about the form of the Baal Shem Tov, and described his form in his words, and [the preacher] said yes. The preacher asked the rabbi, "From where do you know his form?" The rabbi said, "I lay down to sleep a little before the afternoon prayer, and I saw the Baal Shem Tov, and he said, 'Let my disciple preach in the Great Synagogue.' And he said that his uncle was the great one of the generation — and it seems to me that his name was R. Avraham 'the interest' [*dem ribbit*], for I have forgotten his name — and I was with him at the time of his death, and I heard that he commanded his son, saying, 'A man will arise and his name will be Israel son of the midwife. Know that he is true and his Torah is true, and hold fast to him.'" And the rabbi held [hosted] the preacher three weeks and asked him to reveal to him all that he had received from the Baal Shem Tov. And the rabbi wished to travel to the Baal Shem Tov, and died within [a few] days.

And they gave him there thirty gold coins, and had he wished to accept more they would have given him two hundred gold coins. And they made him in the aforementioned holy community of Lemberg white Sabbath garments. On his return he came to the Baal Shem Tov and spent the Sabbath with him; and at night, when they came from the prayer, the Baal Shem Tov stood by the table and said "Peace be upon you," and the preacher said "Peace be upon you" while walking about the house to and fro. The preacher saw that the Baal Shem Tov turned back toward the door and moved his hand, and it came into his mind that the Baal Shem Tov was acting before him as though he saw something — for the preacher had fallen into pride [self-importance]. Thus the Baal Shem Tov turned two or three times, and the preacher was afraid to ask him. On the night after the Sabbath, after Havdalah, it was his custom to lie down and rest and smoke a pipe and recount what he had seen on the Sabbath. He recounted as he went, and the preacher asked, "What is this that I saw, that your honor turned, while saying 'Peace be upon you,' two or three times?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "The *samech-mem* wished to snatch you from my house, and I rebuked him and prayed for you while saying the Amidah. And when he came a second time, I said to him, 'Let him have a punishment, but do not take him from my hand.'"

And so it was: on his journey home he fell ill, he and his household, and the Baal Shem Tov had great trouble until he and his household recovered. And he forgot all the sermons, and the preacher said that he had many adventures and much labor until he could preach again. The main part of the tale I heard from the mouth of R. Moshe, husband of the sister of the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, who heard it from the mouth of the preacher himself in the holy community of Nemirov. And from R. Yosef son of R. Moshe of the holy community of Polonnoye I also heard several versions of this tale.

But once R. David, preacher of the holy community of Tultshin — who was a grandson of the righteous R. David Forkes — spent the Sabbath with the rabbi of our community, and recounted to me a tale: that a certain man brought his mad son to the Baal Shem Tov, and when he reached one *parsah* near the city the madman grew strong and made himself as dead. His father brought him to the city to bury him and laid him in the women's synagogue. The Baal Shem Tov said to the scribe, "We must today strike a certain dead one." And when they brought the dead one, he said to the scribe, "Take a thick shoe-strap and go to the synagogue and drive out the burial society from there," and commanded that they strike him. And he struck him, but he did not rise and did not move; and the Baal Shem Tov commanded that they place him in a certain house and shut the house and guard him until the festival of Passover. And it was three weeks until Passover, and on the night of Passover he commanded that they seat him at the table and prop him with cushions until he could sit, and station a man over him to put into his mouth whatever they were doing, both food and drink, and guard him so that he not flee — "for when he flees it will be hard to find him." At the third cup he could not bear it, and he leaped and bounded from the house and fled. And they sought him for a [whole] day and night until they found him, and [the Baal Shem Tov] healed him.

---

## §97

The tale of the blood libel of the holy community of Pavoloch: the rabbi of the holy community of Krushtisav, called by his name R. David, fled, and wished to flee as far as the lands of Wallachia, for all the esteemed men of their surroundings were afraid lest [the libel] be brought against them too. When he came to the holy community of Mezhibuzh, the Baal Shem Tov detained him, and several times the Baal Shem Tov said that they [the captives] would be saved. But afterward, when they were killed, a letter came to the aforementioned R. David [telling] how they had inflicted harsh and bitter tortures on each and every one; and he came with the letter to the Baal Shem Tov and was greatly grieved.

This day was the Sabbath eve, and [the Baal Shem Tov] went to the ritual bath and wept greatly, and prayed the afternoon prayer with great bitterness, until his treasured men could not lift their heads, and they said, "Perhaps when he begins to receive the Sabbath he will receive it amid joy." But he prayed the receiving of the Sabbath and the evening prayer also with bitterness of heart, and made Kiddush over the wine in weeping, and washed his hands and sat at the table, and went to the room to sleep there. He stretched himself on the ground and lay until very late, he and his household; and the guests waited until the candles began to go out. His wife went and said to him, "The candles will soon go out, and the guests are waiting." He said to her, "Let them eat and recite the grace after meals and go." And his wife too went to the room where the Baal Shem Tov was and lay on her bed. The Baal Shem Tov was still lying on the ground with hands and feet outstretched, and the aforementioned R. David stood by the door to see what would be the end. He grew weary of standing and took a bench and sat by the door. At about midnight he heard [the Baal Shem Tov] say to his wife, "Cover your face." And in a moment the room lit up, and it shone through the cracks, and he said, "Welcome, R. Akiva," and also to the other holy ones [martyrs] whose names he did not know he said, "Welcome." And R. David recognized R. Akiva by his voice. The Baal Shem Tov said to the holy ones, "I decree upon you that you go to take vengeance on the oppressor, the accuser, called the *agitator*." The holy ones answered and said, "Let not this thing go forth from your mouth again, and what you said is annulled, for your honor does not know your [own] power. For when your honor confused the Sabbath, there was a great commotion in the Garden of Eden, and they were fleeing from all the palaces as from before a sword; and when we came to a palace higher than this, there too they fled, and they did not know why, until we came to a lofty place where they know [the secrets]. They cried out at us, 'Hurry and go to still the tears of the Baal Shem Tov.' And we will tell your honor that all the sufferings a man has in life are not like the husks of garlic compared to the sufferings we endured for the sanctification of His Name, blessed and exalted be He. And the evil inclination does its work, and confused our thoughts by a hairsbreadth, even though we thrust it away with both hands. Even so we were compelled to suffer half an hour of Gehenna.

"And afterward we entered the Garden of Eden and began to plead that we wished to take vengeance on our enemies; and they answered us, 'His hour still stands [he still has his time]; and if you wish to take vengeance, you must accept upon yourselves reincarnation.' And we said, 'Thank God that we gave ourselves over for the sanctification of His Name and were compelled to suffer the pain of Gehenna' — for all the sufferings are like the husks of garlic compared to that half-hour of Gehenna — 'and what will be if we are reincarnated again in this world, and perhaps we will be spoiled, Heaven forbid, and have no remedy? Therefore it is better for us not to take vengeance and not to be reincarnated. Therefore our request from your honor is that he annul the thing he said.'" And the Baal Shem Tov asked them, "Why did they mislead me from heaven and not reveal to me that you must be holy ones [martyrs]?" And they answered him, "Had you known of this, then you would have increased in prayer and annulled the decree, and there would have been, Heaven forbid, a greater calamity than this; therefore they did not inform your honor." And from the rabbi of our community I heard that the Baal Shem Tov said that they had promised him from heaven that they would annul the decree, except that a certain preacher preached in the holy community of Brod and made himself a livelihood from the captives and stirred up strife, and caused the captives to be killed, because of our many sins.

---

## §98

I heard from R. Aharon of our community, who heard from the great rabbi of the holy community of Siptivka, that there was in their surroundings a certain deserted wife [agunah] who held a lease. The Baal Shem Tov saw that once she committed adultery with her Canaanite [gentile] slave. She had two brothers in two villages, and the matter was revealed to the brothers, and they had shame, and they thought that she would go out, Heaven forbid, from the fold. They devised a plan that each one would invite her to his village, and toward evening the two of them would go and remove her from the world, and so forth. That night the Baal Shem Tov sat with two candles burning before him, and suddenly one candle went out, and he lit it from the second candle, and in a moment the second candle went out, and he lit it as above. And he heard a voice saying to him, "Murderer, how do you come to [tend] two candles!" And he understood the matter. He took a horse and rode upon it in haste and reached there, and they had not yet killed her, and he saved her from their hands. And she became a completely repentant woman, and afterward they found her standing in the ritual bath at midnight.

---

## §99

A tale of a certain shopkeeper in the holy community of Mezhibuzh, called a *cloth-cutter*, whose son traveled to Breslau and tarried greatly. His mother was greatly grieved and said to her husband that he should go to the Baal Shem Tov to ask about him, where he was. The Baal Shem Tov was not esteemed in the eyes of her husband, and he did not wish to go, but his wife provoked him until he had to go. He came to the Baal Shem Tov and laughed and said, "My wife said that you know certain things; therefore I have come that you tell me where my son is." [The Baal Shem Tov] answered him, "For such things I look in the synagogue," and he took the pipe and went to the synagogue. He came and said, "I saw him, that he is still in Breslau and goes about in a German hat." For such was the custom there: when the commerce is concluded, they exchange their hats, and this is a sign of the conclusion of the commerce. And that man made a sign for himself to remember on what day the transaction was. When his son came from the road, his father asked him on what day the commerce was concluded, and he told him on that day as he had marked, and that they had exchanged their hats. He asked him why he had not sent him a letter by the post from time to time. He told him, "Because a good buyer happened upon me on the road, and I sold him the merchandise, and I had to set up the merchandise at the merchant's house, and I turned aside from the road on which the post goes, and bought other merchandise there and went to Breslau, and through this I was late in coming home."

Once the shopkeeper wished to travel to the holy community of Sdeh Lavan with merchandise, and he feared, for they said there were reckless men on that road. His wife said to him, "I have heard that the Baal Shem Tov is traveling; therefore give his servant some gift, that he inform you on what day the Baal Shem Tov travels, and you will travel with him together, and you will have no fear," and so forth. And he traveled with the Baal Shem Tov together. When they came to that forest where the reckless men were, the Baal Shem Tov commanded his servant to stop and graze the horses. And this man was greatly frightened, and the Baal Shem Tov commanded that the wagons stand opposite each other, and the Baal Shem Tov sat in the middle and opened the book of the Zohar and gazed at it. The reckless men came out of the forest, and when they drew near them, trembling seized them, and the reckless men fled. When they were far from them, the reckless men said, "Let us go upon them once more," and when they drew near, trembling seized them again. And when they had withdrawn, the reckless men did not wish to return upon them again, and said, "This is no simple matter." When the merchant saw this affair, he fell on the Baal Shem Tov's neck and kissed him and said, "Now I know your honor's greatness." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "You suppose that you know me — you do not yet know me." And this tale does not come to my memory from whom I heard it, and it seems to me that I heard it from the rabbi of our community.

---

## §100

Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to Kaminka, and near the city he heard a proclamation to remove the slaughterer of that holy community. He said, "I will remove him and provide him some livelihood," for he was very careful not to deprive any person of his livelihood. When he came to the house of R. Baruch, R. Baruch had prepared himself and slaughtered several animals, and one animal was found unfit [*tereifah*] and was hung in the vestibule. The Baal Shem Tov was smoking the pipe in the vestibule, and R. Baruch came into the vestibule, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him to cut a piece of meat from this animal and roast it for him. R. Baruch supposed that he said so in jest, and said, "Your honor will not eat from this animal." And he said, "I will surely eat from it." When [R. Baruch] saw that he spoke in earnest, he said, "This animal is unfit." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "But this animal asks of me that I eat from it; pray tell me why it was declared unfit." And he sent for the slaughterer and asked him why this animal was declared unfit, and he said, "There are opinions among the codifiers — some declare [such a case] fit and some unfit." And [the Baal Shem Tov] commanded that they cut a piece of meat and make a roast from it. The Baal Shem Tov saw that the matter was difficult in the eyes of his men, and he said that they should send to the holy community of Polonnoye, to R. Shmuel the judge, and bring from him a letter before it was roasted. At once they sent a messenger riding on a good horse, and he came with a letter in his hand from the judge that it was fit [kosher]. And [the Baal Shem Tov] removed the slaughterer and did not wish to provide him a livelihood.

---

## §101

R. Nachman of Horodenka had a trait that over everything he saw or that befell him, he would say, "It is good and for the good," and his faith was strong as a pillar of iron. Once soldiers were quartered in the holy community of Mezhibuzh upon the Jews. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Pray that the soldiers not be quartered upon the Jews," and he said to him that this was for the good. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "How fine that you were not in the generation of Haman, for you would have said of the decree that this too is for the good — and you would have had no virtue except that they hanged Haman, which is also good."

Once R. Nachman traveled with the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye from the holy community of Nemirov to the Baal Shem Tov for the Sabbath, and they traveled on Friday and met a certain lord in a large carriage, who was also traveling on that road, and he was traveling slowly. And they were afraid to pass him, and the rabbi wrung his hands and said, "Woe, woe, for we are desecrating the Sabbath, Heaven forbid," and he was grieved with great grief. And R. Nachman said that it was for the good — and this added to his grief. And after a short time they met a great camp going with loads, and the snow was very deep, and they could in no way pass them, for they too were going on this road, and not toward them. When the gentiles with the loads saw the lord, they all turned aside from the road and let the lord's camp pass; and afterward they came to a crossroads, and the lord traveled on his way, and they arrived while the day was still long. All this I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye.

---

## §102

And from the rabbi of our community I heard that once R. Nachman traveled with the Baal Shem Tov, and they came to a certain village, and it was the time of the afternoon prayer. The Baal Shem Tov said to the beadle, "Go to the house and see whether it is possible to pray there." And R. Nachman took the bag of prayer shawl and tefillin and went to the house. The beadle said to the Baal Shem Tov, "The house is full of gentiles." The Baal Shem Tov said, "Go and call R. Nachman to sit on the wagon, for the time of the afternoon prayer is growing late." The beadle entered and found him wrapped in prayer shawl and tefillin and praying, and the beadle told him to come out, but he did not hear. The beadle went out and told the Baal Shem Tov that he was wrapped in prayer shawl and tefillin and praying. The Baal Shem Tov said, "Surely he will cause some trouble for the gentiles." And meanwhile the fortress was set afire, and reckless men came and drove out all the gentiles to save the fortress, and the house was left empty; then the Baal Shem Tov too went to pray.

---

## §103

I saw that the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye would handle his tefillin before the Amidah, and after the Amidah, when he finished the blessing "who blesses His people with peace," he immediately handled the tefillin; and I wrote this to learn from him diligence in the service of the Creator. And during the Amidah aloud he always gazed in the prayer book and listened to the prayer leader.

---

## §104

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye that once he traveled to the minister of the holy community of Rashkov for some need, and prayed on the road; and when he reached the prayer "And He is merciful" on Monday or Thursday, he clothed his need [in the prayer] as though the Divine Presence were praying [presenting] this very need before the God of Israel. And after the prayer he returned home and said, in his great humility, "I would not have said this thing, except that I know that now I am not on this level."

---

## §105

I heard from the rabbi of our community, who heard from R. Yosef of Kaminka, that the Baal Shem Tov hoped to ascend in a storm-wind like Elijah, of blessed memory, and he was grieved over this with great grief. The household supposed that he was grieved over the passing of his wife, and they asked him, for they knew that this was not his nature. He answered that he was grieved over those higher faculties [*mochin*] that must rest in the earth, "for I hoped to ascend in a storm-wind, and now that I am half a body it is impossible — and over this I grieve."

---

## §106

I also heard [him say]: "If the righteous Redeemer does not come within sixty years, I must come [again] into this world."

---

## §107

And the rabbi of our community said that he heard from his grandfather, who spent one Sabbath with the Baal Shem Tov before the Shavuot on which he passed away, and [the Baal Shem Tov] said a thing — and he had a certain scribe — and the rabbi said that he had two doubts: he does not know whether he said "in fifteen years" or "in fifty years," and further, whether "in sixteen" or "in sixty." And I heard from others explicitly "sixty years." And he asked R. Aharon of Mezhibuzh, and he told me that he said, "Surely I will be reincarnated in this world, but I will not be as I am now." And the meaning is not known, for he said, "Who is this who will ask the meaning of that thing?"

---

## §108

I heard from the rabbi that they were always finding an error in a certain Torah scroll, and they would correct it each time, and nevertheless they found an error in it. They showed the scroll to the Baal Shem Tov, and he said that it had been given to be written from money called *shalder geld* — that is, [money from] playing cards, where they give the householder rent each time, one coin; and the householder gathered this money and wrote this Torah scroll with that money. And so it was. And the Baal Shem Tov said that it had no remedy and would forever be unfit, even if they corrected it many times.

---

## §109

And a tale like this I heard from R. Elimelech: that once a goldsmith came to his neighbor, and he saw a certain ring and took it in his hand, and immediately threw it down and said that this ring was taken from money of interest [usury]; and the goldsmith investigated, and so it was.

---

## §110

A tale: there was a certain man in the holy community of Polonnoye who did good deeds for the Baal Shem Tov. He gave him flour and fish on credit, and when he came near death he asked the Baal Shem Tov to watch over his only son R. Mordechai, called R. Motel. And [the son] was always rising and falling [in fortune]. Once he descended from his property and nothing remained to him but his house. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "When they send for you from the [noble's] court to rent the town's lease, do not go, even several times, until they send a wagon for you." And so it was: several men came to rent the lease and [the lady] did not come to terms with them. The lady sent for him, but he did not wish to go, for he said that he was not in good health and could not go on his feet. And so it was, that he had some ailment, and likewise on the second day and the third day, until she sent a wagon for him. He said that it was not in his power to hold the lease, for he had nothing with which to distill brandy, and she said, "I will give you several hundred [measures of] grain," and he rented the lease, and the lady was filled with joy that she had rented him the lease. And he profited from this about twelve hundred gold coins. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Do not trade in oxen," but he did not heed him, and traded in oxen, and came from the road with an empty purse; and so it was several times.

Once he descended greatly [in fortune] and was greatly in debt to a certain lord, about thirteen hundred gold pieces, and he had nothing with which to pay. The lord said, "If he does not pay me, I will kill him with a firearm." R. Motel heard and was afraid and traveled to the Baal Shem Tov and sat there all the Days of Awe. [The Baal Shem Tov] demanded of him [payment of his own debt], and he said, "I have no money." After the Day of Atonement, once, at the morning watch, the Baal Shem Tov said, "Come with me to the ritual bath," and he went with him; and after this he said to him, "Go home." He asked, "What will be when I go home?" He said to him, "Fear not and be not dismayed." And he traveled home and celebrated all the festival [of Sukkot]. After the festival it was a rainy time, and out of worry he smoked the pipe. He looked through the window and saw a camp of gentiles, called Haidamaks, who had returned from the road; and in that city was always their accustomed route. They went through the whole city to obtain lodging, but did not obtain it, for they were about forty or fifty riders. When they came to his house he received them and gave them lodging and gave them food and drink and whatever their hearts desired. And he bought from his neighbor two quarts of brandy and sold to them, and bought more brandy and profited from them greatly, until he was able to buy a whole *liter* of brandy. And they ate and drank as they wished and pleased; and when they traveled on their way, they asked him to bring after them barrels of honey-mead, for they had a desire to drink all the barrels of mead, and they returned to the city and lodged with him. This happened several times, until he had filled up for himself the sum of the debt, and there still remained, to fill the debt completely, the sum of two gold pieces. And one of the riders slipped away [stayed behind] and came to his house and [paid?] him the two gold pieces. Afterward the lord came riding on a horse and called him to come out to him, and asked him for the money, and he said, "I have money," and brought the lord into his house and paid him the whole sum owed to him. And the lord said to him, "They told me about you that you have nothing with which to pay, and now I see that you have paid me, and the matter was false. Therefore take the money [back] for some time, as long as you wish." But he did not wish to take the money from him, for he said, "Not every day does a miracle occur." And the Baal Shem Tov said, "How fortunate is this lord — had you no money, this lord would have had to die."

---

## §111

I heard from the mouth of the rabbi of the holy community of Nemirov and of the holy community of Polonnoye. It is written in the *Zohar Chadash*, in these words: "All those Tannaim and Amoraim — the whole world stands on their account; for when they stood together, one would say to his fellow, 'From within this teaching that flew forth from my mouth, I see what will be this day or tomorrow.'" And I heard from the Baal Shem Tov that when he heard someone studying, he knew what would be at the end of the year. For once I came to the holy community of Brod and did not find R. Gershon at home, for he had traveled on the road. Meanwhile R. Gershon came home, and his son said to his mother, "I bring you news: that they took the lease-holder in iron chains." R. Gershon said to him, "My brother-in-law Israel said last year that this would be so. For I was studying Mishnah after the prayer, and recited a certain Mishnah, and the Baal Shem Tov said to me, 'Do you know what you recited?' and I said, 'I know.' And I supposed that he meant [a difficulty] against the plain sense of the Mishnah, but he said to me, 'You have studied two new things: you have two enemies here — one will make peace with you, and the second will have a great downfall.'" And so it was: R. Gershon went to greet the magnates on the festival, and went before the house of one of the enemies, and the enemy sent his son-in-law and asked him to enter his house, and appeased him.

---

## §112

A tale: when the Baal Shem Tov came to the holy community of Mezhibuzh, he was not esteemed in the eyes of the pious ones, namely R. Zev Kitzes and R. David Forkes, because of the name they called him, "Baal Shem Tov" [master of the good Name], for this name does not befit a righteous man. And they had a worthy disciple, who fell ill — and he was a good and righteous man — and his teachers went to visit him. [The disciple] wished to call the Baal Shem Tov to him, but they did not wish it. Afterward he grew very weak, and they agreed to call the Baal Shem Tov, and they said to the sick man, "Whatever he speaks with you, inform us." When the Baal Shem Tov came to him, he commanded that everyone leave the house; but there was a certain lad who hid himself.

The Baal Shem Tov said that he would die, "but this matter you did not rectify," and he told him that thing, and [the disciple] admitted [it]. And he said, "I hoped to rectify the matter, and what shall I do now?" The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Do not worry; I will see to it that you have no hindrance in this matter, and I will bring you into the Garden of Eden; and do not reveal to anyone this thing that I speak with you." And he went from him; and when his teachers came and asked him what [the Baal Shem Tov] had spoken with him, he did not wish to reveal it. The lad answered and recounted it to them, and they asked the sick man whether the matter was true, and he said, "It is true; thus he spoke with me." And it was a great wonder in their eyes, how [the Baal Shem Tov] had knowledge above as to whom they would admit into the Garden of Eden. And they compelled the sick man to give them a handshake [pledge] that he would reveal to them how he was treated after his death. When he died he came to them and revealed to them that they had admitted him into the Garden of Eden, but he did not see the Baal Shem Tov there and did not know by whom they admitted him. But he had no place in the Garden of Eden, for when he sat in a place, another came and pushed him from his place and sat in that place; and so in another place, until they moved him from all the places. "And I am wandering and roaming and do not know on what account and why this is; and I thought in my mind that the Baal Shem Tov promised me to bring me into the Garden of Eden, and I have no resting place — and I had great grief from this.

"Once it happened that the people of my palace went to another palace, and I went with them; and when they came to that palace, I leaped and sat by the table, and they moved me from my place until I stood before the table. And I saw that the Baal Shem Tov was sitting among them and saying words of Torah, and he asked them a difficulty and commanded the people of the academy to resolve it for him, but they could not resolve it, and he resolved it himself, and each one went to his place. And I asked the Baal Shem Tov why I have no place in the Garden of Eden. He said to me, 'Because you gave a handshake and did not fulfill it.'" Immediately he came to them in a dream and recounted to them the matter and the difficulty and the resolution that the Baal Shem Tov said in the Garden of Eden. And immediately that Sabbath they came to [the Baal Shem Tov] for the third meal, and he said that Torah; and when he asked the difficulty, they said the resolution, and he said to them, "I know that the dead one told you," and from that day onward they drew near to him. And the rabbi of our community said that they did not admit him into the Garden of Eden, but only gave him a place near the gates of the Garden of Eden; but every time [the Baal Shem Tov] had an ascent of the soul, on his return he would shine upon him.

---

## §113

A tale: there was a certain teacher named R. Moshe Kedes, and he was great in Torah, and the Baal Shem Tov was not esteemed in his eyes. And his custom was to send a messenger to the study hall, and near "Barchu" he would enter to pray. Once a certain Tosafot in tractate Shevuot was difficult for him, and it was very hard for him about two weeks, and he could not arrive at the true plain sense. Once he saw in a dream that he entered a certain palace, and the Baal Shem Tov saw him and said to him, "Why do you not know the plain sense of the Tosafot?" and he told him the plain sense. He woke from his sleep and remembered the plain sense, and rose and took the Gemara by the light of the moon and looked at the Tosafot, and behold, it was very good. Even so he said in his heart, "Perhaps it is [only] a dream, and the Baal Shem Tov knows nothing of it." But the Baal Shem Tov asked him for the third meal and told him the plain sense of the Tosafot, [saying] that it was upright and good. Then he knew that the Baal Shem Tov had told it to him, and he drew near to him and became a great righteous man.

---

## §114

I heard from R. Moshe son of R. Yekel of Mezhibuzh, who was in the holy community of Nemirov; and when his father came with him to the Baal Shem Tov to bless him, the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Take care not to travel to the city of Venice." And when he traveled, he strayed on the road and was near that city, and turned back to another road. And the aforementioned R. Moshe guarded himself never to be in the city of Venice all his days. When he recounted this to me, it was not many years before his death, and he was traveling on the road, and they made a libel against him because of the passport, and they took him to the city of Venice. He pleaded much that they not lead him to Venice, but even so they led him there, and imprisoned him there, and he died in prison. And this was forty years or more after his wedding.

---

## §115

I heard from the mouth of R. Moshe the [field-worker] that the Baal Shem Tov was the circumciser at his [child's circumcision], and he commanded that they not lead him over a bridge called a *greblye* [causeway]. And he guarded this matter several years. Once they had to travel to a wedding, and they led him with them, and a swelling formed upon him.

---

## §116

I heard a tale from the rabbi of our community: the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Kaminka, and said to R. Baruch, "When the rabbi of the holy community of Ostrah comes here, tell him in my name that his father, the rabbi Chacham Tzvi, appeared to me and asked me to tell his son not to travel to the lands of Ukraine. And if he does not believe that I saw him, tell him the sign of his father's form and all his movements and garments, and in what stockings — called *skarpetkes* — he went." When the aforementioned rabbi came to that holy community, R. Baruch told him the words of the Baal Shem Tov, and when he heard these signs he believed. And he asked them to say some words of Torah they had heard from him, and R. Yosef of Kaminka said before him what the Baal Shem Tov had said on the commentary of Rashi on the verse "When a prince sins." And I heard the explanation from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye and have forgotten it, but surely it is written in his books — see there. When [the rabbi of Ostrah] heard the explanation, it pleased him greatly, and he said, "Such an explanation befits the rabbi of the holy community of Ostrah to say," and he turned back from the road and did not travel to the land of Ukraine. But in his old age he traveled to Ukraine and died in the holy community of Chvastov.

---

## §117

A tale of a certain sick man whom a great Jewish doctor despaired of, and the sick man could no longer speak. The Baal Shem Tov came to that place, and they called him to the aforementioned sick man, and he said to cook him a broth of meat, and at once he would begin to speak. And they gave him broth to eat, and he healed him until he recovered. The doctor asked him how he had healed him: "I knew that the sinews had become spoiled, and for those sinews there can be no healing." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "You grasped the sick man in his physical aspect, and I grasped the sick man in his spiritual aspect. For there are in a man 248 limbs and 365 sinews; corresponding to them are 248 positive commandments and 365 negative commandments. When a man transgresses, Heaven forbid, the limb or sinew corresponding to it becomes spoiled; and when he transgresses many negative commandments, many sinews become spoiled, and the blood does not flow in them, and the man is in danger. And I spoke with the soul that it should accept upon itself repentance, and it accepted upon itself; and by this all the limbs and sinews were rectified, and I was able to heal him."

---

## §118

A tale: there was a certain man with the Baal Shem Tov named R. Aharon, and he served the Baal Shem Tov; and he took a wife and did not wish to be a servant [any longer], and he asked the Baal Shem Tov to accept R. Yaakov as servant. Once [R. Yaakov] traveled with the Baal Shem Tov on the road and lodged in a certain village, and they cleared for him a small house near the lease[-holder's inn], and they forgot a pair of stockings on the pole. When the Baal Shem Tov traveled from there, the servant hid the stockings; and while they were still traveling in this village, the Baal Shem Tov commanded them to stop and said to the servant, "You stole something," and he denied it. He said, "The dog calls you a thief, and you deny it?" Even so he denied it. He said to him, "The stockings — where are they?" and he admitted it. And he said to his Canaanite [gentile] slave to lead the stockings [back] to the inn. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "You yourself go and return the stolen thing," and from then on he stole nothing. And the rabbi of our community heard from the servant himself, and I heard it from him.

---

## §119

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Once a certain man wrote down the Torah of the Baal Shem Tov — what he heard from him. Once the Baal Shem Tov saw that a certain demon was walking and holding a book in his hand. He said to it, "What is this book in your hand that you carry?" It answered him, "This is the book that you composed." Then the Baal Shem Tov understood that there was a certain man writing down his Torah, and he gathered all his men and asked them, "Who among you is writing my Torah?" That man admitted it and brought him the writings, and the Baal Shem Tov examined them and said, "There is not here even one statement that I said."

---

## §120

Once he went with his son R. Tzvi, who was still a lad, to greet the rabbi of the town, and he looked and saw there many silver vessels; and on his return he said to his son, "You surely are envious — why does your father have no silver vessels?" His son said, "Yes." He said to him, "Had your father money [enough] for silver vessels, it would be good to support the poor with it, and the rest to give to charity."

---

## §121

And I heard from the rabbi that money never stayed overnight with him; but when he came from the road, he would pay debts, and the rest he scattered as charity that very day.

---

## §122

Once he brought much money from the road, and paid debts and gave charity, and asked his men to find him a commandment by which to scatter the money. Meanwhile his wife took some of the money and said, "I will need [it] for a few days, [not] to buy on credit." And at night he had a hindrance in his service, and he understood, and came to the house and said, "Admit to me — who took some of my money?" And his wife admitted to him that she had taken some of the money, and she returned it; and at night he commanded that the poor be gathered, and he distributed to them the money.

---
