## §123

I heard from the rabbi R. Gedaliah: R. Moshe, the rabbi of the holy community of Kotov, had a daughter-in-law who had the trouble of [losing] children [in infancy], and she dealt with gentile sorceresses. They worked with their witchcraft and buried under the threshold of the house a castrated rooster, and through the witchcraft a certain demon came to be in the house. Once the aforementioned R. Moshe saw that swine were walking on the ceiling at night, and he went out of the house. The preacher came and asked the aforementioned rabbi, saying, "I will enter your house to dwell in it." He answered him, "Had it been a good dwelling, I would have dwelt in it." The preacher said to him, "Even so I will dwell in it," for he had the trouble of [bad] neighbors, and he entered to dwell in it.

Once the preacher sat in his small house in prayer shawl and tefillin, and the house was open, and his child was walking in the vestibule. The preacher saw the demon going toward the child, and he rebuked it and said to it, "What is your business here?" It answered him, "And what are you doing here?" The preacher understood that he must leave the dwelling, except that it was toward evening, and he said, "Tomorrow I will leave here." And he asked three companions to lodge with him, and they sat and remained awake; and afterward the preacher wished to sleep a little, and asked of them, saying, "I will go to lie down, and you, for [Heaven's] sake, do not sleep, but study." And when he slept, they all fell asleep over the books, and the candle went out, and the demon poured upon each book a basketful of earth from the attic. When the preacher awoke and saw that it was dark, he trembled greatly and entered the [main] house, and the demon threw upon him a board from the attic; and had he not turned back a little into the alcove, he would have been injured, Heaven forbid. He stood in one place in fear until he heard the crowing of the rooster, and he took a candle and went to light the candle. And in the rabbi's house there was light [coming] through the window, and he came to [that] house through the window to light the candle. The rabbi recognized that he was greatly frightened and asked him, but he did not wish to answer until he held fire in his hand — for so it is written in *Sefer Chasidim* — and he recounted to the rabbi. And the rabbi said to him, "Did I not tell you?" He came to his house and found them sleeping, and on each book a basketful of earth, and in the morning he left the dwelling. And when the house was abandoned to ruin, the neighbors took, this one a bench and that one a board, and other [pieces of] wood, and the preacher too took [some]. And at night the demon struck the wall and the windows of each one who had taken anything from this house. On the third night the Baal Shem Tov said, "I will go with you [to your house] and [the demon] will no longer strike in the house; and I will lie down to sleep a little, and when you hear it strike at the window or in another place, say, 'Israel son of Eliezer is here,' and it will no longer strike." And so it was. Then the preacher said, "Since the name Israel son of Eliezer is [effective], one may infer from this that he is a righteous man," and then he cleaved to him firmly.

---

## §124

I heard: Once a certain guest lodged with the Baal Shem Tov and slept in the Baal Shem Tov's house, and he made a sound from his nostrils [snored], as there are some who make a sound from their nostrils during sleep. And his son R. Tzvi was a small lad and was frightened by this sound. He went to his father and recounted to him. His father said to him, "Shake the door in the manner of opening and shutting the door, and you will no longer hear a sound from him, and he will sleep peacefully," and so forth — and it helped. The second night his son went himself and performed this remedy, but it did not help. He went to his father, and his father said to him, "Yesterday at that moment they removed the officer appointed over sleep and were appointing another officer, and in the meanwhile the remedy helped; but today [the new one] was appointed, and the remedy did not help."

---

## §125

A tale in the holy community of Nemirov: there was a certain house that was impure [haunted], and they asked the Baal Shem Tov to go and look at the house. The preacher asked to go with him too. He saw a certain demon standing there, and the preacher did not see it. The preacher asked, and he said that there was [one], and asked him to let him see it too. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "But you will be frightened." He said, "No." He said to him, "Shut your eye and open it several times," and he did so, and saw it standing in the corner of the house with its face toward the wall. And he saw it like the appearance of a cloud of utter darkness, and he asked why it had turned its face away, and [the Baal Shem Tov] answered that it was ashamed before him. Afterward the preacher began to be frightened, and [the Baal Shem Tov] said to him, "Shut and open your eyes as before, and you will no longer see it," and he did so, and he saw it no more. The householder asked the Baal Shem Tov to drive it out, but he did not wish to.

---

## §126

I heard from the rabbi of our community, who heard from the pious R. Zev of Olik: Once the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath in that holy community with a householder, and the householder was the monthly warden. On the Sabbath, at the afternoon prayer, the householder went to the synagogue to hear the sermon of a certain visiting preacher, and the Baal Shem Tov waited for him at the third meal to come from the synagogue. Meanwhile he heard that the preacher was informing [speaking ill] against Israel, and the Baal Shem Tov was angry and told the beadle to go and call the householder. The beadle revealed to several people that the Baal Shem Tov was angry at the preacher, and the preacher saw that the people were slipping away one by one, and he ceased preaching. The next day the preacher came to the Baal Shem Tov and greeted him, and [the Baal Shem Tov] asked who he was, and he said, "I am the preacher; why were you angry at me?" The Baal Shem Tov leaped up from his place, and tears spurted from his eyes, and he said, "You would speak ill of the children of Israel? Know that a son of Israel goes about every day on the market day, and toward evening, when it grows dark for him, he is alarmed and says, 'Woe is me, that I will let the time of the afternoon prayer pass,' and he goes into a certain house and prays the afternoon prayer — and he does not know what he is saying — and even so the seraphim and ophanim are shaken by it."

---

## §127

A tale I heard from the mouth of R. Moshe, husband of the sister of the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, and he himself heard from the Baal Shem Tov, who recounted it in the holy community of Nemirov before villagers, in these words: "Once I saw in a dream that I was walking in a field, and I saw in the dream from afar something like a mist. And I walked until I came to the place of the mist, on one side; and the sun was shining on this side, and also on the road, while on the other side was the mist. And the place was like a long slope, and I walked until I reached the end of the valley. And I had a Canaanite [gentile] slave for several years, who had left me, and I saw him walking with a very great load of wood on his shoulders; and when he saw me, he cast the wood off himself and fell at my feet. And he said, 'When I was a slave to your honor I used to keep the Sabbath, but when I left you I served a certain tax-farmer, and he made me work on the Sabbath and commanded [me] to go to the forest to bring wood on the Sabbath. And now the two of us are dead, and I must bring wood on the Sabbath day to Gehenna until there is enough for the tax-farmer for all the days of the week. Therefore I ask of you that you wait until I return, and I will show your honor a place where your honor may ask of them that they release me from the judgment, for your honor is very esteemed in this world; and now it is not in my power, because of the officers who go after me.' He said to him, 'If I am esteemed in this world, leave the wood and come with me at once, and show me the place.' He went with me and showed me a certain palace, and the Baal Shem Tov entered it and pleaded on his behalf, and they released him from the judgment; and when I pleaded for the gentile, I [also] taught advocacy on behalf of the Jew [the tax-farmer], and released him too from the judgment."

---

## §128

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov traveled from the village Chertria and lodged near a village, by a forest where several times several Jewish men had been killed. He stopped there to lodge in order to drive the husk [shell] of murder from that place. And he said to his men, "I wish to sleep, and you, do not sleep — recite Psalms, for our Canaanite slave wishes to kill us, and do not sleep the whole night." In the morning they sent to the village to bring them water to wash. And he traveled to the village, and when they ate, he called his servant and gave him flasks of honey-water, and he drank. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Admit to me that you wished to kill us in the night," and he said, "It is so."

---

## §129

Once again, near the holy community of Nemirov, [the slave] also wished to kill me. He said to him, "True!" He said to him, "Why did you think so? Do you not know that it is impossible to outwit me?" He said, "In the manner of the gentiles — a demon bites at me [incites me]."

---

## §130

I heard from the pious rabbi R. Yechiel Michel of the holy community of Zlotchov. He said, in the city of Linits, when he was here for the wedding of the son of R. Zev, that they commanded him from heaven to accept the Baal Shem Tov as rabbi and to go to study from him; and they showed him the springs of wisdom going to him. And when the Baal Shem Tov passed away, they commanded him to accept the great Maggid R. Dov as rabbi, and they showed him that those springs of wisdom that had been going to the Baal Shem Tov were going to the rabbi the Maggid, of blessed memory.

---

## §131

I heard from the aforementioned rabbi that R. Eliezer of Amsterdam went to the Holy Land on account of R. Nachman of Horodenka, for he said, "When the two of us are in the Holy Land, we will bring the Redeemer." When he came to the Holy Land, all the people of the city went out to meet him; and when he saw R. Shimshon, son of R. Nachman, and asked him, "Where is your father?" he said to him, "He traveled outside the Land." He said, "Woe, woe, I came on his account." And when R. Nachman heard that R. Eliezer had traveled to the Holy Land, he ran quickly to the Holy Land; but before he came to the Holy Land, R. Eliezer died, because of our many sins.

---

## §132

I also heard from the rabbi of our community: There was a certain wealthy man in the holy community of Ladmir who had no children. He sent for the Baal Shem Tov, and when he came they set up for him a certain bed, and he sat on it. At once he was alarmed and rose and said that on that bed they had transgressed the sin against Abraham our father [a forbidden union]. And he said that this bed was not [the householder's] own — for there had been a certain informer who harmed the people of the city greatly, and when he died they plundered all that he had, and the householder took this bed. The Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath there and said to the householder that if he would agree to children who would be blemished [disabled], he would have children. And he agreed, and he had three sons with humps [hunchbacks].

---

## §133

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye that two years before the flight, R. Tzvi son of the Baal Shem Tov saw his father in a dream, [and] that he [R. Tzvi] had great distress, being in debt to the nobles with nothing to pay. He asked his father, "Did you know of my distress?" He said, "I knew." He said, "Who informed you?" He said, "Those who walk at the crossroads informed me." He asked, "Who are those who walk at the crossroads?" He said, "Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob — they walk at the crossroads to know the troubles of Israel, and they go and make [them] known at the graves of the righteous. And they informed me of your distress; but there is no time to speak with you, for I must go to annul libels from robbers called Haidamaks." And the dream was about three weeks before Passover, and on that festival of Passover a libel was made in the holy community of Sdeh Lavan, and several men were seized, and they sent by a messenger to the rabbi [R. Tzvi?] that he pray for them; and on the seventh of Passover he said that he went down before the ark in great joy, and at the table my father-in-law heard from him, and he said, "It seems to me that the Divine Presence rested at the time of the singing [of the Song at the Sea], and I am confident in the Blessed Name that the decree will be annulled." And so it was.

On the last days of Passover they came out of imprisonment, and the libel was annulled completely. And after Passover there were robbers, and they fell upon several villages, and afterward they gathered until they were about five hundred robbers. At night they fell upon the city of Krasne and remained over the day, and the people of the city all fled to the fortress and fought from the fortress. The gunpowder in the fortress was almost spent, and the robbers found three women and took wagons with straw, and the women drove the wagons with the straw while [the robbers] pushed behind the wagons and wished to burn the fortress. And a camp of Poles was stationed near the city of Toveriv, and at that time those Poles came with great firearms called *cannon*, and all the robbers fled, and not one of them remained; and they had quiet from them all the summer days.

---

## §134

I heard from a certain man named R. Zev, brother of R. Leib the pious of Linits, who fixed his dwelling in the Holy Land for more than thirty years and passed away in Jerusalem the holy city. And R. Zev too was learned and God-fearing, worthy to be believed. And from the rabbi of our community I also heard a different version, and I will write the rabbi's view on this too. Once the Baal Shem Tov fell very ill, until he had no power of speech. His men stood over him, and he signaled to them that they should clothe him in tefillin, and they did so, and he lay in tefillin until very late. Afterward he began to speak, and they asked him what this was. He said that there was found upon him a transgression he had committed in his youth, and there was a great accusation against him, and his master [from on high] came and informed him and said, "Quickly put on tefillin." And afterward the accuser came in the form of a gentile with an iron [implement] trembling in his hand, and wished to cut off my head, but he could not draw near me because of the tefillin. And he cried out to me in the Russian tongue, "Cast off the [tefillin] straps from upon you!" but I paid no heed to him, and he cried out until he was nullified. And the rabbi [of our community's version] said that he saw the gates shut, and they did not wish to let his brother-in-law R. Gershon enter, and [R. Gershon] took a great staff and struck at the gates until they opened, and entered and cried out in anger at the court and said, "For such a small matter would you decree death upon him, Heaven forbid?" And this is what is said in *Tikunei Zohar*: there is a commandment called *matronita* [the Matron], and it takes the person under its wings and does not leave him under the hand of an accuser — such as the commandment of tefillin.

---

## §135

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov led the rabbi R. Yechiel Michel to the rabbinate of the holy community of Horodno; and on Friday night they strayed on the road, and it was very dark — literally, no man could see his fellow. The Baal Shem Tov descended from the wagon to search for the road, and afterward the rabbi R. Yechiel Michel also descended, and found the Baal Shem Tov lying with hands and feet outstretched on the ground, weeping a great weeping and tearing the hair of his head; and he said that they would, Heaven forbid, desecrate the Sabbath. The aforementioned rabbi said, "Had we seen nothing in him but this — that he wept a great weeping over the desecration of the Sabbath — it would be enough." Afterward they came to the holy community of Horodno, and the rabbi preached on the Sabbath, and there was a great [Talmudic] debate in the synagogue during the sermon. There was a great scholar there called R. Michel the judge, who was very sharp, and he raised difficulties against him, and the rabbi asked of him that at the third meal "I will resolve for you all the difficulties." The community had a large house that they did not use except when receiving a new rabbi; they made the third meal for all the people of the city and seated each one according to his honor. The judge R. Michel sat by the rabbi, and R. Zev Kitzes sat at the end of the table, and the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him, in these words, "I hate a [false-]humble one; come and sit here."

The rabbi began to repeat the sermon, and R. Michel raised difficulties, and R. Zev debated with him until he prevailed; and R. Michel was ashamed in the matter and stood firm on it, even though it was crooked [his position], and he did not wish to admit the truth. The Baal Shem Tov began to entreat and said, "Do not confuse my table," until [R. Michel] grew quiet. After the Sabbath the Baal Shem Tov traveled to a village for a circumcision, with the rabbi, and R. Michel pressed himself to sit with them too. They took him, and on the road he began to speak of the wisdom of astronomy, and R. Zev Kitzes answered him, and [R. Michel] saw that R. Zev was learned against him in the wisdom of astronomy, and he wondered and said, "How did such things come to a pious one?" When the Baal Shem Tov wished to travel on his way, he feared lest he grieve the rabbi [R. Michel], and he took his Torah from him, and he became an ignoramus. Now his custom was to rise at midnight to study Gemara and codes and Maimonides, and when he opened the book he knew nothing. And he supposed that his mind was not clear because he had drunk much honey-water, and he shut the book and smoked the pipe and slept a little, and lay until morning. In the morning, after the prayer, he would study his lesson, and he took the book and opened it and knew nothing, and he understood that the Baal Shem Tov had done this to him. And he came to the Baal Shem Tov and said, "Rabbi, return my Torah to me." And the Baal Shem Tov reproved him and said, "And is it for the sake of prevailing and provoking that one studies Torah?!" And he reproved him, and he became a completely righteous man.

---

## §136

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye: when there was a war in his days between Greece and Ishmael [the Ottomans], the Baal Shem Tov said, "I saw two [celestial] princes warring, and the prince of Ishmael prevailed, and I saw the prince of Greece going back in burning anger. And there will be from this a great calamity to Israel, Heaven forbid. And I prayed that it be the reverse — that the prince of Greece prevail; only two Jews I could not save from their hand."

---

## §137

I heard that the Baal Shem Tov spent the Sabbath with a certain righteous man, and the candle began to sputter. The Baal Shem Tov said, "The candle tells me that you had marital relations by the light of the candle." And in truth it was so, except that [the man] was not culpable in this matter, for the candle had gone out, and he supposed that it had gone out entirely, and afterward it began to give light, as a candle does at the end.

---

## §138

I heard from R. Zelig of the holy community of Letishev that he was traveling on the road and came to the holy community of Ispoli, which was still a village, and he lodged with a slaughterer. They heard that the rabbi R. Meir, the rabbi of the region, had come, and I went to greet him. And he asked R. Zelig to sit there several days until all the people of the villages gathered for judgment, that he might have company; and he sat with the rabbi and recounted before him:

"Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled to the holy community of Brod and traveled by way of Horodenka, and he stopped before my house and sent for me that I should come out. And I was not yet head of court, and he said, 'Meir, sit with me on the wagon and travel with me to the holy community of Brod.' And I took the bag with my prayer shawl and tefillin and a coat, and traveled. When we came to the holy community of Brod, he stopped at a lodging in the manner of a merchant, and no one came to him except two men — one of the magnates and the second was not a magnate. They were esteemed men and greeted him, and each time they would come to him. And I saw that the money in the Baal Shem Tov's hand was spent, and he had not even a single penny, and I was distressed, why I had not taken my own money from my house. And I heard him say to the householder, 'Prepare yourself for the road,' and I went and informed those two men that the Baal Shem Tov was traveling on his way, and they came. When they saw that his journey was delayed, they said, 'We will go to the study hall, and when he is ready to travel, let R. Meir give us word and we will come.' And so it was, that he informed them, [and] they came; and the magnate gave the Baal Shem Tov, the first time, one gold coin, and when he came a second time the Baal Shem Tov took that gold coin and gave it to the aforementioned magnate and said, 'Give twelve gold pieces to the men of the klaus' — for they were esteemed in the eyes of the Baal Shem Tov, who said that he saw the Divine Presence resting upon the klaus — 'and the rest, twelve gold pieces, distribute to the poor.' And they traveled from the city. When I saw that he had squandered that gold coin, my heart melted within me, for I knew that he had not even a single penny. And I asked him why he did so, for your honor has not even a single penny for expenses. And he answered me with an elegant expression, 'Know faithfully, as long as the Blessed Name lives, we have nothing to worry about.' And so it was: when they came to the holy community of Radivil, the people of the city began to come to him for remedies, and so in every place, until he brought money home."

---

## §139

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov sent to the rabbi of the holy community of Bitshov [a message] that his slaughterer was of the sect of the prayer leader [Shabbetai Tzvi] — may his name be blotted out — and that he was feeding [the community] forbidden meat [*tereifot*]; for after he showed [the rabbi] the knife, he would strike the knife on a hammer in order to notch it. And the rabbi investigated him, and so it was.

---

## §140

A tale: R. David Forkes came with the Baal Shem Tov to the holy community of Konstantin, and the Baal Shem Tov commanded that the slaughterer come to show him the knife. He did not come at once, and the Baal Shem Tov stood to pray. The slaughterer came and did not wish to wait, and rebuked the householder's wife to let him slaughter, and she let him, and he slaughtered and went off. After the prayer the Baal Shem Tov said, "The slaughterer has not yet come." They answered him, "He has already come and slaughtered the fowl," and the fowl were still lying in the salting. The Baal Shem Tov looked at them and said that one should eat [them]; but the aforementioned R. David did not wish to eat, and the Baal Shem Tov was not particular about this.

---

## §141

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the lands of Poberezhye, and he said that a certain righteous man had passed away near his place — "and it seems to me that it is the rendering of legal decisions [the halachic authority] of the holy community of Konstantin, for I saw the ascents of his soul and recognized his movements."

---

## §142

I heard from the rabbi of our community that a certain nobleman was an enemy of the Baal Shem Tov and said that if he saw the Baal Shem Tov he would kill him with a firearm. Once the Baal Shem Tov was at the fortress and was walking to and fro in the courtyard of the fortress with the commissar. And the aforementioned hostile lord came to that fortress, and the commissar left the Baal Shem Tov and went to speak with the lord, and brought him into his house. The Baal Shem Tov too entered the house, and took leave of the commissar and went home. The commissar wondered at this matter, for he knew that the aforementioned lord was very angry at the Baal Shem Tov. And at mealtime the commissar spoke with the lord about the Baal Shem Tov, and [the lord] was angry at him and said as above. And the commissar said to him, "But he was here when my lord came, and he even entered this house and took leave of me." And he said, "I did not see him." He said, "Since he is such a man, that when he wishes [he is not seen], I will appease him."

---

## §143

I heard from the rabbi of our community that once the fringe of the Baal Shem Tov's prayer shawl became soiled with mud. He said that R. Moshe of Shatanov had died. And he said, "I did not hold him in esteem to this [degree], that they should mark it [his death] in my fringes."

---

## §144

And in passing I will write what I heard from the rabbi R. Yechiel, head of court of the holy community of Kowel. In that city there was a certain man named R. Zusman, and he was very wealthy and was everything in the city. Once the rabbi came out of his prayer on the Sabbath night to his house and heard that they were [still only] receiving the Sabbath in the synagogue. He sent to the synagogue to call the beadle and asked him why they were late in the synagogue with the receiving of the Sabbath. He answered that R. Zusman had been at the fortress and they waited for him. He said, "And if R. Zusman had not [come] until tomorrow, would they not have received the Sabbath until tomorrow?" R. Zusman's wife heard and said, "And if the rabbi will pray in the synagogue and they will not wait for my husband for the Kedushah and Barchu and Amen, I will close your study hall on you" — for [the rabbi] prayed in a special small house within the study hall and heard from there the Kedushah and Barchu and Amen. In the morning, when she rose, she went to the basin and washed her face and hands, and went to the towel to wipe [herself], and she stood like a silent stone. They ran to the rabbi, and the rabbi was praying and saying "and cut off," and he gestured with his hand, and she died.

---

## §145

Another tale: that [the rabbi] decreed upon the people of the villages that they be in the city for the festival of Passover. And such was the custom: he stationed two Torah scholars who would go from house to house to scald [kasher] the vessels in their presence, and likewise the matzot were baked in their presence. And in a certain village there was a learned tax-farmer who paid no heed to the rabbi's decree and did not come to the city for Passover, and his innkeeper too did not come to the city. On the intermediate days the tax-farmer came to the city, and [the rabbi] sent for him, and he came. And he said, "Because you did not keep Passover in the city on the first days, you will be in the city for Passover on the last days." And so it was: he came to the village and found that his wife had died, and he brought his wife to the city on the sixth [day] of Passover, and until they buried her the time stretched until night, and he had to spend the last festival day in the city; and the innkeeper too died at the time of the Kiddush of the last festival day.

---

## §146

A tale of the head of court in the holy community of Horodno: there was a circumcision, and he did not wish to be the *sandak*. They urged him greatly, until he said that the child was a *mamzer* [of illegitimate birth]. And [the householder] was the head of a family, and when the family heard his words they began to cry out, and there was a great outcry in the synagogue. And the rabbi said to the beadle, "Go and take my staff in your hand and go to the mother who gave birth and show her the staff, and at once she will confess; and if not, she will die." And she confessed and said that she had been coerced — for she had gone to the cellar to bring wine, and a certain lord ran after her and ravished her. And all her family members fell silent.

---

## §147

I heard in the name of R. Eliyahu of the holy community of Sokolovka. When he was still tender in years, he dwelt in the holy community of Mezhibuzh, and he heard once that the Baal Shem Tov said, "Whoever wishes that his prayer ascend to heaven, let him pray with me word for word." And R. Eliyahu did so: when the Baal Shem Tov said "Lord," he too said [it], and when he said "of the world," he too said "of the world," until he finished the whole prayer; and he did so a long time.

Once the Baal Shem Tov came, in the Verses of Praise, to the verse "A horse is a false hope for deliverance," and repeated it many times, until very late, and the first time [R. Eliyahu] said it with him; and again he wondered what [the Baal Shem Tov] was intending by this verse, and he looked in the book *Mishnat Chasidim* and there were no [mystical] intentions upon it, [so] he left off praying with him. Once he came to the Baal Shem Tov's house, and the Baal Shem Tov saw him and said to him, "Eliyahu, you stopped praying with me." He recounted to him the affair — that he had said the verse several times [and could find no meaning]. And [the Baal Shem Tov] answered that a certain Jew had met him on the road on the Sabbath eve and could not reach a settlement before the Sabbath, and spent the Sabbath in the field; and it became known to a certain robber that a Jew was spending the Sabbath in the field, and he rode on a horse to find him to kill him, "and with this saying I spoiled the road for him so that he could not find him."

---

## §148

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Nemirov, and the Baal Shem Tov was walking from the large house to the small house, until he strayed into the cellar; and he entered the cellar and commanded that the mezuzot be inspected. A certain man asked him, "And if some person strays, he will at once attribute it to some cause — perhaps it is [mere] chance?" The Baal Shem Tov answered him, "With me there is no chance" — and from this every person should believe that everything comes by [divine] providence, and not attribute it to chance, Heaven forbid.

---

## §149

The rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye had an amulet from the rabbi R. Naftali, and he showed that amulet to the Baal Shem Tov. The Baal Shem Tov recognized in it that it had been written while [R. Naftali] was [fresh] from the ritual bath and fasting, and he said, "I would write such an amulet after eating, while sitting on my bed."

---

## §150

So I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye, that the aforementioned rabbi would select for himself a certain old woman and always give her gold — called a *gulden* — for four sets of fringes, doubled eightfold, [on condition] that she not interrupt from the beginning of the spinning to the end with any word. And when they brought fringes from the holy community of Kalish for the first time, he bought them, for they were very beautiful; but he was afraid to put them in his prayer shawl lest they had not been spun for the sake [of the commandment]. And he showed them to the Baal Shem Tov, who said, "If you are afraid to put them in your prayer shawl, give them to me and I will put them in my prayer shawl" — and he recognized in them that they had been spun for the sake [of the commandment].

---

## §151

I heard: Once they placed the turnip [dish] before the Baal Shem Tov at the table, and the Baal Shem Tov did not wish to eat. They asked him why, and he said, "This turnip grew in the cemetery of the gentiles," and they too did not wish to eat it, and he placed it at the foot of the table.

---

## §152

I heard from the rabbi of our community that there were in the holy community of Mikolaiev two pious men, and both their names were R. David. One was old, whom we knew, and one died in his youth. Once they traveled on the road with the Baal Shem Tov and asked him, saying, "Our rabbi, tell us what we need to rectify." And he commanded each one to recite before him from the book; and one recited before him — and I do not know whether Mishnah or *Ein Yaakov* or Zohar — and afterward he commanded the second to recite, and the Baal Shem Tov said, "I know." And to one he said that he had sinned against his fellow, and the other he commanded to descend from the wagon, and he descended with him and told him in secret, for there was some shame in the matter. And after one R. David died, the second R. David mourned greatly over him for many days, for they had loved each other. Once he came to the Baal Shem Tov, who wished to comfort him, and said, "Do not worry about your fellow, for I was in the palace of King David and saw your fellow there, and I asked him whether he had rectified that thing he needed to rectify, and he said that he had already rectified it in [his] life. And I saw the Tanna R. Akiva standing on guard, clothed in red with a spear in his hand."

---

## §153

I also heard from the aforementioned rabbi that the Baal Shem Tov saw the angel Hadarniel, who is the second angel that Moses our teacher, peace be upon him, encountered in his ascent on Mount Sinai, as is said in the holy Zohar.

---

## §154

I heard from the rabbi of our community that once a certain young man was killed for the sanctification of the Name in the holy community of Polonnoye — they made false libels against him, and he sanctified the Name greatly. Once the Baal Shem Tov came to the holy community of Polonnoye and stopped by the house of his mother and sent to call her, and she came out, and he comforted her. And he said to her, "Know that at the time of his killing, all the worlds opened that opened for Isaac at the time of his binding." And he was afraid to enter the house, for the house was a gentile dwelling, and he said that on account of that house the aforementioned young man was killed.

---

## §155

I heard from the aforementioned rabbi that once the Baal Shem Tov said, "I wonder: on the Sabbath night, during the prayer, I searched for R. Gershon and did not find him in the Land of Israel; and on the Sabbath in the morning, during the prayer, I saw him in the Land of Israel. And I do not know how to judge this, unless he went outside the [Sabbath] boundary." And after several years R. Gershon came to him and asked him about this matter, and it was hidden from R. Gershon. And afterward he remembered: they had given him the commandment of circumcision in Acre, and he spent the Sabbath there. And in Acre there are two synagogues — one [reckoned as] outside the Land and one in the Land of Israel — "and I went and prayed on the Sabbath night in the synagogue that is outside the Land, and afterward my heart pained me over this matter, why I had not prayed in [the synagogue of] the Land of Israel, and the next day I went and prayed in the synagogue that is in the Land of Israel."

---

## §156

And a tale concerning the head of court of the holy community of Horodno, when there was a circumcision: I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye and from the rabbi of our community that the Baal Shem Tov said, "Whoever recounts the praises of the righteous, it is as though he is occupied with the Work of the Chariot."

I also heard: in whichever of the worlds a person is, it is as though those worlds go before him.

---

## §157

I was once in the holy community of Tultshin with a certain man named R. David. At that time there was the match between the pious R. Baruch and the pious R. Yosef son of R. Yechiel Michel, of blessed memory. And the aforementioned R. David heard from R. Yosef in the name of the Baal Shem Tov: "And he sent angels before him" — these are the angels that go before him; and he said that before Jacob our father there always go angels at his right and demons at his left, and before the Baal Shem Tov too there go angels at his right and demons at his left. But he did not wish to make use of the angels, because they are holy, nor of the demons, because they are liars.

Once a certain widow dwelt in a village, and a certain man came to take the lease from her. He went to the lord to rent the lease, and a certain demon answered [offered] that he be sent to [the lord], but [the man] did not wish. But [the demon] urged him greatly to send him, and promised that he would do as he commanded, and he sent him and commanded him, "Take care, do him no harm at all; only when he wishes to speak before the lord, stop his mouth with your hand." And the demon let him speak all he wished and waited until he took the lease and received the contract, and on his return, when he came to the bridge, he harmed him and he fell on the bridge. And the Baal Shem Tov ran and came and decreed upon [the demon] at once that he leave him without any harm in the world. He answered and said, "See, my lord, I heard [obeyed] as you commanded me." And I do not remember, but it seems to me that [the man] stood up healthy and strong.

---

## §158

And from my father-in-law R. Alexander I heard a tale like this: There was a certain man who wished to take a village from the hand of a certain widow, and the Baal Shem Tov reproved him, but he did not heed him. And he rode on a horse to the Baal Shem Tov's house and knocked at the window and said, "See that I am going to rent the village, and I will see what God will do to me." And the Baal Shem Tov shed tears. When he came to the village, he rented the lease at a great increase [over the widow's rent], and the lord planned to write him the contract. Meanwhile his head began to ache, and he said to the lord, "My head aches me greatly!" He said, "Sit, I will not delay to write the contract," and he waited a little. He said a second time, "My head aches me greatly!" He said, "Lie on the bed," and froth — called *foam* — began to come from his mouth, and the lord commanded his servant to throw him among the swine — as it is said, "They draw a person near only at the time of their benefit, and do not stand by a person in the time of his distress." And they brought him home, and his hands and feet were [paralyzed,] taken from him, and his tongue too was taken from him. And they called the Baal Shem Tov to heal him, and he began to speak a little. Meanwhile the Baal Shem Tov sensed that the household was engaging gentile sorceresses to heal him, and from then on he did not wish to go to him, and [the man] could speak only a few words.

---

## §159

I heard from the rabbi of our community that there was a certain man in the holy community of Mohilev named R. Israel Shapir, and they informed against him to the minister. He traveled to the minister and turned aside from the road to the Baal Shem Tov to recount his matter and to ask of him concerning this. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Do not fear this; only, every person must be imprisoned at some time." And he traveled to the minister in fear, and when he came to him he found favor in his eyes, and [the minister] even lent him more money. And on his return from the minister he passed through the holy community of Bar, and the minister of the city seized him into imprisonment over some matter, and the people of the city said to [the minister], "Leave him be, for he is a man who can pursue you," and [the minister] released him home in peace.

---

## §160

A tale concerning the head of court in the holy community of Bar: one of the people of the city was at odds with him and traveled to the minister to bring a complaint against him — and it is not known to me whether over the matter of the rabbinate or another matter. And [the rabbi] turned aside from the road to the Baal Shem Tov, that he pray for him that he obtain his desire from the minister, and the Baal Shem Tov promised him and he traveled on his way. Now the preacher, of blessed memory, was at that time in the holy community of Mezhibuzh, and he knew that the rabbi, head of court, was righteous in his case against the people of the city, and the opponent was a base man; and he was afraid to tell the Baal Shem Tov of this. What did the preacher do? He gathered a quorum to himself in secret and took a Torah scroll and annulled the judgments against the aforementioned rabbi, head of court, so that they could not do him harm.

Once — it seems to me it was the New Moon — the aforementioned preacher was with his relative by marriage — it seems to me it was R. Zev Kitzes — reclining at the Baal Shem Tov's table. In the midst of the meal the Baal Shem Tov said, "I do not know how to judge the matter — that I prayed for a certain man of the holy community of Bar, and they seem to answer me, yet they do not [fully] answer me." And the preacher, of blessed memory, began to laugh a little. And his relative said, "What is this that I saw — that they called a quorum of ten to you, and you slipped away?" but [the preacher] answered him nothing. And the Baal Shem Tov pressed him to tell him the matter, and the aforementioned preacher answered, "I will not conceal it from our rabbi: that man of the holy community of Bar spoke well before your honor, but I know the inside of the matter — that the rabbi is righteous in his case against the people of the city — and I gathered a quorum and took a Torah scroll and annulled the judgments against him." And the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "May a blessing rest upon your head; you did well!"

---

## §161

I heard from R. Yekel of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, an ignoramus, that there was in that holy community a prayer leader of the study hall named R. Moshe, and he was a wondrous righteous man. Once a prayer was given to [him to lead] on Rosh Hashanah in the study hall — and it is not known whether the morning prayer or the Additional prayer of the second day — and there was a certain prayer leader who had this prayer on the first day, and he said to the aforementioned R. Moshe that he should exchange his prayer with him, and he would pray on the first day, and so they did. And R. Moshe prayed on the first day, and in the midst of the Amidah aloud, R. Moshe turned his face to the congregation and answered and said in his voice to the aforementioned prayer leader, by his name, "How did you set me up as a target for an arrow?" And he said words of moral instruction about half an hour, and there was great weeping in the synagogue, and he returned to his prayer. And the scholars were angered at him for interrupting his prayer during the Amidah. After the afternoon prayer, when they went to say Tashlich, the aforementioned R. Yekel asked him why he had interrupted during the Amidah, and he knew nothing of it and did not remember anything. And R. Yekel said to him, "I would not have believed this thing; but because I heard about the Baal Shem Tov, that he would fall upon the holy ark and afterward not remember [it], therefore I believed this matter too, that it could be so."

---

## §162

I heard from the rabbi of our community that when the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye was head of court in the holy community of Rashkov, the angels said to the Baal Shem Tov, "Why are you silent — the rabbi of the holy community of Rashkov has no livelihood?"

---

## §163

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye that when the aforementioned R. Moshe the prayer leader circumcised a Jewish infant, a radiance of the face was recognizable in him until the third day. And the rabbi of our community said that his brother was a slaughterer in the holy community of Kotov, and the aforementioned R. Moshe too was from that same region.

---

## §164

I heard that once the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, was sitting at the meal of a redemption of the firstborn, and at the end of the meal the Baal Shem Tov took a piece of bread and chewed it, and his face became red and his senses changed, and they supposed that he was choking and wished to save him. R. Zev Kitzes looked at his face and said, "Leave him be." And it was thus until very late, and he returned of himself to his [normal] state. And they asked him what this thing was. He answered, "When I ate the piece of bread, I directed the intentions of eating, and I attained, with one intention, what Moses our teacher, peace be upon him, intended when Zipporah gave him to eat the first time. And when I directed this unification, Moses our teacher, peace be upon him, came here, and what was [happened]."

---

## §165

I heard, and I do not remember what I heard, when the famous pious R. Yechiel Michel was with the Baal Shem Tov. Once, on Friday morning before the morning prayer, the Baal Shem Tov told him to travel home that day, but he did not answer him, for he said in his heart, "Today is the Sabbath eve, and I still have no wagon," and he went to the study hall to recite [the weekly portion] twice in scripture and once in Targum. When mealtime came, the Baal Shem Tov's wife asked where R. Michel was, and the Baal Shem Tov said, "I commanded him to travel home." Even so she sent for him, and they found him in the study hall and called him to come to eat. When he came, the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Sit and eat, and you will have a wagon." After the meal he went outside and saw a certain lad standing with a wagon in the market with three horses. He asked him, "Whence are you?" He said, "From the city of Olik." And so it was in truth: that he had left the merchant in some place and was returning with the empty wagon home; and they said that from the city of Mezhibuzh to the city of Olik is seventeen *parsah*. And the wagon-driver asked him in what place or what settlement he would spend the Sabbath, and he answered that he did not know how it would fall out to reach there by the Sabbath. And he hired the wagon-driver and went to take leave of the Baal Shem Tov. And the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Go home in peace, and you will find your wife having a difficult labor, and you will find there many women — take them out of your house, and whisper in her ear what I taught you, and you will have a male child, for good fortune." And so it was, that he came home two hours before night and did as the Baal Shem Tov commanded him, and she bore a male, and this child is R. Yosef of Yampol.

---

## §166

I heard from the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye that he asked the Baal Shem Tov about the matter of "clothing" [*hitlabshut*] mentioned regarding the upper worlds — how this concept exists in an example by a person. And he said to him, "When you give benefit to your fellow, whether in money or in Torah, this is the clothing — that one clothes his fellow, the giver to the receiver, or the reverse."

---

## §167

I further heard from him that the Baal Shem Tov asked him why he lingered so long in the ritual bath, "and why is it that when I go to the ritual bath, I shut my eyes once, and I see all the worlds?"

---

## §168

I heard from R. Avraham of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, son-in-law of R. Michel of the holy community of Tultshin, that R. Yosef, preacher of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, before his death fell ill in his bowels, and there was no one to serve him properly. Once a certain lad from the slaughterhouse came to him and had pity on him, and took the garment from beneath him and cleaned him well and washed the garment in the river and dried it and spread it beneath him. And he did so every day until the day of the preacher's death; and not many days [later] that lad fell ill. And the aforementioned preacher came to the Baal Shem Tov and said to him, "Why do you not go to visit my friend, who is ill?" And at once the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, and his men went to visit the sick one, and the lad passed away to his world; and the Baal Shem Tov saw that the preacher, of blessed memory, walked before his bier.

---

## §169

I heard from the son of the righteous R. Chaim of Krasna, of blessed memory, that once the aforementioned R. Chaim asked the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, to remember his name and his wife's name to pray for them. The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "And by what shall I remember your names?" R. Chaim, of blessed memory, answered him, "Set a sign: Chaim son of Rachel, the initials [spelling] *chaver* [companion]; and 'the wife of a *chaver* is like a *chaver*,' the initials [of] Chaya daughter of Rivka also [spelling] *chaver*." The Baal Shem Tov said to him, "What kind of sign is this — perhaps the *chet* is [for] Chaikel or the like, and the *chet* of her name is Chana or the like?" And the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, said, "There is [a better] sign, for Chaim and Chaya have the numerical value of [the divine names] Havayah [and] Adni."

---

## §170

I heard from the rabbi of our community that the famous rabbi R. Nachman of Kosov held a certain village [lease]. And he would send to the city, to the members of the holy society, to each and every one, what they needed to rectify in this world from their sins, and it was all true; but it greatly grieved them, the prophesying, for there was a condition among them that none of them should prophesy. And they wished to send one of them to ask what this was, and the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye answered, "I will travel to him, for I need him [anyway], and I will ask him" — for [R. Nachman] was a teacher to his sons, who were at his table, and [R. Nachman] would send him flour and grain. And it was a long while that he had not sent him [any], and the preacher-rabbi [of Polonnoye] traveled to him and did not find him at home. And he saw a wagon ready and asked about him, and they said that he had gone to a village, and he waited for him. When he came, he saw him carrying a great load of straw on his shoulders, and the preacher said to him, in these words, "Crazy Nachman! You yourself must carry straw — should you not have commanded your Canaanite slave to carry this straw?" And R. Nachman answered him, "You, the crazy one — behold, the Torah said, 'And I will give grass in your field for your cattle,' and afterward 'you shall eat and be satisfied'; and you say to honor the Canaanite slave with this commandment?" And the preacher fell silent, for he spoke well. And R. Nachman's horses, of blessed memory, were harnessed to the wagon to go to the city, and the preacher said to him, "Why did you not send me flour?" And he answered him, "See, the wagon is ready to travel — I myself [will go] with the flour for you."

Afterward [the preacher] asked him, "What is this — that you prophesy?" And he answered him, "I am no prophet, nor the son of a prophet, [but] you know the incident: that I sat at the third meal with the rabbi R. Moshe, head of court of the holy community of Kotov, and they tarried into the night. And the slaughterer was one of the members of the society and was also sitting there. And a certain butcher named Leib, called Leib Glitz, came and called the slaughterer to slaughter, once and twice and three times, but the slaughterer did not wish to go, for he longed to hear words of the living God — for their conversation was always about how to serve God. When the butcher saw that the slaughterer did not wish to go and depart from there, he said in his heart, 'I will come and confuse them, and they will pray the evening prayer, and the slaughterer will go to slaughter,' and so he did.

"And when he brought a candle, the rabbi was angry at him and placed him under ban, and the butcher died. And I saw in a dream that I went to a certain synagogue and heard someone expounding words of Torah, and I ran to the synagogue, and when I came to the door of the synagogue I recognized in him that he was a husk [a demonic shell] — for the rabbi said that they had a sign to know whether it was this dead one himself or not. And I ran toward him, but my father held me back and said, 'You will yet shame a person' — for there had been an incident, when awake, that my father-in-law, of blessed memory, once dragged a certain preacher down from the sermon [in error]. And I slipped from my father's hand and spat in [the husk's] face, and it remained standing naked, with only a kerchief over its nakedness. And I recognized that it was that butcher, and I said to him, 'Leib! From where did you get the words of Torah?' And he answered, 'Because of the ban they do not judge me, and I go around the Garden of Eden and hear what they study within, and I can come neither to Gehenna nor to the Garden of Eden; therefore I ask of your honor that you tell the rabbi to release me from the ban.' And he told me, concerning each and every one, what they need to rectify, and this is what I sent you.

"And I asked, 'What do I have to rectify?' And he led me to a certain house and showed me a certain gentile lying dead, and said to me, 'This you must rectify.'" And the incident was: that once R. Nachman, of blessed memory, traveled on the road and lodged in a village, and did not wish to lodge with a Jew — his reason I do not know — and he gave a flask of brandy to that gentile and lodged there and gave fodder to his horses. And at night he heard from the gentile words between him and his wife, and could not restrain himself from [lustful] thought, and had an emission, God spare us. And he fasted over this from Sabbath to Sabbath and killed the husk, but the trace still remained. And [the dead butcher] said that this [the gentile] he must rectify, and he began to weep; and afterward he saw, and it seemed to him like a sword hanging on the wall, "and I took the sword and cut the gentile in two," and he said that he had already rectified it by this. "Therefore I wished to travel [to you]; but since you have come to me, I need not travel — and you will see that they will release him from the ban. Here is flour, and go in peace."

---

## §171

I heard from my father-in-law, of blessed memory: Once, before the first flight — which was before this flight that was in our days — on the Sabbath eve after midday, at the time when they were all cooking for the honor of the Sabbath, the Baal Shem Tov sent to all the people of the city that they should all flee. And he himself fled at once in order to receive the Sabbath beforehand, as was his way. And he had good horses, and the way was not far, only one *parsah* to the Dniester river. And he crossed the river and received the Sabbath. And the whole city strained the broth from the meat and also fled across the Dniester, only they were a little late, for they had to kindle the Sabbath candles at the crossing; and thank God, they were all saved. And on the Sabbath day the robbers came to the city and did not find the Jews. And it is not known to me whether the incident was in the holy community of Tluste or the holy community of Kolomyya, for he dwelt afterward in the holy community of Kolomyya.

---

## §172

I heard from the rabbi of our community, of blessed memory. When the Baal Shem Tov was with the lease-holders of the holy community of Slutsk — where the name of the one was R. Shmuel and the name of the second R. Gedaliah, and the name of R. Shmuel's wife was Tovele — she urged her husband to send for the Baal Shem Tov, for they had built a stone house and were afraid to dwell in it. They sent for him, and he came to them, and the men were not at home, and she wished to make a meal, and he said that he must see the knife. And the slaughterer had to come and show him the knife, and the people of the city were greatly angered — how did his heart fill him to inspect the knife of the town slaughterer, and especially in a great community? But they could do nothing to him, for the [lady] magnate had so commanded.

And at the afternoon prayer they heard that he prayed the Sephardic rite, and they were even more angered. When R. Shmuel came home they informed against him, but he answered them, "It is not proper conduct to do anything to him without a pretext." Also, before the afternoon prayer she had led him to the buildings, and in several places he made movements as though he saw something, and they suspected him of falsehood, for literally the whole city was there. And [R. Shmuel] said to his wife that he wished to test him in a certain matter — and I have forgotten the matter. And the Baal Shem Tov heard, from his lodging, this thing that [R. Shmuel] was saying to her in bed, and the Baal Shem Tov sent his servant [to say] that she should come at once and not be concerned about the guards standing there. And at once she rose from her bed and went, and her husband prevented her from going, but she paid no heed to him and went; and when she came, the Baal Shem Tov hinted at her, "The fool wishes to test me!" And she was greatly afraid and appeased the Baal Shem Tov until he was appeased.

When she came home she told her husband, and [the Baal Shem Tov] became esteemed in his eyes, and the next day he made a great meal for him and spoke with him about traveling to the Holy Land, for he wished to send him [there] by land. And he asked the Baal Shem Tov how much expense was needed for this, and he answered that sixty thousand was needed for the expense, and [R. Shmuel] answered him, "It is a small matter to me," and promised that he would send him money every year for his livelihood. And the Baal Shem Tov thought that he was not concerned about his livelihood, but the main thing was that he should merit to be there in the Holy Land. And the Baal Shem Tov stayed there about three weeks.

Once the aforementioned woman Tovele asked the Baal Shem Tov how long the days of [her] fortune would last, and the Baal Shem Tov rebuked her and said that one does not ask such things. But she urged him greatly, and he shut his eyes a moment and said, "They will last twenty-two years." And when her husband heard this, he was filled with fury against him. And another thing happened: that with one of the magnates there was a sick child, and [the Baal Shem Tov] came and said that it would live, and afterward he saw that it would die, and he understood that they would wish to shame him, and he traveled from there at midnight by the leaping of the earth [*kefitzat ha-derech*] about fifteen *parsah*, until he went out of their territory to another kingdom. They came to a village and went out of the village, and through the length of the village or the city he traveled in the manner of all the world; and when they went out of one village they reached at once another village, and R. Tzvi the scribe asked him, "What is this?" but he answered him nothing, for it is not permitted to interrupt, only he pushed him away with his hand.

So I heard from the aforementioned rabbi several times, that one must not interrupt. And when they had fled to another territory and he stopped to rest, he wrote a letter: "Know faithfully that I could have given myself counsel, so that you could do nothing to me; only I feared lest this harm me in another place; therefore I turned away from you. And not one of my words will fall [unfulfilled], as I said — that after twenty-two years you will have a downfall through your [own] servant." And he signed at the end, "Spoken by Israel Baal Shem Tov, servant of the Lord," and he sent this letter with a special messenger. And R. Shmuel was greatly angered, and R. Shmuel was ashamed to show the letter to others, only he showed his signature.

---

## §173

I heard from other people that he came home in three days by the leaping of the earth, even though there were heavy rains. For this incident was before Rosh Hashanah, and he longed [to be] home for the festival. And I heard from a certain man of our community named R. Eliezer — and he was from the city of Birtza — that the Baal Shem Tov, on his return, spent the Sabbath about a small *parsah* from that city. And he told me the name of the village, but I do not remember it. The Baal Shem Tov came to the village, and when they said in the lease-holder's house that the Baal Shem Tov had come, the lease-holder ran outside toward him and rejoiced greatly, for he had never had children, and he said in his heart, "Perhaps I will have a [good] result." And he greeted him. The Baal Shem Tov asked, "Perhaps I may spend the Sabbath with you?" and he answered the Baal Shem Tov, "Indeed, indeed, and I ask your honor greatly to spend the Sabbath with me." The Baal Shem Tov asked, "And will there be a quorum with you?" And the men who came with him numbered only nine, and he said to the Baal Shem Tov, "There are only nine men with us." The Baal Shem Tov answered, "There will be a quorum," and he did not move from the wagon and commanded that the wagon be driven into the vestibule of the house. And the gate was very low, and the gate had no proper lintel, only a beam of the wall was laid upon the two doorposts; and the arched [supports] of the wagon were high, and when the servant drove into the vestibule he stopped the horses before reaching the arch. The Baal Shem Tov said, "Why did you stop?" And the servant drove on, and the lintel with the whole structure raised itself, and the wagon with the arches entered.

And about this I did not ask how he got out from there afterward, while all the world stood and watched this wonder; and the lease-holder rejoiced greatly and said in his heart, "God willing, I will have a remedy." When it came past midday, [the Baal Shem Tov] said to gather the quorum to stand and pray, as was his custom always to pray on the Sabbath eve immediately after midday. And he said to them, "There are ten men among us," and they answered, "There are only nine men." And he said, "I know that there will surely be a quorum," and they laughed behind his back, for they saw that there were not ten. Meanwhile it fell into the lease-holder's mind that across the river there was a small lease[-holding], and there was there a sick man, about ten years, who was ill without hands and feet and without tongue, and he lay like a silent stone, only they fed him a little food. And the householder said to the Baal Shem Tov, "Perhaps you mean him," and the Baal Shem Tov said, "Go call him to come here for the quorum." And he gave them his staff that [the sick man] should take in his hand and come, and they went after him, but he did not rise and did not move, and they returned empty-handed. And the Baal Shem Tov took the hat from his head and sent them and said, "Clothe him in my hat and put the staff in his hand and he will come." And so it was, that this man came to the quorum and lived ten years after this, and was a large creature, healthy as a hall [robust].

And this gate the aforementioned R. Eliezer saw twenty years later, and they showed him the sign — the lintel had still not been moved back to its original place. And when the lease-holder saw the two awesome wonders, when they sat to eat he began to entreat the Baal Shem Tov and said, "I am in my middle years and have no children, and in some time I will be old and unfit to beget, and what will be the purpose of me?" And the Baal Shem Tov answered him, "You will have children." And he asked him whether to divorce his wife, and he answered him, "What is that to you? You will have children." And after this he lived with his wife until he reached sixty years, and his wife died, and he married another wife, and she bore him two sons — and it is not known to me whether twins or separate [births].

The end of the lease-holders I heard from R. Yitzchak Aizik, of blessed memory: Thus, the duke of Slutsk was Radziwill, and he borrowed from them a sum of money and they could not collect from him, and they feared lest he ask of them yet another sum of money as a loan; and it came into their mind to fix their dwelling in the city of Breslau. And they spoke with the king [of Prussia] that he take them under his power in the holy community of Breslau, and they gave him the aforementioned debt as a gift, and the king would collect from [Radziwill] and from his property — and so they did. And they went by ship on the water, and when they came opposite the holy community of Slonim, where the duke's seat was, they took with them three barrels of money to place in the holy community of Breslau, and their steward also traveled with them. And when they drew near to the harbor, R. Shmuel went with the steward to his lodging and adorned himself to go before the minister. And while he was adorning and clothing himself, the steward slipped away from him, and [R. Shmuel] was greatly frightened and went in haste to the duke. And when he saw him, and saw that the steward was standing with the duke in an inner room and speaking with the duke, he came to him and struck the steward on his cheek. The duke responded, "That you struck him in my presence — although it is not proper conduct — nevertheless I forgive you this, because you are confident in my great love for you, that even if you struck my son in my presence I would forgive you; but answer a reply to his words." But he had no mouth to answer, and the aforementioned duke sent to the ship and found [it] according to the steward's words. And at once he was seized into imprisonment, and [the duke] commanded that dragoons be sent to the holy community of Slutsk with an officer, the major, and commanded the major to seal up all their money and belongings.

And when the letter came from the aforementioned duke, dread fell into the major's heart, and he said, "Perhaps the wheel will turn for their good and he [R. Shmuel] will become my enemy"; and he made himself as drunk and slept very much until night. When he saw that all the city was sleeping, the major himself went to the woman Tovele, wife of R. Shmuel, and informed her of this, [telling her] to clear away and remove all that was possible, for in the morning everything would be sealed. And she sent her son-in-law, and he fled with the jewelry and ornaments, and afterward became head of court in the holy community of Breslau, and from this he became very wealthy, and had great riches. And [the duke] seized all their family that he could find, for they themselves had to write wedding-invitation letters to all the relatives, and when [the relatives] heard on the way they turned back, and whoever did not hear was seized. And the aforementioned Tovele asked the people of their city to send to the Baal Shem Tov, [that] perhaps he would help them with his prayer; and the people of the city hated them, [yet] nevertheless they sent a special messenger, and put their words in the messenger's mouth in deep secret — that he should ask the Baal Shem Tov not to help them. And it was two or three days until [the messenger] revealed the request of the people of the city. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "I cleared some way by which I could help them; but since [these] Jews ask that I not help them, I will not help them." And this incident was after the twenty-two years had passed, as the Baal Shem Tov had written to him.

---

## §174

Once, on the Sabbath before Passover, the rabbi [the Baal Shem Tov] prayed the morning prayer, and after the prayer he removed the prayer shawl and went outside, and they supposed that he needed to relieve himself. When he was in the courtyard of the synagogue, he saw that they were leading a bear, with youths running after it as is the way. And he too went after it. After this they led the bear from the gate of the synagogue courtyard into the market, and he too went after it, and it was a great wonder in the eyes of the people. And they led the bear opposite the judge's [magistrate's] house, and after this the bear broke the rope and became as one gone mad. And all the people who were following it fled out of fear that it would run through the whole city and harm people.

But the rabbi stood at his post, and the bear ran into the judge's courtyard and entered his vestibule. And it opened the door of the house by force, and at once the Baal Shem Tov stood by the window to see what it would do, and also several men and most of the people stood by the windows to see the deed the bear would do. And at once the bear removed the table from its place and raised the floor and dug in the ground until it found a certain dead *mamzer* [of illegitimate birth] hidden under the ground. And the [bear] took the *mamzer* on its two hands [paws] and raised it up before the eyes of all. And the rabbi cried out, "You are witnesses" — in the Polish tongue, "*protest*" — for [the judge's] intention had been to make a [blood] libel against the Jews. And he went to the synagogue to complete the prayer.

---

## §175

Once, on the rabbi's journey, he lodged in a village with one of his men, who made a great meal in his honor. Meanwhile the rabbi spoke with him about himself, in the way of the world — whether he had a livelihood and how his standing was. And he recounted to him of his good standing, all of it. The rabbi said, "You have good horses." The rabbi said, "Let us go to see your horses." And they went to their stables, and a small horse pleased him, and the rabbi asked the householder to give him the horse as a gift. He answered him, "My lord, do not ask of me this horse, for it is precious in my eyes and very dear; for at every time and difficult place where three good horses cannot pull the wagon, it pulls by itself, and this happened to me several times; therefore the love of the horse is fixed in my heart." After an hour they spoke again of his good standing — that he also had many debts owed by people [to him]. The rabbi said, "I ask that you show me your promissory notes," and he showed him all the notes. And when he saw a certain note, the rabbi asked him to give him that note as a gift. And he answered, "What does the rabbi want with this note, since the debtor died several years ago and nothing remained after him with which to pay?" The rabbi said, "Even so I want this." And he gave him the note as a gift, and [the rabbi] took the note and tore it into two pieces and forgave the dead man with complete forgiveness. After this the rabbi said, "Go now and see the state of the horse." And he came and saw, and behold, the horse lay dead, and he understood that it was no empty matter. And the rabbi said that the man whom you held in debt, and who had nothing with which to pay you — his judgment was decreed that he give you satisfaction and serve you for the money, and he was reincarnated in the horse; therefore he gave you satisfaction, doing hard labor; and when his obligation was forgiven, he returned to his place, and the life-force of the horse was cut off, and therefore it died entirely.

---

## §176

I heard from the rabbi of our community: Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the holy community of Polonnoye and traveled from that holy community to the town of Chudnov, and the road is by the cemetery of Chudnov, and the Baal Shem Tov saw a great pillar of fire standing over a certain grave. And he sent the servant to see on the gravestone who was buried there, and it was written that a certain righteous man was buried there, called Moshe, servant of the Lord. And the rabbi said that beside him a certain man named R. David would be buried. And there was with them in the city a certain man called R. David Tzeres, and he was greatly alarmed when he heard this thing, for he supposed that the Baal Shem Tov was hinting at him, that he would die. The rabbi said to him, "Do not fear; you are not the David I spoke of." And about twelve years after the passing of the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, the rabbi R. David Forkes came to the holy community of Polonnoye for the Sabbath of [the haftarah of] Vision [before the Ninth of Av]. And he stopped in his wagon before the rabbi of that holy community [the Maggid] and showed his face to him and took leave of him to spend the Sabbath in the town of Chudnov, and he came to the house of the rabbi there and did not find him at home, for he had traveled on the road, and he spent the Sabbath there. And R. David kept wondering at himself all the Sabbath day and said, "I do not know what I am doing here; the rabbi the Maggid is not at home, and I came only to greet the rabbi, who was righteous." And on the night after the Sabbath, which was the Ninth of Av, he grew weak, and he called the [burial] society and made a [last] testament, and lay on his bed and passed away to his world on Sunday. And they buried him beside the grave of the aforementioned R. Moshe. And when they were in the cemetery they saw what was written on the gravestone, and it was a wonder in their eyes, and they recounted throughout the whole city this wonder. Then the mother of the preacher of Polonnoye, who was very old, remembered the words of the Baal Shem Tov, what he said more than twenty years [before] — that beside this grave R. David would be buried. And they saw that it was the word of the Lord.

---

## §177

Once the Baal Shem Tov went with the rabbi R. David Forkes to the bathhouse, and on their way a handsome man came toward them, his locks arranged in curls and in fine garments. And when the man came near them, the Baal Shem Tov leaped to the side, very far off; and when [the man] passed before them, the Baal Shem Tov returned and passed his hands over his eyes and said, "See who this is that passed before us." And he saw that he was a brand plucked from the fire — at all his edges the fire had consumed [him].

---

## §178

Once the rabbi traveled, and on the road he smoked the pipe, and the pipe-stem was very long, so that the pipe extended out of the wagon. And as they traveled thus, a certain minister and two soldiers riding on horses came toward them, and they took the pipe from him and traveled on their way, and the rabbi traveled on too. And after an hour the rabbi stopped and said to his servant, "Take a horse and ride upon it to overtake the soldiers, and take the pipe from them," and so he did. And when he overtook them, he saw that they were sitting on their horses and sleeping, [and] he took the pipe and went on his way.

---

## §179

Once a certain man traveled to the Baal Shem Tov for the Day of Atonement. And behold, on the day before the eve of the Day of Atonement, the man traveled the whole day and the whole night in order to hasten his journey there, and he came at the light of morning of the eve of the Day of Atonement, one *parsah* near the city of Mezhibuzh. Then he said to himself, "Behold, it is near the city, and the horse is very weary, for it has traveled all night; I will stop here to pray, and meanwhile the horse will graze." After the prayer, sleep overcame him. He said in his heart, "What of it if I sleep here two hours? The horse will rest from its weariness, and then afterward I will travel quickly, and however it be, I will surely come to the city before midday." And he lay on the wagon and fell into a deep sleep, and when he woke from his sleep he saw that it was the twilight of the Day of Atonement. And he was very greatly grieved, for after all the toil he had labored to travel to the Baal Shem Tov for the Day of Atonement, he had to be on the Day of Atonement by himself in the field near the city, and through his own negligence. And he cried out and wept in the bitterness of his heart the whole night and the whole day. And on the night after the Day of Atonement he traveled quickly to the Baal Shem Tov in the bitterness of his soul, and when he came to the Baal Shem Tov, [the Baal Shem Tov] laughed at him. And the matter was that he had needed to raise up, in his prayer, the prayer of the people in the fields, and from heaven they decreed it upon him for this purpose.

---

## §180

Once the Baal Shem Tov sat at a meal with his men, and in the middle of the meal he raised his hands and made many movements like a man swimming in a river, and said, "Fool, do thus and thus and you will be saved!" And it was a wonder in the eyes of those who saw. And after about an hour a certain man came and recounted how he had fallen with his wagon into a river, and he did not know how to swim, and at once it came into his mind, "Let me try to do with my hand thus and thus," and, thank God, it helped me and I came out of the river.

And likewise once at a meal he cried out and said, "Fool, did you not see how many gentiles are in the field? Take your *bagel* and throw it before their eyes, and they will come to you!" And likewise after an hour a certain man came and said that he had fallen into a river and there was no one to save him, for the gentiles were on the hill and he was in the stream and they did not see him. And he remembered that he had a *bagel* with him, and he threw the *bagel* to the gentiles, and at once they came and saved him, with God's help.

---

## §181

Once, at the time of the assembly of the Four Lands [the central Jewish council], the chief warden was the famous magnate rabbi R. Avraham Abba. He presented the matter before them all: "Behold, the fame of the Baal Shem Tov is heard, and by what is heard he is an ignoramus — and how can it be that he has the holy spirit, for an ignoramus is not pious?" And they sent from the assembly to the Baal Shem Tov that he come at once before them, and so he did. And on his coming before them, the aforementioned rabbi was the head of all the speakers. He said to the Baal Shem Tov, "By the manner of your conduct it appears that the holy spirit rests upon you, and some say that your honor is an ignoramus; therefore let us hear from your mouth whether your honor knows some law."

And the incident was on the New Moon. He asked him, "If one forgot [to say] 'May there ascend and come' [the New Moon addition], what is the law?" And the Baal Shem Tov answered, "This law is needed neither by your honor nor by me. For your honor, even if he returns and prays [again], will forget once more (for in truth so it was, that he forgot 'May there ascend and come' the second time too), and I will surely not forget." And when he told him the truth, [R. Avraham] was greatly alarmed at his knowledge of what was hidden. And at once he resolved in his mind to go ahead of the Baal Shem Tov on the road, to the place where they would stop, in order to see his conduct. And he hid himself in a certain room and saw through the hole what [the Baal Shem Tov] did. And when he saw that [the Baal Shem Tov] was near to setting out, he traveled on again from there to the lodging house, to the place where [the Baal Shem Tov] would lodge, and hid himself in secret. And when the Baal Shem Tov came to the lodging, they set up for him an honored bed; and when the Baal Shem Tov came and saw the bed, he cried out, "Woe, on this bed they had relations with a gentile woman — how can I sleep on it?" And at once the householder came and recounted to him with a great complaint about his son-in-law, how he had had relations with a gentile woman on this bed. And at once the Baal Shem Tov said, "R. Abba, did you hear what he says?" And at once he came before him.

---

## §182

Once, on Simchat Torah, the men of the holy society, disciples of the Baal Shem Tov, were rejoicing and dancing in a circle, and the Divine Presence was blazing around them.
