And in the midst of their dancing, the shoe of the youngest of the company tore — and he was a very poor man — and it greatly grieved him, his being prevented from dancing with his fellows to rejoice in the joy of the commandment on account of this. And [the Baal Shem Tov's] righteous daughter, Mistress Adel, was also standing in the house at the side to watch their joyous celebration. She said to that disciple, "If you promise me that I will bear a male child this year, I will give you at once good shoes" — for she had shoes in [her] shop — and he promised her that she would surely have a male child. And so it was, that the rabbi R. Baruch of the holy community of Tultshin was born to her.

---

## §183

In the holy community of Konstantin Yashan there was a certain man who was bereft of children, and he had been before the Baal Shem Tov several times. Once [the Baal Shem Tov] promised him that his wife would bear a male child, and commanded him to give him word when she gave birth. And so it was, that his wife bore a male child, and he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov to bring him the news of his wife's birth-giving with great joy. And when he came to the Baal Shem Tov and brought him this news, [the Baal Shem Tov] restrained himself for a long while and began to weep a great weeping, and his hands grew very slack. And [the man] asked him, "Why, my lord, are you weeping?" And he said, "Because I see that on the day the child becomes bar mitzvah, he will drown in a river, God spare us." And the Baal Shem Tov said, "Surely you will forget this entirely; but let this be a sign for you: that on that day he will put both stockings on one foot and search for the second one. And if you guard him then the whole day with excellent watching, so that he not see the face of the water, then 'when judgment is annulled, the judgment is void'; but if you take your eye off him, then he will surely drown in the river, God spare us."

And in the course of time the whole matter was entirely forgotten from him. And on the day [the child] became bar mitzvah, the man went to pray in the synagogue, and the lad was still asleep; and when he came from the synagogue, the lad was searching and going from corner to corner. His father asked him, "What do you seek?" and he said, "A stocking of mine is lost." And his father looked, and behold, he was wearing both stockings on one foot; and when he saw this deed he remembered the law [the prediction], and he set his heart to guard the child that day. And on that day the sun grew very hot, and all the people of the city ran to the river to bathe. And the child too slipped away and ran to the river, and at once they ran after him and returned him to his house. And when his father saw that he was pressing himself greatly to bathe in the river, and that it was impossible to guard him, he put him in a certain room and shut [it] behind him. And all day [the child] cried out bitterly, "Leave me — where is your mercy, like the mercy of a father for sons, for the sun has scorched me," but they paid him no heed and did not give him even a little water to drink. And after the afternoon prayer, when the people of the city were all at the river, the likeness of a head with two hands rose up, and it struck with its hand upon the water and said, "This one of mine is not here," and at once it sank and was seen no more. And at once the lad rested from his fever and slept. And, with God's help, he grew up in goodness to length of days, as the Baal Shem Tov, of blessed memory, had said.

---

## §184

We heard in the name of the holy rabbi R. Menachem Nachum of Chernobyl, of blessed memory, that in a certain community near the holy community of Mezhibuzh there was a certain distinguished and very wealthy man, who was a merchant to Leipzig, and he had been bereft of children for several years and did not wish in any way to travel to the Baal Shem Tov. Once he traveled on some matter to the holy community of Mezhibuzh and lodged there with someone who was a very faithful friend of his, and they asked after each other's welfare, and he recounted to him all his affair. And the man said to him, "Behold, all the world travels to the Baal Shem Tov, and why are you slack about going to him? Perhaps it will be a time of favor and you will be remembered for children." And he could not refuse him, and the two of them went together to the Baal Shem Tov. And [the Baal Shem Tov] said to him, "My counsel is that you give up your commerce to Leipzig and sell all the merchandise and wagons, and take the lease with your minister, and within this year your wife will bear a male child, for good fortune; and when she bears, then inform me." And his heart sank, for he did not believe him, especially since the aforementioned minister was a hater of Israel, who for fifteen years had not wished any son of Israel to dwell in his villages.

When he came home he did not wish to go to the minister, for he supposed that surely these were idle words. And after two days the minister himself sent for him and gave him the lease, as the Baal Shem Tov had said; and he supposed, "Perhaps the minister has great need of money today, and it pressed him to transgress his way [his policy]." And within the year his wife bore a male child. But it did not enter his mind to inform the Baal Shem Tov, for he said, "It is chance." And the child grew up, going and growing, and became literally a wild man — his hand against all and the hand of all against him — and it was impossible to lead him to the schoolhouse. And he literally loathed his life and greatly regretted his beginning [wish], that he had desired children; for it would have been better for him had he no children at all, for in truth his death was better than his life.

Now in the course of time it happened that he traveled to Mezhibuzh and lodged with his aforementioned friend, and recounted to him the distress of his heart. And [the friend] said to him, "Behold, you acted foolishly in not informing the Baal Shem Tov at once when your wife gave birth, as you were commanded by his holy mouth; but even so, let us now go to him and ask his mouth." And when they came before him, the man began to apologize before him for not having informed him then. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "My counsel is that you leave the lease and sell all the grain and goods for cash, and travel to Leipzig, and delay on the road for the space of a year; and when you come home, you will find your son turned into literally another man, and he will be distinguished in Torah." And he did as his words, and sold all that he had for ready money and traveled to Leipzig, and did his work slowly, slowly, and bought little by little until he bought all his merchandise well. And behold, all the merchants traveled, each to his place, and he alone remained, and he said in his heart, "What more shall I do here alone, and the money too is gone?" And although only four months had yet passed, nevertheless he placed all the merchandise in the wagon and traveled on his way with his wagon-driver.

Now in their traveling on Friday, the Sabbath eve, they strayed from the road and traveled the whole day and found no place to lodge; and toward evening they agreed that the wagon would stop on the road, and this one would turn to the right and the second turn to the left to search [for] whether they could find some resting place that would be good for them. And when he had gone from the road a hundred cubits and more, he looked, and behold, there stood in the forest a small house with candles burning there, and he took heart and went there. And when he entered there, he saw a certain man standing and praying and a certain man standing and serving; and since he had taken nothing with him from the wagon, the servant at once went and brought him a shirt and garments for the Sabbath. And he stood and prayed too, and after the receiving of the Sabbath and the evening [prayer] the householder said, "A good Sabbath," with great joy, and they brought them wine for Kiddush, and delicacies of kings — nothing was lacking in it. And after this they brought him an honored bed; and when the householder saw that the guest was hesitating to sleep on the bed, he said, "We have a secluded woman [in confinement]" — and in truth he saw no woman there except the householder and the servant. And they rose early in the morning and prayed the morning and Additional prayers as is proper, and ate and drank and made their hearts merry and slept the midday sleep. And because he had drunk much wine, he slept until the afternoon prayer, and they prayed the afternoon prayer and ate the third meal and made their hearts merry until evening, and prayed the evening prayer and made Havdalah. And behold, after Havdalah the householder bade the guest "peace" [greeting] — for on the Sabbath one does not give greeting. And the guest was greatly worried, for he did not know [the whereabouts] of his wagon-driver and his merchandise. And the householder said to him, "If you wish, sell me your merchandise on this condition: that I pay half the merchandise in cash and half on credit until the Leipzig fair, to pay there." And although the matter was very hard for him, to give credit to a man he did not know who he was, nevertheless he was compelled in his deeds, and an agreement was made between them on the price; and after they had agreed on the price, he looked out the window, and behold, his wagon was standing with the wagon-driver and all the merchandise by the house. And he went out to him and was very angry at him, but he could not change the matter; and he was thinking in his heart in what place he would put the merchandise, for there was no building there except the house alone. And the householder said, "Give me the merchandise," and they began to unload the merchandise, and they opened a door to a cellar and placed there all the merchandise. And they made a reckoning between them, and it came to a great sum, and [the householder] paid him the half in cash, about eighty thousand gold coins, and for the second half he gave him a note.

And as they traveled from there, he said in his heart, "Behold, the Baal Shem Tov commanded me to delay a year on the road, and otherwise, behold, I have sold all the merchandise — what shall I do at home? It is better for me to travel once more to Leipzig and buy little by little all the merchandise I will need." And in his traveling he took his note and saw that it bore the signature "Nasyona." And he began to investigate and inquire after him, but no one knew him; and also in Leipzig, after he delayed the whole time of the fair and bought all the merchandise, he began to inquire after the man. And it became a laughingstock and mockery in the eyes of all who saw, how he had given credit of such a great sum to a man whose place and deeds were unknown. And when all the merchants began to return, each to his place, he too placed all his merchandise in wagons and traveled on his way. And when he came to the gate of the city, he looked, and behold, the man was standing opposite him, and his heart sank, for he did not recognize him. And the man said to him, "Behold, your honor holds a note of mine for the sum of eighty thousand gold coins; let your honor give me the note, and I will pay him in cash." And he asked him and said, "Pray tell me who you are," and he answered and said, "What is it to you to know who I am? Behold, I am paying in cash." But even so he greatly wished to ascertain the truth of him, and the man said to him, "If you give me my note, I will pay you; and if not, I will surely not pay — and do as is good in your eyes." And he gave him the note, and [the man] paid him the money down to the last penny. And after this [the man] said to him, "Convey for me 'peace' [greetings] to the Baal Shem Tov (may he live)." And he asked him, "How shall I say [it] to him in your name?" and he said to him, "Tell him, 'Jonah conveys peace to you.'" And he traveled on his way in joy and gladness of heart to his home, and found that his son had been turned into another man and was diligent in his study. And at once he traveled to the Baal Shem Tov and recounted to him the whole affair, and the Baal Shem Tov asked about Jonah, how his welfare was, and he said, "He commanded me to convey peace to my lord in his garden." And [the Baal Shem Tov] said, "Know that this is Jonah son of Amittai." Thus far.

---

## §185

In a certain community there were three brothers, distinguished scholars and God-fearing. Two of them were obedient to the command of the Baal Shem Tov, and the third was an opponent of his. And every time the Baal Shem Tov was in that holy community, his lodging was with the two brothers, and each time he would ask and inquire after the welfare of the third brother. Once he came to that holy community on Thursday, and before he entered the lodging he asked the people of the city about the welfare of the third brother, in these words: "How is my opponent doing?" And the people supposed that it would be as good news in his eyes [and] said that he was lying on his sickbed and had no power of speech. The rabbi said that they must go to him to visit his illness. And on Friday, the Sabbath eve, he said to the two brothers, by way of a question, whether it was possible to pray there with their brother on the holy Sabbath. And they said, "Had he been healthy, surely he would not let the rabbi pray with him; but now that he lies like a stone, who has none to turn him, what does he care? Surely it is possible, for there is a special room there to pray [in]." And they commanded that the holy ark and a Torah scroll be brought there, and he prayed there on Friday, the Sabbath eve, the afternoon and evening prayers, and in the morning he prayed the morning prayer and prolonged the prayer until two hours after midday. And before the reading of the Torah he went to the vestibule in order to cool himself, and at once he heard a sound of wailing, that the sick man was expiring. And he said to his men, "Let us go visit the sick man," and he sat by the sick man, and his mother said to him, "Why do you not give greeting to the rabbi?" but he answered her nothing. And she placed his hand into the rabbi's hand. The rabbi asked the sick man whether he had studied Gemara, and he was silent; and so several times he asked him, and he was silent, until the rabbi rebuked him, "Where is your proper conduct, that I ask of you and you answer me nothing?" Then he answered him, "I studied Gemara." And he asked him in which tractate is the statement "Are sufferings dear to you?" He said to him, "In tractate Berachot." "And what is said there?" He said to him, "Neither them nor their reward, etc." The rabbi asked him, "Are sufferings dear to you?" and he answered, "Neither them nor their reward." The rabbi said to him, "Give me your hand!" He gave him his hand, and he raised him up. After this the rabbi commanded him to lower his feet from the bed, and he did so. After this the rabbi commanded that he clothe himself little by little and wash his hands, and he did everything as he commanded him; and after this he went with him to the house of prayer and prayed the Additional prayer with everyone, and he recovered, with the help of God, blessed be He.

---

## §186

Once [someone] was recounting in praise of the rabbi R. Nachman of Horodenka in his presence, while [R. Nachman] was sitting far off from him; then he drew himself near and inclined his ear to hear. And it was a wonder in the eyes of the people, and they asked the rabbi [the Baal Shem Tov], and he said to them that the rabbi R. Nachman had asked of the Blessed Name that it be given him as a gift that he hear only what he needs to hear. "And this that I recount in his praise — he does not hear; and when he saw my lips moving but did not hear my voice, he supposed that I was speaking words of Torah; therefore he inclined himself to hear from near. And the proof of this: when I speak words of Torah, then he hears even from afar."

And in another manner I heard, that the rabbi did this on purpose; for when he recounted his praise, [R. Nachman] saw his lips moving but did not hear his voice, then [R. Nachman] inclined himself to hear, and it became a laughingstock in the eyes of all. And the rabbi did this because it was not pleasing in the rabbi's eyes that [R. Nachman] had asked of the Blessed Name to hear only the needful thing, saying: either way — if he is worthy of this according to his deeds, the Holy One, blessed be He, will not withhold his reward, as we find with Abraham our father, peace be upon him, that after the circumcision five [more] limbs too were given into his control, the power of hearing being one of them; and if it is not according to his deeds, why should he rise in levels?

---

## §187

A certain man was among those close to him and dwelt in a village, and the rabbi always drew him near with all kinds of drawing-near, and on the Days of Awe his lodging was in the rabbi's house. Once he came to the rabbi, and [the rabbi] turned his face from him. And it was a wonder in his eyes, and he supposed in his mind, "Perhaps his thought is roaming in great matters." And because his disposition was [familiar] with him, after an hour he entered to him again, and [the rabbi] turned his face from him too. And so he did three times, and his heart sank greatly, for surely it was no empty matter. And he saw that this was a new thing, for [the rabbi] was accustomed to draw him near with all kinds of drawing-near. And he went to the rabbi R. Gershon of Kotov and recounted to him the whole affair. The rabbi R. Gershon went to his brother-in-law the Baal Shem Tov and said to him, "Why do you keep him distant for no violence in his hands, and all his deeds being proper?" And he answered him, "As for your wishing to bring me into the affairs of the world, to draw near and speak with [people] — I do not desire this."

Now when the aforementioned man saw that he had no remedy, he came to the rabbi R. Gershon and cried out with a bitter and broken heart, "I understand that this is no empty matter; literally my life is no life, and I cannot bear this in any way." Then the rabbi R. Gershon went to the rabbi a second time and said that it was literally a matter of life and death, that he reveal to his ear what this was and on account of what. Then he answered him that there was marked on his forehead the sin of [relations with] a married woman. And the man argued that this was not true, for it was more than sixteen years that he had separated from his wife, and how could this be? Then the rabbi R. Gershon said to the rabbi that it was incumbent upon him [the Baal Shem Tov] to investigate this, "for behold, I see nothing — only you see, alone; and surely the sin is not in a physical [act] but in subtle matters." And he said to him that on Friday, the Sabbath eve, at the afternoon prayer — for that is [a time of] ascent of the soul to the upper palaces — he would investigate this.

And in the ascent of his soul he ascended to the palace of the Tosafists and found no mark of the sin that he had sinned. And after this he ascended higher, to the palace of Maimonides, and there he found written the mark of the sin upon that man. And the matter was that he had taken upon himself separation from his wife, and according to the opinion of Maimonides it is as though he said, "Behold, you are to me as a mother," and one who vows [abstinence] from his wife is forbidden [to benefit from] her ornaments. And he had needed money for the marrying-off of a [poor] bride, and he took his wife's ornaments and pawned them, and it turned out that he derived benefit from her and transgressed his vow, and it is as though he had relations with a married woman, according to the opinion of Maimonides. And the rabbi argued with Maimonides (and I also heard that he summoned the Rif and the Rosh, and they too argued with him); then Maimonides conceded to their words, and the mark departed from that man. And from then on there is not found in the book of Maimonides any hint of this.

(And the matter is as R. Chaim Vital wrote on the statement "R. Shimon opened, etc." — meaning that he opened the channel to expound this. For a righteous man has the power to open new channels, which is not so with Elijah, who is of the aspect of an angel — of whom it is said "by the mouth of Elijah it was decreed." And as is said in the name of the Baal Shem Tov on the statement "The whole world is sustained on account of Chanina My son" — meaning, on account of what he made [opened]: when R. Chanina ben Dosa revealed a certain secret, then a path and channel was made for the whole world, and it became easy that other people too would understand this. And as is told, that the rabbi the Beit Yosef labored to understand a certain statement, and when he ascertained its truth, he heard [it] from several men of lower rank too; and he asked the Ari, who answered him, "Since you opened the channel, it is easy for everyone to grasp." And this is [the saying], "Had I not lifted up the potsherd for you, you would not have found the pearl beneath it." And therefore, when Maimonides too conceded to this ruling, then of itself in this world too the plain sense is understood according to Maimonides, as his judgment was concluded in the upper world — and a word to the wise is sufficient.)

---

## §188

The rabbi R. Nachman of Kosov was an opponent of the Baal Shem Tov (and I heard that the Baal Shem Tov was of the soul of David, king of Israel, and the rabbi R. Nachman of the soul of King Saul, peace be upon him; on account of this he was hostile to David all the days). Once the Baal Shem Tov said, "The rabbi R. Nachman pursues me to kill me" (as is known that there is an intention in [the verse] *Nefilat Apayim* to slay the enemy), "but he will not overtake me, with God's help."

Once the disciples approached their rabbi, the aforementioned R. Nachman, and said to him, "What is this — that all the world travels to the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov and marvels greatly at his praise? Why do you not come to a common ground to take his measure, that we may know the truth, with whom [it is]? Why should this be a stumbling block for us?" And he gave place to their words and traveled to the holy community of Mezhibuzh, to the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov, who received him with great honor. After this the two of them went to a special room and put every man out from them, except for one man who hid himself in such-and-such a place. And the rabbi R. Nachman said, "Israel, is it true, as you say, that you know the thoughts of a person?" He said to him, "Yes." He said to him, "Then do you know what my thought is now?" And the rabbi answered, "Surely it is known that the thought is not at rest and roams among many things, putting off one form and donning another; if you concentrate your thought on one thing, then I will know." And the rabbi R. Nachman did so. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "The Name Havayah is in your thought." And the rabbi R. Nachman said, "This you could understand of yourself, for behold, this thought I must think always, as it is written, 'I have set the Lord before me always'; and of itself, when I lay aside all thoughts and concentrate my thought on one thing, the Name Havayah must be before my eyes." And the Baal Shem Tov said, "Behold, there are several holy Names, and you could intend [meditate on] whichever you wish." Then the rabbi R. Nachman conceded that it was as his words. And after this they spoke of the secrets of the Torah.

---

## §189

Once the Baal Shem Tov saw that the rabbi R. Nachman was traveling on his way, and the Baal Shem Tov traveled after him too, so that R. Nachman would come to the lodging first for the Sabbath, and he would come after him and be a guest with him, and R. Nachman would have no fish for the Sabbath, and it would be a disgrace to him. And when the rabbi R. Nachman saw that the Baal Shem Tov was pursuing him, he twisted the road into crooked paths and traveled two days with no road and without any village. And the Baal Shem Tov recognized his way and traveled after him too. Now on Thursday toward evening the rabbi R. Nachman came to a certain village, and a certain Jew dwelt there; and immediately on his coming there he asked whether it was possible to obtain fish here, for on the Sabbath eve he would not travel from there. And the householder said to him that it was impossible to obtain [it] in any way, for it was far from every side, about twenty *parsah* or more, from rivers. And the rabbi R. Nachman asked him how it was possible for a Jew to be without water for drinking and for the washing of hands and for immersion. And he answered that he had two pools of water — one of clear water for drinking and one of murky water for washing and immersion. And he said, "If so, I will surely obtain fish." And he said at once to his servant, "Behold, I once saw a fisherman who took a rod and wound a cord on it and cast it into the river and caught fish with it; therefore take a rod and cord and go to the pool at once and do so." And he did so, and brought up from the pool a large fish. And the next day, when [R. Nachman] saw that the Baal Shem Tov was about to come near him, he went toward him in joy to greet him and gave him peace. And he said to him, "With God's help, I have fish for the Sabbath." And [the Baal Shem Tov] answered him, "Thanks [to you] for sending donces [troubles?] upon me, that I was compelled to busy myself on their account, and on account of this you obtained fish; and otherwise you would surely not have obtained fish in any way."

---

## §190

Once the rabbi R. Nachman heard from the followers that they were speaking ill of the Baal Shem Tov, and it greatly grieved him for this. And he told them a parable about this: Once two great ministers made a crown for the king and spent much wealth on it. And when they came to finish the crown, a dispute fell between them as to how to set the precious stone — this one saying thus and that one saying thus — and both of them had as their intention the greatness of the king. And when a quarrel arose between them over this, one of the common folk passed by and said [a word] like that of one of them, and they rebuked him with reproof: "What is it to you here, to bring yourself into a quarrel that is not yours? If we dispute over this, behold, we know the greatness of the king very well, and according to his greatness it must be thus or thus." "And so too the dispute that is between us is an ancient dispute that was between Saul and King David, peace be upon him, and after this between Hillel and Shammai — and how can you bring your head into this?"

---

## §191

In Istanbul there was a certain very wealthy man, and he had an only son, distinguished, who suddenly became blind in his eyes. They asked the doctors to heal him, and after all the stratagems they made, nothing helped at all, and they despaired of his healing. And when the Baal Shem Tov was in that holy community, he said to the aforementioned man that he would heal him a complete healing. And [the man] rejoiced greatly at his words and brought him into his house with great honor. But when the wife of the aforementioned magnate saw [him], she despised him in her eyes, for he did not go in esteemed garments because of the wearisomeness of the road and the days; and she was angry at her husband and said, "Why did you bring him to me? Behold, all the doctors helped nothing, and what will this one accomplish with [magical] Names?" And she spoke against the holy Names, and the Baal Shem Tov was angered that she did not believe in the power of the working of God's Names. And he said, "Where is the blind child? Bring him to me and I will heal him so that he will see at once, in the manner of every person." And as he spoke, her spirit was stirred, for "one who wishes to lie sets his testimony far off," and he says that in this moment he [the child] will see — [so] it could be that he will verify his words. And at once they brought him the sick one, and he whispered something in his ears, and at once he commanded that a Gemara be brought before him, and commanded him to read, and he read in the manner of all the world. And there was great joy among the people of the house, and at once the rabbi passed his hand over [the child's] eyes, and he returned to as before [blind], and there was great weeping in the house. And they entreated him with bowing and prostration, and wished to give him a huge sum of money. And the rabbi answered the wicked woman, "You who mocked the holy Names — it is forbidden for you to benefit from them; for from the beginning I did not wish to do [this] for my own honor, Heaven forbid, or for money, but only for the sanctification of the Name, may He be blessed. I showed you with your [own] eyes the great power of the holiness of the Name; but you shall not benefit from them" — and he went from them thus.

---

## §192

In the lands of Wallachia there was a certain famous rich man, and suddenly his wife fell ill in both her hands, so that she could not raise them in any way. And he went with her to all the doctors, and they helped nothing, and they despaired of her healing. And he also inquired of the Tatars, and no cure arose for her affliction; and he resolved to travel with her from city to city, perhaps a cure would arise for her affliction through masters of the [holy] Name or doctors. And in his traveling he heard the fame of the Baal Shem Tov from afar, and he traveled to the holy community of Mezhibuzh. And when they came to that holy community, they went to the Baal Shem Tov, and he commanded that she dwell in that holy community, and she dwelt there many days. And in the course of time they came several times to the rabbi, and he commanded [her] to dwell longer. And as the days went on, once he commanded that the wagon with the horses be prepared, and commanded the aforementioned man that he too travel with his sick wife after them. And in their traveling they came to a certain village, and one of the Baal Shem Tov's men dwelt there; and when they came there, the rabbi asked the tax-farmer whether he would give him lodging. And it was a great wonder in the eyes of the tax-farmer at this question, for in truth it was a great privilege for him that the rabbi should lodge with him. And the rabbi answered him that when he lodged with him, the windows and gates and doors should be shut, not to open for any person, whoever it be, even some great minister; and if [someone] should press himself greatly, then to answer that the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov is here. And the tax-farmer promised to do so. And they stopped with him at the inn, and at once they did as the rabbi said and prepared themselves for the evening meal. And after the meal they all slept, except the Baal Shem Tov sat by the table and studied in a certain book, while the sick woman sat in the rear of the house.

Now on that day the brother of the minister came to that inn, [a brother] whom no man had seen — this brother of his — for more than twelve years, and there was great joy between them, and they ate and drank and became drunk. And in the midst of the talk, the minister began to boast how at a certain known place he had built a good and very splendid inn, and he praised it greatly. And the guest asked him to give him a horse to travel there, and they gave him a good and choice horse; and since the place was near the fortress, he traveled in just one garment. And at once when he traveled, a storm wind began, with snow called a *blizzard*, and he was delayed in his traveling several hours until he came to the inn. And he knocked on the door that they should open for him, and they answered him that it was impossible to open for him, because the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov was there. And he asked several times with great pleas to open for him, since he had grown very cold, and they answered him as above, until at the last time the Baal Shem Tov signaled that they should open for him.

And when the minister entered the house, he walked to and fro until he warmed a little, and when he returned to his [normal] state he asked who this Baal Shem Tov was, and they showed him the rabbi, and at once the fire of anger began to burn in him. And he walked again, time after time, and asked again who this is and which is the Baal Shem Tov, for whom they did not open the door for me, until it was literally possible to die from the great cold. And they showed him the rabbi, until at the third time the fire of anger burned in him completely, and when they showed him the rabbi he drew his sword from its sheath and raised his hand over the rabbi to kill him. At once the rabbi cried out to the sick woman, "Raise your two hands," and she did so. And at once an exchange was made: to the woman her hands returned to their health as at first, and the minister could not return his hand [lower it], and his hands withered. And he entreated the rabbi greatly, and [the rabbi] said, "I cannot help you, for the exchange has already been made, and it is impossible to change it any more."

---

## §193

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in a certain community and his lodging was in the upper [chamber]. Once he saw a certain man passing in the street, and at once he commanded his servant, "Run quickly and call me this man." And the lad ran and called him, and at first his heart was stirred [with fear] and he did not wish to go; but even so he could not be brazen, and he went to the rabbi. And when he came to the rabbi, the rabbi said, "You committed a transgression today." The man said, as one denying, "What is it to your honor?" The rabbi said, "Behold, you committed a great transgression, for you struck your father." And the incident that occurred was thus: for his father was old and shut up in the house that day. His father went to bring brandy into the house and tarried there, and the aforementioned man went to see what this was, that his father was delaying so, and saw that he was drinking brandy from a large vessel standing under the barrels, and the man was greatly angered and struck his father with that vessel on his head. And when [the rabbi] told him the truth, at once he wept a great weeping and asked that he give him a repentance for this. And he gave him a repentance, that he fast on Monday and Thursday and also stand in the synagogue in the place of the mourners, and other things; and he took upon himself to perform the order of repentance. And the rabbi warned him that if he changed anything, Heaven forbid, then he would not complete his year. And in the course of time, while he was performing the order of repentance, his mother began to speak with her son and to entice him, and she mocked the rabbi greatly until she enticed him with words, and he annulled the order of repentance. And so it came about for him, that he did not complete his year, God spare us. And so the rabbi of Turov recounted, that the incident was before him.

---

## §194

The aforementioned rabbi further recounted: Once at the third meal the rabbi [the Baal Shem Tov] said words of Torah, and they tarried several hours into the night. And he called his servant and commanded him to take from his chest several gold pieces and go to such-and-such a house, "for there a certain woman has given birth and has nothing to eat, not even the first meal." And at once the servant went there with the bundle of money, and the door was still shut, for they had not yet cut the [infant's] navel. And he gave her the money according to the rabbi's order.

---

## §195

Once the disciples of the Baal Shem Tov came to his house on the eve of Passover after midday to bake the matzah of the commandment, as is the custom to bake in assembly and with the recitation of Hallel. And behold, the Baal Shem Tov was walking in the courtyard of the synagogue to and fro, very preoccupied in the depth of thought. And the disciples waited several hours and were greatly grieved by this. Meanwhile they saw that the Baal Shem Tov saw the priest from afar and went to him and began to speak with him and to stroll with him at length and at short. And after this he brought him into his house and seated him and honored him with the honey-mead of Passover and spoke with him a great deal more. And after this the priest went and [the Baal Shem Tov] escorted him. After this he said, "Now quickly, quickly let us begin to bake the matzot, for the day has already turned toward evening." And the disciples asked him what this was, that he spoke with the priest very much. And he said that the priest had it in his mind to throw a stabbed *mamzer* in the street of the synagogue on the night of Passover, and to cast the blame on all the people of the city; and by speaking with him much and honoring him, it was uprooted from the priest's heart to do this.

---

## §196

Once the Baal Shem Tov was praying before the ark, and in the midst of the prayer he interrupted his prayer and went to the street in front of the synagogue. And he saw a certain gentile selling wood, and he bought from him a wagon of wood. And the gentile led the wood after him to the study hall, and the Baal Shem Tov commanded that they pay him for the wood; and for [the labor of] leading the wood to the study hall he commanded that they give him brandy. And the gentile said, "Blessed is the God of the Jews, that He has such a holy people!" — for if [the gentile] had bought from another gentile, surely he would have given him nothing for this. And the disciples asked the Baal Shem Tov why he interrupted in the middle of his prayer to buy the wood. And he answered that during his prayer he saw an accusation above against the people of the house of Israel who dwell in the villages — that they deceive the gentiles in the reckoning — and he had to stop the mouth of the accuser by this, that the gentile praised the Jews very much; by this the claims of the accuser were stopped.

---

## §197

An incident occurred: the preacher-rabbi R. Aryeh Leib of Polonnoye greatly longed to learn the wisdom of the speech of beasts and birds and the speech of palm trees, since he needed this for [his] reproof. And since his intention was for the sake of Heaven, he resolved to travel to the Baal Shem Tov and ask of him that he teach him these aforementioned wisdoms. And it was in his mind that surely the Baal Shem Tov would fulfill his wish in this, since his intention was for the sake of Heaven.

Now when he came to the Baal Shem Tov, into his room — and there were many people there — the Baal Shem Tov did not turn to him at all. And afterward, when he had stood there a long while, the Baal Shem Tov gave him greeting with a slack lip and offhandedly, and the aforementioned preacher was astonished at the sight, for he was close to him and the Baal Shem Tov used to cherish him. Nevertheless, out of the greatness of his longing to learn these wisdoms, he did not fall from his longing, and he delayed with him longer, for he said, "Perhaps it is chance, and I will find the fitting moment to come to him."

And one day a journey arose for the Baal Shem Tov to travel to the holy community of Kaminka from Miropoli, and the Baal Shem Tov said to the preacher to travel with him, and he was filled with joy, [thinking] that on the road he would surely obtain his request. And they traveled together, with several other men too. And on the road, at the lodging, the Baal Shem Tov went to sleep in a special room in the upper [chamber], and the preacher waited until the time of his waking from his sleep. And he went up the ladder to the upper chamber where the Baal Shem Tov was, and on coming to the top step he saw the Baal Shem Tov sitting and performing unifications, his face burning like torches, and he trembled greatly, until, out of the greatness of fear, he almost fell from the step, until the Baal Shem Tov rebuked him and said to him, "Stand, do not fear; what do you seek?" And out of fear he was struck dumb and did not answer. And when they traveled from the lodging, the Baal Shem Tov told the preacher to sit with him in his wagon. And he sat with him, and the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Behold, it is known to me that the whole essence of your coming to my camp was only that you might learn the wisdom of the speech of birds, etc. Come and I will teach you, [explaining] well."

And the opening was: Behold, it is known that in the upper Chariot is the face of an ox, the face of a man, the face of an eagle, the face of a lion. And behold, the choicest in the Chariot is the face of a man, and from it the life-force is drawn to the lower man; and from the face of the ox above, in the unfolding of the levels by way of cause and effect and many contractions, the life-force is drawn to all the beasts below; and from the face of the lion the life-force is drawn to all the [wild] animals below; and from the face of the eagle to the birds below. And this is the secret of *Perek Shirah* [the "Chapter of Song"], for according to the speech in the Chariot, which is the highest in every living thing, so it unfolds down below in beasts, animals, and birds. And behold, one who is wise and understands of his own mind and looks at each thing to its root above in the upper Chariot can know, from the source of the matter, all the particular modes of speech in beasts, animals, and birds. This is the general principle of the matter; and the particulars of the matter in this he told him — awesome and wondrous secrets — until the preacher knew the matter fully. And behold, the Baal Shem Tov, after he revealed to him the essential depth of the secret of this wisdom, was explaining to him, in passing, statements in the Zohar and the Tikunim, and [the preacher] was hearing them with one ear, while with his second ear he was hearing how the birds speak and how the beasts and animals speak. And all these secrets he was explaining to him on this journey, until near the city.

And when they came near the city, the Baal Shem Tov said to him, "Have you understood this wisdom well?" and he said to him, "Yes." And the Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over the preacher's face, and all the particulars of the secrets of this wisdom were forgotten from him, and there remained to his memory only the introduction. And the Baal Shem Tov was silent, and said, "Now, had it been necessary for you to know this wisdom — that which would be of benefit to you for the service of the Creator — of myself I would have hastened to teach you it. For this [reason] I taught you this wisdom: to slake your thirst; and it has been forgotten from you, for this is not part of your service. And 'you shall be wholehearted [with the Lord your God],' etc. — and a word to the wise is sufficient."

---

## §198

Once the rabbi the Baal Shem Tov sat at the third meal and was very preoccupied in the depth of thought, and he said no Torah at the third meal at all, which was a wonder in the eyes of the disciples. And after he recited the blessing of Havdalah — for his custom was every night after the holy Sabbath to travel outside the city about half a *parsah* and then return to the city — and on that night after the Sabbath he also said to prepare the wagon with the horses to travel, and that some of the disciples should travel with him. And he commanded that provisions be prepared for the road and that the chest with all the garments be taken, which was a wonder in the eyes of all his household; and he said nothing at all as to where he was traveling.

And when they traveled from the city gate, he commanded his disciples to turn their faces toward him, and the wagon-driver too he commanded to turn his face toward him and to tie up the reins, and the horses would go of themselves. And so it was; and the disciples saw that they were traveling from city to city — literally every quarter-hour they entered a [different] city — and they traveled thus the whole night until the day grew light, and they traveled a very great distance that night by the leaping of the way [*kefitzat ha-derech*]. And they came to a certain large city, and the horses stopped by a certain large and splendid house, and the Baal Shem Tov told the wagon-driver to drive into the courtyard with the horses. And the householder rose from his sleep and gave them greeting and asked them, "Whence are you?" They said to him, "From Volhynia," and he asked them whether they were merchants — [in which case] they should lodge in the lodging houses set aside for that — and if they were preachers and expounders, they should lodge in the house of the head of the rabbinical court. And the Baal Shem Tov answered him, "What is it to you? I will not delay but a few hours," and the householder went off.

And the Baal Shem Tov rose from his wagon and took his pipe and entered the kitchen to take from there a coal to draw the smoke of the tobacco. And behold, a certain young woman rose from her bed and came too to the kitchen, clothed in part of her garments. And the Baal Shem Tov said to her to take a coal and place it in his pipe, and she did so. And the Baal Shem Tov said to her, "Do you recognize me?" and she said, "No." And he said to her, "Behold, I am your uncle, and I dwell in Mezhibuzh; and what is your business here?" And the woman said, "I was the wife of the householder's son, and my husband died, and my aforementioned father-in-law wishes that I take his brother's son for a husband — and he is still a lad — and I cannot be brazen against him, but this is against my will." And the Baal Shem Tov said to her, "Do not worry; I will be a father to you. If you wish, travel with me today to my place, and there I will give you a good man for a husband as you wish." And the woman said, "Yes, I will travel," and he said to her, "If so, go prepare yourself, for we will not delay here." And the woman went and took her garments and her ornaments and put them in a certain chest.

And when her father-in-law saw her deeds, he asked her, "Where are you traveling, that you are preparing yourself?" And she said to him, "This man is my uncle, and he wishes to take me to his country and will give me a man there." And the householder was angered at the Baal Shem Tov and ran to him in his fury and said to him, "What is this you are doing to me — to take my daughter-in-law without my knowledge, with all the garments and ornaments?" And the Baal Shem Tov took him to a certain closed room and said to him, "Be it known to you that I am the Baal Shem Tov, and yesterday I went out from my house and traveled all this great distance by the leaping of the way. And the matter is that your daughter-in-law, after the death of her husband, became dissolute and committed adultery with several profligates; and, in short, recently she promised a certain minister to change her religion and marry him.

"And behold, yesterday, at the time of the third meal, there came to me from the upper world her elder grandfather, who was a famous righteous man and the great one of the generation, and he asked me to rectify his granddaughter, the aforementioned woman, who has a lofty soul that has fallen into the depths of the husks [the demonic shells], that it not remain there, Heaven forbid. And I promised him; and behold, the aforementioned woman has fixed the time for this very day, and surely after two hours the minister will come with soldiers and take the woman with all the garments and ornaments by a strong hand, and she will change her religion. Therefore I will counsel you, and may God be with you, and it will be reckoned a great commandment and the saving of souls." And his words entered the householder's heart, and he believed him; and the Baal Shem Tov promised him that he would return to him all the belongings the woman had taken that did not belong to her. And the Baal Shem Tov took the woman and led her back with him, and the disciples, who knew nothing of this, were astonished at the sight. And the whole way the Baal Shem Tov drew her near, and she called him "my uncle."

And when they came to the holy community of Mezhibuzh, the Baal Shem Tov made a match for her with a very honored man, a famous magnate. And at the time of the wedding, before the canopy, the woman came to the rabbi's room to confess before him and to ask repentance for her sins; for during her being in the rabbi's house, the evil that was in her had been uprooted from her heart. And when the Baal Shem Tov saw that she spoke from the point of her heart, then the rabbi said, "How can it enter your mind that I am your uncle? Know that there was never any such thing; only, because you had fixed a time to change your religion, and your elder grandfather asked me to save you — and if you do not repent properly, you will surely die at once." And she wept a very great weeping and asked him to give her a repentance. And the rabbi answered that this is her repentance: this match shall be annulled, for you are not worthy to marry such a man, since she knows the bitterness of her soul; rather, she shall marry a baker, and she will sit in the market with bagels. And all the belongings that her father-in-law gave her, she shall send back to him; for what is his offense and what is his sin? — and he gave [them] only on my assurance, in order to save her soul from the pit. And so she did, and accepted the repentance with a whole heart and became a completely righteous woman. After this her elder grandfather came to the Baal Shem Tov in a dream and said, "May your mind be at ease, for you have set my mind at ease."

---

## §199

Once, on the eve of the Day of Atonement before Kol Nidrei, the whole congregation gathered in the study hall. And the Baal Shem Tov stood and did not begin to pray, and it was recognizable in him that he was in great perplexity. And they waited a long while, and all the people burst into weeping, for they all understood that it was no empty matter. After this the rabbi looked from the window [and saw] how a certain old priest was walking in front of the study hall, and he went out toward him. And the rabbi began to speak with him and to ask after his welfare, and engaged him in conversation until he went with him to his house. And the rabbi spoke with him: "Why do you not take a wife? Behold, [the world] was 'not created to be void,' etc." And the priest answered that although his words are true, by their laws a priest is not permitted to take a wife. And the rabbi argued with him much about this — that at least in his old age he should fulfill the commandment "be fruitful and multiply" and renounce his priesthood. And the priest said that to take a wife of low degree was not befitting his honor, and from a fitting family the woman would not consent that he take her. And the rabbi said that with such-and-such a minister there is a beautiful daughter who would surely consent to give her to him for a wife; and he spoke with him much until he consented, and her beauty entered his heart, until, out of the abundance of his desire, he had a [seminal] emission. And at once the rabbi went to the study hall, and they began to pray Kol Nidrei. And after the prayer his treasured men came to him, and he recounted to them the affair: that there was a great accusation, so that it was not possible for the prayers to ascend above, because this priest was old and had never had an emission; therefore he was compelled to speak with him until he had an emission. And they asked him from where he knew that he had had an emission, and he said that at once it was no longer possible for him to stand near him. And, with God's help, the mouths of the accusers were stopped.

---

## §200

I heard that the great rabbi the Maggid of the holy community of Mezeritch did not himself sound [the shofar], but rather the holy rabbi R. Menachem Mendel, who passed away in the Holy Land, would sound, while [the Maggid], of blessed memory, would call out [prompt] the blasts. And even before his passing, when he was ill in his legs for several years, he would call out from his room. Once the rabbi R. Menachem Mendel was not in that holy community for Rosh Hashanah, and [the Maggid] commanded the great rabbi R. Levi Yitzchak, head of court of the holy community of Berditchev, that he sound. And he prepared himself for this and went in awe and fear and great trembling, as was his way. And at once, when [the Maggid] called out before him the first blast, he saw great lights and fainted. The rabbi the Maggid said, "I do not know what happened to him — and surely Mendel sees more and is not so frightened." And they were compelled to give the blasts to another man, who would not see and not be frightened.

---

## §201

Once the rabbi R. David Forkes dreamed that he came to the Baal Shem Tov and saw standing by him four men. Two of them he recognized, and the third was Elijah, of blessed memory, and the fourth was hidden from him, for he did not know who he was, and he was also ashamed to ask the Baal Shem Tov. Once, after [the Baal Shem Tov's] passing, he was with the aforementioned rabbi the Maggid, and asked him about [the fourth one's] appearance and form and movements. And when R. David recounted to him, the Maggid said that this was R. Yitzchak Luria [the Ari], of blessed memory.

Also, once they heard from the Baal Shem Tov, who said of the great rabbi R. Chaim Tzanzer of Brod that he was a spark of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai. The aforementioned Maggid said, "Why do you wonder at this? For he had literally the form of Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai."

---

## §202

Once the Baal Shem Tov was in the land of Wallachia, and they had there a "holy wine," which is that which drips from the grapes; and when one mixes into a cup two or three small drops, it is impossible to drink it because of its strength. And the householder honored the Baal Shem Tov with a small cup of this wine. And when he tasted it, the Baal Shem Tov said, "Behold, your wine is good — why is your cup small?" And the householder answered that it is dangerous to drink a large cup. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "I do not fear this," and they gave him a large cup, and he drank all of it. And they all gazed at him, [seeing] that his face became red and all his hairs stood up like literal fire, and they were all alarmed; but the Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over his face and dispelled his wine in a moment. And they wondered greatly at this, and he said that our Sages, of blessed memory, said, "Wine is hard [strong], [but] fear dispels it"; and when he contemplated the greatness of [God], may He be blessed, fear and trembling fell upon him from the greatness of [God], and it dispelled the wine entirely.

---

## §203

Once the Baal Shem Tov traveled with his disciples, and they were compelled to lodge in a forest. The disciples said in jest, "Let us see the meal that the rabbi will make for us." And the rabbi said, "Lodge here, and tomorrow, God willing, I will make you a great meal with several kinds of delicacies." And the next day they came to a large city and lodged in the house of a certain magnate. And to the aforementioned magnate had come his uncle, who was a rabbi in a certain community eighty *parsah* distant from him, and whom he had not seen for more than twelve years. And in his honor he made a great meal and invited all the people of the city. And behold, although they had heard the fame of the Baal Shem Tov, they did not esteem him so much. And after the prayer in the synagogue, all the people of the city came to his house, and his uncle the rabbi sat at the head of the invited guests, while the Baal Shem Tov was still praying in the rear of the house. And the uncle the rabbi asked, "Who is this who is still praying?" and the householder answered, "This is the Baal Shem Tov," and esteemed him for nothing. And his holy disciples too they did not call to recline with them.

And when the Baal Shem Tov finished his prayer, he came to the place of the rabbi and said to him, "I have a secret matter for you," and went with him to a secret place. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "Behold, your honor has a certain defect," and he said, "No." And the Baal Shem Tov answered, "Behold, every time you wish to concentrate in [reciting] 'Hear, O Israel,' there stands opposite you a defect of [...]," and [the rabbi] denied it to him. And the Baal Shem Tov answered, "How can it be a lie? Behold, you fasted for this eighty fasts." And he was compelled to admit it to him, and he wept before him that he help him. And [the Baal Shem Tov] said to him that his healing was that he not think suspiciously about the great righteous ones. And he accepted upon himself with a whole heart that he would not return to [this] folly. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "Today try to concentrate this intention, and you will see that he is restored and healed." And so it was. And at once he took the Baal Shem Tov by his hand and seated him at the head of the invited guests, and all his disciples too reclined at the head, and he honored them with great honor.

---

## §204

In the holy community of Mezhibuzh there was a certain man bereft of children, and he was always making mention [of himself] before the Baal Shem Tov, and [the Baal Shem Tov] promised him that he would surely have children. And when he came on in years, he troubled the Baal Shem Tov several times, and, with God's help, his wife bore him a male child in her old age. And the days were not long before [the child] died within the days [following] the birth. And at once he came to the Baal Shem Tov with commotion and anger: "Why do you delude me, to give me a false thing that is cut off from me at once? Surely it would have been better for me had I no children at all." And the Baal Shem Tov answered, "Did I not say that you would have children? Surely the child will live." And he waited a long while, but the child did not rise and did not move, and there was no breath of life in it; and [the man] had no face to speak any more with the Baal Shem Tov, after he promised him with a faithful promise. And he came to the rabbi and spoke with him from one side, and asked him whether he should prepare himself for the circumcision and whom he should honor [with the honors]. And [the rabbi] commanded him to prepare himself as is customary, and the [honor of] *sandak* he commanded him to give to a known man, and likewise the cutting, and for himself [the rabbi] the *metzitzah* [suction]; and so they did. They brought the child to the synagogue, and [the rabbi] circumcised it, and no blood came out, as [when] cutting the flesh of a dead [body]. And the rabbi began to recite the blessings after the circumcision, and when he said the words "Sustain this child for his father and his mother," he lingered a long time, until the child's soul returned within it and blood spurted from the circumcision up to the ceiling beam; and, with God's help, the child lived completely.

---

## §205

Once a certain great and famous doctor came to the duchess of the city. And the duchess boasted in praise of the Baal Shem Tov, that he is a great man and also expert in remedies. The doctor said, "Send for him to come here." And she said, "This is not his honor — rather, to send for him an honored wagon, in the manner of the nobles, for he is a great man." And she sent for him, and he came before them. And the doctor asked the rabbi whether it was true that he was expert in remedies. And he answered him, "It is true." And he said, "In what place and from what expert did you learn?" And he said, "The Blessed Name taught me," and it was a laughingstock in the eyes of the doctor. And he asked him further whether he understood [diagnosis] by the pulse. And the rabbi said, "Behold, I have a certain defect — understand it by my pulse, and I will see and discern your pulse." And the doctor took the rabbi's pulse [and] understood that there was a certain defect, but he did not know what it was, for in truth it was the illness of lovesickness for the Blessed Name, which is above his understanding. After this the rabbi took the doctor by his pulse, and the rabbi turned his face to the duchess and asked her, "Were such-and-such precious belongings stolen from you?" And she said, "Yes, it is several years that they were stolen, and I do not know where they are." And the rabbi said, "Send to his [the doctor's] lodging and open his chest, and you will find all the belongings intact." And she sent at once and found [it] according to his holy words. And the doctor traveled from there in disgrace and contempt. (And the matter is that the "back" [aspect] of [the supernal] Abba is [the numerical value] 184, the letters [spelling] *kefak*; and therefore the righteous would recognize, by the pulse [*defek*], all the sins, as is written in the holy Zohar and the Tikunim.)

---

## §206

Once, on the Baal Shem Tov's journey, he lodged with the rabbi of a certain community, that he might spend the Sabbath there. The rabbi said to his wife, "Know how to honor him, for he is a great and famous man." And his wife said, "Behold, with God's help, we have bought meat; we will choose from it every good cut in honor of the guest." And when she went to look at the meat she found nothing, and her heart was literally broken within her. And she went to her neighbor, [thinking] perhaps she could borrow from her, but the neighbor too found nothing of what she had prepared. And she went also to a second [neighbor], and they found nothing. And she took counsel with her husband what to do. And behold, meanwhile she saw the town slaughterer passing before the window, and she recounted to him the distress of her heart, of all that had befallen her. And he said, "Behold, it is two or three hours since I slaughtered a calf, tender and good." And the Baal Shem Tov answered and said, "I very much love to eat the head; if it is possible for it to come here whole, and he will trim it here." And the slaughterer said, "The matter is good," and rose and went diligently and brought the head to the rabbi's house. The Baal Shem Tov began to speak with the slaughterer, "People say that there is a certain discernment and distinction in the teeth; pray discern [and] count how many teeth it has." And he opened the mouth of the animal and put his hand into it to count the teeth. And at once it pressed his hand so that he could not draw his hand out from there, and it pressed his hand very hard, until he could not bear it, and he cried out greatly. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "What is with you, that you cry out? Behold, there is no breath of life in it." And the Baal Shem Tov cried out at him, "Wicked one, give acknowledgment!" And he confessed his sin: that he had never inspected any lung — what he wished he would declare fit, and what he wished he would declare unfit. And [the Baal Shem Tov] gave him a repentance for this. And after this they went to the synagogue, and the Baal Shem Tov passed his hands over the face of the rabbi, and [the rabbi] saw several destroyers and spirits sitting in the synagogue, and he wondered greatly at this. And the Baal Shem Tov answered that the prayer leader, before his prayer, has an emission each time, and they removed him.

---

## §207

I will write a little of his passing — that his departure was on the first day of Shavuot. On the Passover before this, the rabbi R. Pinchas of Korets was with the Baal Shem Tov and grew a little weak; and because of this, on the last festival day of Passover, there was a weighing back and forth with him whether to go to the ritual bath or not, and he resolved not to go. And on the seventh day of Passover, during the prayer, he saw how it had been decreed upon the Baal Shem Tov that he would soon pass away, because of his great struggle against the sect of the prayer leader [Shabbetai Tzvi], as is explained above in the tale of the Day of Atonement, when they wished to burn the Babylonian Talmud. And the rabbi R. Pinchas began to struggle in prayer, but it did not help, and he was greatly grieved that he had not gone to the ritual bath; for had he seen this matter in the ritual bath, in his opinion it would have helped for this. And after the prayer the Baal Shem Tov asked him whether he had gone to the ritual bath yesterday, and he said no. And the Baal Shem Tov said, "The deed is already done, and after the deed there is nothing." And after Passover he had the illness of the flux [dysentery]; nevertheless he strengthened himself a little to pray before the ark, and those disciples who were known to struggle in prayer he informed of nothing, and he sent them to other places, and the aforementioned rabbi R. Pinchas did not travel home.

On the night of Shavuot his treasured men gathered to remain awake the whole night according to the rite of the Ari, of blessed memory. And he said before them words of Torah on the matter of the [Torah-reading] order and the matter of the giving of the Torah. In the morning he sent to his treasured men that they all gather, and he commanded R. Leib Kessler and another, whose name I have forgotten, to occupy themselves with his burial, and he taught them [using] his [own] body. On each limb he showed them signs how his soul was withdrawn and departed from this limb, and afterward from another limb, in order that they might understand [the process] with the sick, for they were of the men of the burial society. And he commanded that a quorum come in to pray with him, and commanded that they give him the prayer book, and he said, "I will yet converse a little with the Blessed Name." And after the prayer R. Nachman of Horodenka went to the study hall to pray on his behalf. The Baal Shem Tov said, "In vain does he make a commotion; if he could enter through this gate that I was accustomed to enter, he would accomplish with his prayer."

And at that time the soul of a certain dead one entered to him to rectify it, and he rebuked it and said, "Eighty years you have been wandering and roaming, and you did not hear until today that I am in the world. Go out, wicked one." And at once he said to the servant, "Run quickly with a great shout, that they clear the road, for I have angered him, lest he harm some person." And so it was, that it harmed a certain maiden, the daughter of the beadle. And the servant returned and heard the Baal Shem Tov say, "I forgive you those two hours; do not torment me." The servant said to him, "With whom is your honor speaking?" He said, "Do you not see the Angel of Death, who always fled from me — as people say, [I would] drive him to the place where black peppercorns grow — and now that they have given him permission over me, his shoulders have broadened and there is great joy upon him." And afterward all the people of the city came to greet him on the festival, and he said before them words of Torah, and afterward, at the meal, he commanded the servant to put honey in a large flask, and he put [it] in a small one. He said, "There is no rule on the day of death — even the beadle does not obey me." And afterward he said, "Until now I have done kindness with you, and now you shall do kindness with me." And afterward he went to the privy, and the servant wished to follow him. He asked him, "Why is this day different, that you wish to follow me? What did you see in me?" and [the servant] did not follow him. He also told them the sign: that when he passed away, the two clocks would stop. And on his coming [back] he washed his hands, and the large clock stopped, and his men surrounded it so that he would not see the stopping of the clock.

He said to them, "I know that the clock stopped, and I do not worry about myself, for I know for certain that I will go out of this gate and at once enter another gate." And he sat on his bed and commanded that they stand around his bed, and he said to them words of Torah on the pillar that one ascends by from the lower Garden of Eden to the upper Garden of Eden, and so in each and every world, how it is, [explaining it] with the [acronym] BeShN and with an explanation on the order of the [Temple] service. And he commanded them to recite "May the pleasantness," and he lay down and sat up several times and directed [his] intentions until they no longer heard the articulation of the letters; and he commanded that they cover him with a sheet, and he began to tremble and shudder as in the Amidah, and afterward he grew quiet little by little, and they saw that the small clock had stopped. And they waited a long while and placed a feather on his nose and saw that he had passed away.

All this I heard from R. Yaakov of the holy community of Mezhibuzh, who passed away in the Holy Land; and the rabbi [R. Yaakov] said that R. Leib Kessler saw the departure of his soul like a flame the color of sky-blue [*techelet*]. Once the aforementioned R. Leib came to the Baal Shem Tov several weeks before his passing, and [the Baal Shem Tov] told him that he would surely die, and the aforementioned R. Leib said, "[Give me your] head and eyes in place of [my] eyes, etc. [let me die in your place]," but the Baal Shem Tov refused. And he said, "Let them return to me what they took from me about a year ago," and he did not wish to live, for why was this life to him?

---

## §208

I heard that when the Baal Shem Tov's wife died, his men and household said to him that he should take another wife, and the Baal Shem Tov answered them in a tone of wonder: "Do I need a woman? I was abstinent for fourteen years in [the matter of the] bed, and this Hershele, my son, was born by [divine] command." And on the day of his death his son R. Tzvi was sleeping, and they said to the Baal Shem Tov, "Why does he not give a [last] charge to his son?" And he said, "What shall I do, for he is asleep?" And this that he slept was because he did not believe that his father would die. And they roused him and said to him, "Your father said that he will surely die today." And he came to his father and began to weep. And his father said to him, "I know that I gave you a soul, for at the time I joined with my wife all the firmaments quaked; and when I wished, it was in my power to bring the soul of Adam the first man by the secret of *ibbur* [impregnation], and I knew all that one needs to know. But you have a holy soul and do not need all this." And he asked of him, "Even so, tell me some thing," and he made an effort to tell him [but] said, "I do not understand what you are saying." And he said, "The world has grown dark for me, and I cannot speak with you." And he taught him a certain [holy] Name and said, "Intend [meditate on] this Name and you will see me, and I will study with you." He said to him, "Perhaps I will forget the aforementioned Name," and he told him a remedy that he would remember the Name — but I have forgotten what he told him.

---

## §209

One of the disciples of the holy rabbi R. Yechiel Michel of Zlotchov once traveled to his aforementioned rabbi, and on his coming to him the rabbi said, "At once travel home," and he did as his words. And he was literally as one confused and did not know on what account and why the rabbi commanded him to return home, when he had great longing to spend the Sabbath with the rabbi. And behold, on his journey, a certain small dog, beautiful and splendid, attached itself to him, and it found favor in his eyes, and he took it onto his wagon. And he amused himself much with it the whole way. And when they came to the water-crossing [ferry], the dog leaped into the river and began to swim in the river, but it could not swim well and began to drown in the river, and it made many movements and labored much until it drowned. And that man had very great grief over the drowning of the dog that had found favor in his eyes.

And meanwhile, while he was delayed, the fishermen raised a large fish in their net. And he was near his home, and it was the Sabbath eve; therefore he bought the fish from the fishermen, and when he came home he prepared the fish with all kinds of preparations in honor of the Sabbath. And when he came from the immersion house he tasted the fish — as our Sages, of blessed memory, said, "Those who taste it [the Sabbath food] merit life" — and at once he was turned into another man, and his face shone, and the whole Sabbath his prayers were like [those of] a great and holy man, and at every single meal he said secrets and mysteries of the Torah such as no ear had ever heard. And it was a wonder in the eyes of all the people, and also in his own eyes it was a wonder; and when he finished the evening prayer and the order of Havdalah, then he returned to as before, and he wondered greatly.

And the next day he traveled again to the rabbi, and [the rabbi] called him "a scholar for one day." And he asked him, to know what this was and on what account. And he answered him, saying that his father, of blessed memory, had a certain sin that he had sinned, and was reincarnated in the dog; and therefore, because a son knows the bitterness of his [father's] soul and loved him greatly, and by this that he grieved much at its drowning, [the father] was elevated and merited to be reincarnated in the fish that the fishermen raised; and by this that he prepared it in honor of the Sabbath, they gave [the father] permission to be impregnated [enter] into him. Therefore he had the whole Sabbath wondrous matters, and after the order of Havdalah [the father's soul] ascended to the place fitting for it, [and the man returned to] his standing as before.

---

## §210

Once, with the holy rabbi R. Michel of Zlotchov, his small son was very gravely ill, and at once word reached the aforementioned rabbi how in a certain land they wished to burn the holy books of the rabbi R. Yaakov Yosef of Polonnoye. And the rabbi traveled from his house and commanded his household that if, Heaven forbid, his son died, they should wait with his burial for his coming home. And after his journey [the son] fainted — what is called in the vernacular *hinerflet* [a deathlike swoon] — and they supposed that the child had died. After three days sweat fell upon him and his soul returned to his body. And when he recovered a little he recounted to them the whole affair. At once when his soul went out, a certain angel took it and led it to a certain palace, and the angel had no permission to enter the palace, but he himself entered the palace. And he stood by the door and saw that the great court was sitting, and at once he saw two messengers carrying a book in which were recorded all the transgressions, and it was as a heavy burden upon them. And at once a certain angel came with the book of merits, and these did not equal those [were not as weighty]. Until they brought a third book, which is the book of the sufferings he had received — as our Sages, of blessed memory, said, "Up to what is the limit of suffering, etc." — and many of the transgressions fell away [were cancelled]. And even so, on account of those that remained, they wished to sentence him to death, and they wished to write a [death-]note.

And at once his father, the aforementioned rabbi R. Michel, came with great commotion and a great outcry over this, that they wished to burn the aforementioned books: "Behold, it is revealed and known before the Master of all worlds that he did not do [it], Heaven forbid, for his own honor, but for the honor of the Lord and His Torah, may He be blessed." And when he saw his son standing by the door, he said to him, "My son, what are you doing here?" And he answered him, "I have been standing here a long time and do not know on what account and why; I ask that you plead well on my behalf." And he said, "If I do not forget, I will speak well on your behalf," and he cried out again concerning the books as before. And the court answered that this matter does not pertain to our court, but to "the High One above the high, etc." And the aforementioned rabbi went off and did not remember his son. And meanwhile there ascended to the aforementioned palace the holy rabbi R. Yaakov Yosef, the author, and he cried and cried out three times in weeping and great complaint over this, that they wished to burn the holy books. And the aforementioned rabbi saw him standing by the door and asked him, "Yosef, what are you doing here?" And he answered him as above and asked that he plead on his behalf, and [the rabbi] answered him as above: "If I do not forget, I will speak well of you." And they answered the aforementioned rabbi as above.

And meanwhile he too went off, and [the boy] stood with a fallen spirit, for there was none to help and none to support him. And at once a great noise was heard, so that literally all the worlds quaked, and they proclaimed, "Clear the way, for the Baal Shem Tov himself is coming to the palace." And he saw him standing by the door [and] asked him, "Yosef, what is your business here?" And he answered him as above, "and I also asked my lord father and the rabbi of the holy community of Polonnoye to plead well on my behalf, but they forgot me. Therefore I ask of your exalted holy honor that he plead well on my behalf." And the rabbi asked the court to release the lad in peace, and he commanded him to go home. And he wished to delay there and see what the Baal Shem Tov would do there. And at once two men came and took him and went with him until they found a stinking carcass lying on the refuse heap. They said to him, "Enter this carcass," and he refused in any way. He cried out and pleaded before them, and they struck him to enter against his will; and when he entered the carcass he began to sweat [revive].

The end — completed; praise to God, Creator of the world.
